Our Summer at the Summit

xMaxReboxluvr

Summary:

It’s summertime in Shenandoah National Park, where peak season is well underway with thousands of visitors traveling to the park for thrill seeking adventures. At Lanes Family Adventures, LLC, Adventure Guide Vi spends most of her days leading tourists on white-water rafting expeditions on the Shenandoah River, or Hiking tours up to the best views the park has to offer.

Meanwhile, Caitlyn Kiramman has been hired for her first ever season as a Climbing Ranger for the National Park Service. Despite being a new ranger, she has years of expert climbing experience under her belt—both as a competitive indoor climber and as an outdoor free-climbing enthusiast.

OR

Adventure Guide Vi x Climbing Ranger Caitlyn
National Park AU

Notes:

Hi, Everyone!

Welcome back to yet another CaitVi National Park AU!

I know I have two other WIPs at the moment, but this idea came to me in my sleep a few nights ago and I couldn't resist writing it.

As always, please do let me know what you think in the comments below and leave a kudos if you'd like to read more of this story - your support really encourages writers like myself, and I would love to hear from you! (Feel free to follow me on twitter @Laur2d2_)

If you're interested and want to read more National Park AUs, check out my completed works:

"To the Mountains" and "Up on Melancholy Hill (Are You Here With Me?"

Chapter 1: The Moon Turned Black When I Left You

Chapter Text

Late May

Part of New York City’s charm was that no matter who you were or what tax bracket you belonged to, if you tried to beat the rain by fast-walking home from a long, arduous day at work, you’d get caught flat-footed like a duck stuck in a well.

The early-summer storms that travelled across the mid-latitude jet stream were rather predictable this time of year and yet, Caitlyn Kiramman had still forgotten an umbrella.

It was truly the cherry on top of what had turned out to be yet another shitty, mundane day working as a lawyer for one of the city’s largest tech corporations.

And while her legal prowess far surpassed the combined capabilities of her fellow colleagues, the case she’d been handling for weeks now was becoming more than she could bear.

The past few weeks have entailed constant late nights and throbbing headaches, all while arguing a case that was actively harming working-class people while lining her employers’ already deep pockets.  It made her skin crawl and her eyes twitch.

What’s worse, Caitlyn Kiramman had zero interest in being a corporate lawyer in the first place.

Her career had been the result of her parents’ wishes, both incredibly successful professionals in their own fields; they’d set high expectations for their daughter very early on in her childhood.  Even before the Kirammans moved to the U.S., Caitlyn had always existed in the shadow of their reputations.

Growing up, Caitlyn and her parents—Tobias and Cassandra Kiramman—lived in a modest estate in Cambridge, England.  Or, rather, in the rural outskirts just outside of the city, surrounded by chalk grasslands and fenland wetlands.  The acres of natural landscape gave the young Kiramman girl the freedom to run and roam, often finding herself reading beneath an ash or sycamore tree.

In the earliest days of his career, Tobias worked both as a surgeon at Nuffield Health Cambridge, and as an anatomy professor at the university.  Caitlyn rarely saw her father in those days.

Her mother, on the other hand, worked as a barrister in the high courts.  Cassandra Kiramman had a reputation as a powerhouse in the legal field, which also meant that she spent long days in the office or in courtrooms trying to make a difference.  This left Caitlyn alone in the care of her Nanny, a woman named Matilda whom she adored as a child.

Caitlyn’s parents hadn’t completely neglected her in childhood, however, despite their busy schedules.  She had fond memories of her mother and father taking her to visit the university’s Botanical gardens, exploring the vast collection of plants while chasing after butterflies.  They’d always made time in their hectic schedules to spend time with her, even if it was just for a brief afternoon between meetings.

But as she got older and more capable of handling herself, especially when she began attending the academy and participating in extracurriculars such as piano lessons and badminton, Tobias and Cassandra grew more distant and focused on their work.

It wasn’t until two weeks before her fourteenth birthday that Tobias and Cassandra had sat Caitlyn down in the parlor of their Cambridge estate to tell her they were moving to New York City.  Tobias had accepted a new job as the Chief of Neurosurgery at NYU-Langone, and her mother had recently been awarded a tenured professor position at Columbia.

So, in the span of a month, the quiet of the English countryside was replaced by the constant buzz of life in New York City.

It was a city that could make you feel like the center of the world and completely invisible within the same hour.  Millions of lives unfolded beside one another in a relentless choreography.  Paths intersected for seconds, sometimes only for the length of a crosswalk, before disappearing forever into the city’s currents.

That was the paradox of New York.  It was both intimate and indifferent.  You might spend years living above the same neighbors without learning their names, but if ever you needed a hand carrying a load of groceries up ten floors, a stranger was always there to help.

Perhaps that was the city’s greatest contradiction.  In all its noise and impossible density, New York offered endless chances to connect, and yet, also provided countless places to hide.  You could lose yourself completely in the chaos and still feel utterly alone.

Caitlyn had since developed a sort of symbiosis with that feeling since moving to the American city.  Even since graduating college and being introduced to her girlfriend Charlie, who she’d been seeing now for four years, she’d still existed in a kind of transient state of mind—like some day, all of this would change.

It wasn’t that her relationship had left her completely bereft of love.  There’d been a reason she’d stayed with her girlfriend for so long, even though it had been her parents that’d made the match.  Charlie had genuinely been kind to her in the beginning.

They were thirty-two when her parents introduced them at a fundraising gala that Columbia’s law school had been hosting.  Charlie, recently barred, had signed with a firm Cassandra was well acquainted with.  Her mother insisted on introducing the attorney to Caitlyn, citing their common interests—though Caitlyn wouldn’t say that those said interests had been a complete façade.

As the months went on, their relationship developed quite naturally.  They were both lawyers, sure, but they did have the same taste in music and cinema.  Weekends were spent attending concerts at Madison Square Garden or catching matinees at the Paris Theater.  Anniversaries consisted of dinners at Michelin star restaurants and the hottest sex she’d ever had.

Until recently, their relationship had been steady and spontaneous—far from the mundane chore that it had become in the past several months.  The more serious their relationship became, Charlie’s grip on Caitlyn’s proverbial leash tightened exponentially.  It was as if her girlfriend no longer trusted her.  She wasn’t allowed to go to the climbing gym alone or meet up with her free-climbing friends for their monthly trips to Thacher State Park in Albany.

And whenever Caitlyn suggested that they go out with their friends to the bars like they used to, Charlie would change the subject or distract her by pulling her into the bedroom and fucking her senseless until she all but forgot what she’d been asking for in the first place.

She couldn’t remember the last time they’d left the apartment for anything other than work.

Caitlyn tried to ask Charlie about it several weeks ago, but her girlfriend’s only response was simply a desire to spend more time together.  Which, sure, she would’ve believed if it hadn’t been for her girlfriend’s noticeable absence the last few months.  Even when Caitlyn knew that Charlie wasn’t actively working on a case, her girlfriend wouldn’t come home some nights until after 11PM.

It got to the point that even Cassandra was complaining about Caitlyn’s lack of visits to the Kiramman Penthouse. 

She was completely and utterly stuck in a life that she never wanted, in a relationship that was doomed to fail, and a career that she hated with her entire being.  To put it simply, Caitlyn Kiramman was trapped.

And wet.

Rain soaked through her midnight-hair like she’d just stepped through a waterfall, and her trenchcoat did nothing to prevent the moisture from seeping through her favourite three-piece suit.  She hid her briefcase beneath her blazer, just praying to the gods that the motion she’d spent the last six hours writing would be dry enough to file with the court in the morning.

The storm clouds had rolled in swiftly, blocking out the setting sun and blanketing the streets in a dreary blackness that even the neon lights of the clubs and restaurants could barely alleviate it.  She cursed when a taxi cab drove past, splashing a wave of polluted runoff all over her expensive Italian leather boots.

“Did you forget your umbrella again, Miss Kiramman?” A deeply amused voice called out to her over the din of the torrential rain.

Caitlyn found the familiar kind expression of the man that stood like a sentinel by the front door of her apartment building.  Scott had been her doorman for years, a stalwart of their little community with his helpfulness and comical remarks. 

“Would it kill you to go one day without mocking me, Scott?” She chafed, though her annoyance was only short lived in his presence.  A smile prickled at the corners of her lips.

The comely man chuckled and pulled the door open wide enough for her to slip through, “Go dry yourself off, Miss Kiramman, and have a lovely evening.”

Inside, she was met with a wall of vetiver and patchouli, a scent she knew was supposed to smell rich and luxuriant but thought only came across as tacky and just plain overwhelming.  She rather preferred to breathe in the petrichor outside than these scented oils. 

Her heels clacked across the marbled floors of the lobby as she made her way past the concierge and down the hall toward the elevators, leaving a trail of droplets in her wake.  Exhaustion lingered in the bags beneath her eyes and in her deflated posture, evidence of her workload and the weight she carried on her shoulders.  She felt like a valve within her was leaking, the increasing pressure about to break it open completely.  The only thing keeping the pieces together was the idea of taking a warm shower and crawling into the comfort of her own bed.

Thankfully, the elevator was empty when she stepped on.  Caitlyn shrugged off her soggy trenchcoat and draped it over her arm after the weight of the fabric became too overstimulating.  She leaned against the handrail, head resting against the dark wood of the elevator wall and closed her eyes, listening to the ding indicating each floor as it continued to climb. 

By the time Caitlyn stepped off the elevator and walked down the familiar hallway toward her apartment, a sinking feeling had already begun to settle in her stomach.

At first, she blamed it on the disastrous day she’d had.  But the feeling persisted, deepening with every step, like some primitive part of her mind was screaming a warning that the rest of her hadn’t caught up to yet.

She stopped outside her door and dug through the pockets of the trench coat draped over her arm, searching for her keys.  Her fingers fumbled against soggy receipts and loose change until they finally brushed the cold metal of her keyring.

Just as Caitlyn was about to slot the key into the door lock, she heard a muffled sound coming from within her apartment.  She frowned, pressing her ear to the door’s surface when she heard a soft creak.  She hadn’t expected Charlie to be home so soon. 

The uneasy feeling tightened in her stomach as Caitlyn unlocked the door and stepped inside.  The apartment was unusually dark, with only a faint glow spilling from somewhere past the living room.

“Babe?” She called, voice soft in case her girlfriend was sleeping.

There was no answer, so she figured that her assumption was correct.  With all the late nights Charlie had been pulling recently, she figured the woman just needed to catch up on her sleep and had found a free evening to do so.

Caitlyn pushed the door shut behind her and bent to slip off her ruined heels.  As she set them on the mat beside the door, something caught her eye—an extra pair of shoes.  Men’s shoes.  Large, black, neatly placed on the mat next to her girlfriend’s loafers.

Her mind immediately searched her mental rolodex for an explanation—maybe Charlie’s brother had flown in for a surprise visit, or perhaps the maintenance man was finally following up on their service request to fix the leaky faucet in the guest washroom.  Anything except the explanation that was already beginning to reveal itself in her reality.

Slowly, Caitlyn padded through the living room and down the hall toward the bedroom, where she could see the orange glow of the lamplight through the door left ajar.  Her entire body went rigid and she froze in place when she heard a breathless moan, clear as day, drifting into the hall.  The blood drained from her face when Charlie’s familiar giggle followed shortly after.

Every instinct she possessed begged her not to continue down that hallway, to turn around, to go pick up Chinese takeaway from their favorite place down the block, anything to preserve the fragile uncertainty that still existed.  Because the moment she saw the truth, there would be no taking it back.

Part of her, however, wanted to keep moving.  Despite the hurt already making its way up her throat, Caitlyn wanted this chapter of her life to end.  And if it all came crashing down the moment she walked into her bedroom, well, maybe it was for the best.

She stopped just outside the room, her heart pounding so hard it hurt.  Behind the door, the sounds continued, and without further hesitation, Caitlyn pushed the door open and accepted what she already knew.

There was something sacred about standing alone beneath a sky full of stars on a clear summer night.  The air held that perfect balance that only came a few times each year, warm enough that a jacket felt unnecessary, yet cool enough that the heat never clung to your skin.  A gentle breeze wandered through the valley and brushed against her cheek like a familiar hand, leaving behind the lingering scent of pine and distant campfires.

It was the best part of living in small town Luray, Virginia, where light pollution was minimal and the wilderness was vast.  On clear nights like this, you could even see the Milky Way galaxy shimmering above your head.

As a kid, Vi Lanes had always thought that the stars made the world feel bigger.  Now, though, she thought they made it seem smaller, too.  Looking up at thousands of distant lights scattered across an endless darkness reminded her that she was just one person among billions. 

That fact probably would’ve made anyone else feel insignificant, but Vi knew that it connected her to everything around her, an inseparable piece of the Earth.  The mountains, the trees, the Shenandoah River that wound through the valley, and every stranger tucked in their homes existed beneath the same sky.

In moments like this, solitude never felt the same as loneliness.  Standing there with nothing else but the whisper of the wind, the feeling of the cigarette between her lips, and the chirp of the crickets, she still felt accompanied.

For only as long as it took to smoke her cigarette, there was no past to mourn and no future to worry about, just the night, the mountains, and the comfortable certainty she felt in her bones that she wasn’t quite as alone as she sometimes believed.

Vi was pulled from her thoughts by the familiar jingling sound of the bar door, the bell chiming as it opened on its hinges, “Either you’re smoking the entire pack or you’re taking the slowest drags ever!” An amused voice called out to her.

She turned in the direction of the voice, finding her friend Jayce Talis peering out at her with unfocused eyes and a wide grin.  He was clearly wasted.

“Oh, excuse me for enjoying some peace and quiet!” She retorted with an exaggerated expression, feigning annoyance. 

Ninety percent of their interactions always devolved into some sort of friendly banter, pressing each other’s buttons for sport.  Vi met Jayce a few years ago, when he was just a junior ranger and she had just gotten her swiftwater rescue certification. 

A couple of reckless teens had taken their kayaks out on a set of class 3 rapids, unsupervised and inexperienced.  It didn’t take long for their yaks to capsize, leaving both teens stranded in the ripping currents trying to keep their heads above water.  Miraculously, they were able to grab hold of and climb up on a flat boulder in the middle of the river, but that didn’t solve the problem of getting to land.

Jayce was on shift at the nearest pilot ranger station when the call came in, and the only registered certified swiftwater rescue technician in the area where the teens had capsized was Vi.  So, they met on the bank of the Shenandoah River, where Jayce served as Vi’s spotter for her run downstream with the rescue raft.

They’d managed to get the teens safely out of the water, and from that day on, Jayce and Vi were like two peas in a pod.  Now, Jayce got promoted to Chief Ranger of Shenandoah National Park, and Vi was guiding tourists down various classes of rapids on the Shenandoah River and taking them on hikes up Old Rag Mountain as an Adventure Guide for her family’s small business.

Lanes Family Adventures, LLC, was founded and operated by her adoptive father, Vander, who’d been the very reason that Vi had been able to achieve her dreams of becoming a Level 5 Raft Guide.  She loved life on the water, in the Shenandoah Valley in general, and it was all thanks to him.

“You can have peace and quiet when you’re dead. Come on, the Irish Car Bombs won’t drink themselves!” Jayce exclaimed, waving her inside.

Vi took one last drag of her cigarette, smothered it before tossing it in the receptacle, and stepped back into the chaos of one of the only bars in town.  The crowd was rowdy and the music was blaring.  Neon beer logos blanketed the room in vibrant colors, turning Vi’s pink hair into shades of green, purple, and blue.

There were several faces in the crowd that she recognized—most of them locals that she’d worked with at least once, from park rangers to maintenance techs and members of the rescue squad.  All of them gathered in the small bar to release some steam before peak season came into full swing at Shenandoah National Park and the nearby Luray Caverns.

In just a few weeks, Jayce would be working long hours as the new man in charge, and Vi was preparing for a busy season full of fun adventures.  She looked forward to summer every single year.

She followed Jayce’s broad back, shouldering her way toward the little section of countertop he’d carved out for them, where the bartender was just setting down two pints of Guinness and a couple shots of Jameson.

Vi pressed her forearms onto the sticky surface of the counter and leaned toward the red-haired bartender with a wicked grin, “Trying to get me sloppy tonight, Fortune?”

Sarah Fortune had been the bartender at Jericho’s for years, and also just so happened to be someone that Vi hooked up with regularly. 

They weren’t dating, nor were they necessarily friends with benefits.  Everything between them was better off left unsaid.  But on nights when Vi was feeling particularly insatiable, she’d find the bartender after her shift and follow her back to her apartment for a night of tangled limbs and twisted bed sheets.

Sarah rolled her eyes playfully and leaned over the counter, her breasts practically spilling out of her low-cut shirt, and whispered in Vi’s ear, “In your dreams, Lanes.”

Vi swallowed thickly as the bartender pulled away, casually wiping down a glass with a dishtowel.  Her mouth had gone dry and the tips of her ears grew hot. “Yeah, we’ll see about that,” she murmured before turning to Jayce, “Count to three?”

He grinned, picked up one of the shot glasses of whiskey, and started to count.

“One, two, three!”

In a matter of seconds, they dropped their shot glasses into the pints of Guiness and chugged the concoctions before they could foam up and spill all over Sarah’s countertop.

When Vi finished and set down her empty glass, she wiped the dribble of dark beer from her chin with the back of her hand and grinned, “Fuck sake, that was horrible.”

Jayce burped, “Yeah, remind me whose idea that was?” He pinched his brows together, frowning at the now empty glass in his hand.

“Yours!” Vi exclaimed in disbelief, shaking her head.

He chuckled, swaying slightly on his feet before she caught his elbow, “That does sound like me.” Jayce hiccupped, grinning.

Before Vi could suggest that they close out their tabs for the night, Jayce’s phone began to ring.  He pulled it from his pocket and frowned immediately upon reading the caller ID, “I’ve gotta take this.” His voice turned serious, and he stumbled through the crowd toward the door, leaving Vi alone at the counter with a confused expression.

“How much you wanna bet he won’t be back in to pay his tab?” Sarah appeared seemingly out of the blue with another pint and handed it to her, which Vi accepted happily.

She chuckled and swallowed a gulp of the lager before responding, “Anything I could bet wouldn’t make a dent in the tips you got tonight.”

Sarah hummed at that, “I can think of something else you could use to bet.”

Her husky voice was laced with innuendo, and Vi was such a sucker for it, “Oh yeah? Why don’t you show me.” She dared with a quirk of her eyebrow.  Her tongue jutted out slightly, wiping away a drop of beer that lingered on her bottom lip.

Vi smirked when she caught Sarah’s green eyes tracking the movement, clearly falling for her trap.

The bartender didn’t say another word, just cleared her throat and shuffled over to the barback and whispered something in his ear.  He eyed her suspiciously, before shifting his gaze to Vi, and then back to Sarah, before finally relenting to whatever was said and nodding.

Sarah swiftly circled around the bar, grabbed Vi’s wrist, and tugged her through the Staff Only door.  Behind it was a long, narrow hallway lined with linoleum floor-tiles and flickering incandescent lights, which were kind of eerie and made Vi’s pulse pound in her ears. 

They rounded a corner and slipped past another door into a dark room, which Vi quickly realized was a broom closet after knocking into a mop and hearing its long handle clatter to the floor.  But before she could react, her back was pressed into the door and Sarah’s warm breath ghosted her neck.

Vi swallowed, “What are you doing?”

“What does it look like I’m doing?” Sarah quipped, nibbling at Vi’s earlobe before whispering in her ear, “Are you just going to stand there, or are you going to fuck me?”

Like a fuse lighting a match, Vi grabbed the collar of Sarah’s shirt and flipped them around, pressing the bartender into the door as she wedged her knee between shaky legs.

The bartender gasped at the feeling of Vi’s toned quad muscles flexing against her aching core, her vision going slightly blurry with pleasure.  She wrapped her arms around Vi’s shoulders and pulled her in closer.

Vi chuckled lowly, warm like honey, “Since when did you get so desperate, Fortune?” She husked, grabbing Sarah’s hips in a grip that was tight enough to claim but not enough to bruise. “You’re so needy for me, huh?”

Their faces were so close that their noses brushed and breaths mingled, thick with wanting. 

Sarah huffed, “You talk too much,” she whispered before pressing soft lips to Vi’s neck.  The softness of it drew a low sigh from Vi’s lips as she melted into the bartender’s touch.

The thing about Sarah was that neither of them pretended it was anything more than what it was.  There were no promises exchanged in the dark and no conversations about futures that would never arrive.

They existed within the boundaries they’d drawn for themselves, slipping in and out of each other’s lives whenever loneliness or desire nudged them together.  Yet, for all its impermanence, there was something unexpectedly tender about it.

Sometimes intimacy wasn’t found in grand declarations of love or shared plans for the next ten years.  Sometimes it was a warm body beside you after a difficult week.  A hand absentmindedly tracing circles across your back while the rest of the world slept.  The quiet understanding that, for a little while, someone had chosen your company over solitude.

Vi knew that whatever existed between them would eventually fade when one of them decided to let it.  It had no destination and no deeper meaning.  But there was comfort in being known, even briefly.  Comfort in discovering that not every connection had to last forever to be worth having.

“Caitlyn, wait…” Charlie pleaded as she scrambled out of their bed, nude aside from the socks on her feet.

It was as Caitlyn feared.  Her partner of four years had been fucking someone else, a man, no less, and for who knows how long. 

She didn’t have a problem with Charlie being bisexual—that wasn’t the issue.  The issue was that the whole time they’d been together, her girlfriend identified as a lesbian.  Not just that, but a monogamous lesbian—strictly so.  What hurt even more was that her girlfriend hadn’t even felt comfortable enough to talk to Caitlyn about exploring her sexuality further.

Caitlyn would’ve understood.  She would’ve…well…she doesn’t know what she would’ve done.  But she wouldn’t have judged Charlie, nor would she have held her back if this was something she needed to do.

But the fact that her girlfriend chose to cheat on her instead, especially after the way she’d been treating Caitlyn for the past few months, set her on edge.

The moment she walked into that room, Caitlyn’s vision narrowed into a tunnel.  Everything in her periphery was hazy, and it sounded like the world around her was underwater.  Her pulse pounded in her eardrums so loud it felt as if she were on stage at an orchestra made up solely of percussive instruments.

She ignored Charlie’s pleas and the other man’s existence entirely, focusing only on walking toward the closet, where she dug around for her duffle bag and robotically began shoving things into it.  Her mind went into autopilot to protect her from the pain, at least until she could get out of there.

The only sounds that registered to her were the front door opening and closing again—probably the man leaving—and Charlie’s shuffling somewhere in the room behind her.  Putting on her clothes, if she had to guess.

Caitlyn moved from the closet to the dresser on the other side of the bedroom, pulling open drawers and packing as much as she could.  Clothes, jewelry, some of her old indoor climbing medals, and her climbing gear. 

If she weren’t so desperate to get out of there, she would’ve spent time packing the rest of her things, but she figured her parents could just come over there another day to pick up the rest of her belongings and put them into storage.

Before she could finish packing the last of her bras, a hand gripped her arm.  Her body froze in response.

“Come on, you can’t just leave.” Charlie huffed, her voice laced with an undertone of frustration, like she was somehow insinuating that Caitlyn was in the wrong, despite having cheated on her own girlfriend.

For years, Caitlyn had felt like a passenger in her own life, watching decisions accumulate around her like bricks in a wall she didn’t have a hand in building.  First, it had been the crushing weight of her parents’ expectations, then law school, her prestigious career, and then Charlie—someone who fit perfectly into the carefully crafted life her parents desired for her.

She’d simply kept saying yes because it was easier than disappointing the people who loved her, easier than admitting she wanted something different.

Her life had become a series of obligations disguised as opportunities, each one narrowing the road ahead until she could no longer tell whether she was following a path or simply being pushed down it.

Standing there now, with Charlie’s hand wrapped around her arm, Caitlyn felt the walls closing in from all sides.  The betrayal hurt, but beneath it lurked something uglier and far older.  The realization that if she stayed, she would continue surrendering pieces of herself until there was nothing left but the version everyone expected her to be.

Suddenly, the prospect of leaving felt less daunting than it had years ago, that one hard semester in law school when she’d seriously considered running.  Back then, she didn’t seize that opportunity.  Now, though, she would grasp it with everything she had.

Caitlyn pulled free from Charlie’s hold on her, not even bothering to look at her Ex-girlfriend before zipping up the duffle, sliding on her tie-dye crocs (which she only ever wore in the comfort of her own home and would, in any other circumstance, feel horrified at wearing them in public), and grabbed her umbrella before walking out the front door with her head held high.

Back down in the lobby, Caitlyn’s crocs were much quieter against the marble floors than her heels had been.  It was late, and the concierge had long gone home.  Lights were dim and no one else was around. 

Outside, the rain continued to slam against concrete and asphalt.  Caitlyn sighed, at least she remembered her umbrella.  She hugged her duffle close to her chest with one arm and held her umbrella in the other, using her hip to push through the lobby door and onto the street.

Scott still stood stoically by the door, his hood pulled tight over his derby hat and trench coat draped protectively over his suit.  He turned when he heard Caitlyn struggling through the door, grasping the handle to hold it open for her, “Where are we off to now, Miss Kiramman?” He asked, surprise in his voice when he caught sight of her duffle.

Despite trying to keep her neutral mask, a tinge of sadness flickered across her expression at the thought of possibly never seeing him again.  Caitlyn touched his arm affectionately, giving him a soft smile that didn’t reach her blue eyes, “Away.”  She answered with a squeeze of his arm before turning away and walking down the rain-soaked street.

The city never paused for heartbreak.  Yellow taxis still splashed through puddles, sending murky rainwater cresting over onto the sidewalk and onto unsuspecting passersby.  Steam curled from subway grates like tentacles.  Somewhere across the street, laughter drifted from crowded bars while strangers squeezed beneath awnings with cigarettes perched between their lips.

Caitlyn walked through it all like a ghost, her mind drowning in the emotions that clung to her throat.

The umbrella above her head shielded her from the rain, but not from the strange hollowness spreading through her chest.  Every block she passed held a memory she suddenly wished she didn’t have, like the little wine bar where Charlie once insisted on celebrating a courtroom victory, which led to one too many glasses of Merlot and a not-so-victorious night spent over the toilet bowl.

She passed the bookstore they’d wandered through on a snowy afternoon last year, the one where Caitlyn had found the custom special edition of The Priory of the Orange Tree and gushed over it for hours afterwards.

Caitlyn crossed Columbus Circle, watching headlights smear across wet pavement like streaks of liquid gold.  The reflections of the streetlights and the neon signs of bars turned the city into a painting she could’ve sworn she’d seen in the MoMa.

For the first time in years, Caitlyn had absolutely no plan.  She hadn’t even been sure where she was walking to—perhaps she wasn’t walking anywhere at all.  She’d just been numb to everything but the tightening of her chest.

She stopped beneath the glow of a streetlamp and took a long breath.  The air smelled of rain-soaked concrete and the food carts that stayed open no matter what time it was or what the weather did. 

It took her a second to orient herself as she looked up at the street sign and found that she’d walked almost a mile already, somehow making it to 76th street without realizing it.

Caitlyn looked across the street, eyes locking on the illuminated sign of The Wallace hotel behind the sheet of rain, its brick façade and striped awning stood like welcoming arms, offering shelter and comfort away from the gloom.

It wasn’t until she crossed the street and approached the hotel’s front door that she remembered what she was wearing.  Her suit hung like a wet towel over her tall frame and her crocs squelched beneath her feet with the rain that’d seeped in through the holes. 

She grimaced.  The Wallace was a 5-star luxury hotel—definitely not the kind of place you wanted to be seen wearing crocs.

Well, Caitlyn supposed she’d already suffered the deep humiliation of her girlfriend cheating on her, so what’s one more small moment of embarrassment in comparison?

Drawing a breath, she pulled open the door to the lobby and inhaled the aroma of fresh bergamot and jasmine.  The crisp, jagged lines of the geometric black and white floor tiles were a bit jarring considering the headache that poked at the back of her skull.  Across from the door stood a smooth, marble check-in counter.

The concierge’s gaze raked down her form, taking in the state of her suit before pausing with a look of distaste at the sight of her shoes.  The expression was short-lived before the brown-haired woman plastered a fake smile and greeted her.

“Hello, do you have a reservation?” The woman got straight to the nitty-gritty, probably trying to save their pretentious reputation by moving her along.

Caitlyn sighed and brushed a strand of her midnight hair behind her ear before pulling out her ID and handing it over to the concierge, “I don’t, but I was hoping you would have something available.” Her voice was tired, low, like the rumble of thunder in the distance.

The woman inspected the ID, her eyebrows raised to her hairline once she read Caitlyn’s name, her demeanor flipping in a heartbeat, “Certainly, Miss Kiramman. I have the penthouse available for you.” She chirped, handing over a keycard without further fanfare.

Caitlyn muttered her thanks and slung her duffle over her shoulder, heading for the elevator.  A bellhop who looked way too young to be of legal working age asked for her bag, before escorting her to the room at the top floor.

She tipped the kid well and closed the door, not even bothering to walk any further into the penthouse before ultimately crumbling to the floor in a fit of tears that she’d been holding back ever since leaving her apartment.

Caitlyn cried for her relationship, she cried for her inability to leave sooner, cried for how alone she felt, even in the fanciest hotel room that money could buy.  What was the point of this successful career that she didn’t even enjoy, if all the money she earned and the reputation she built still couldn’t fill the hole in her chest?

She felt physically ill with the thought of how lonely she was, trapped in this life she’d never imagined for herself.  Everything had strayed so far off from the path she dreamed of as a kid, the one that led to muddy boots and scraped knees rather than billable hours and courtroom sidebars. 

Nothing ever made her feel as alive as the rough texture of stone beneath her fingertips and the dull ache in her muscles after a long day on a route, standing at the top of a climb with her heart hammering against her ribs while the world stretched endlessly beneath her feet.

Somewhere along the way, she’d lost that girl—the one who dreamed of mountains and adventure and the wind in her hair.  For years she’d been climbing up the wrong mountain.

But, she realized, this didn’t have to be the end of everything.  Caitlyn still had a chance to turn back, to find her way back to where she wanted to be.

She wiped a tear from her cheek with the back of her hand and pulled her phone out from the pocket inside her blazer.  It took only a few seconds to find the contact she needed.  It rang, long enough for Caitlyn to realize that maybe her best friend had been asleep.

Just as she was about to hang up, however, a familiar voice answered, “Hey, Sprout!”

His voice filled her with the warmth she’d been missing all night, proof that she’d made the right decision by calling him. 

“Hi Jayce…” Caitlyn murmured.  She cleared her throat, trying to sound normal but it was no use when it came to Jayce Talis.

She and Jayce met years ago during one of her trips to New River Gorge on one of her outdoor climbing trips in college, where they’d been assigned as each other’s belay partners.  It’d been not long since he’d lost his mother to cancer and he hadn’t been climbing in months—Caitlyn encouraged him, comforted him when the grief reared its ugly head, and made him laugh with her awkwardness.

Ever since, they have stayed in touch.  Through law school for Caitlyn and Forestry School at Virginia Tech for Jayce, they made time for all sorts of trips between semesters, exploring new places and climbing challenging routes to see which of them ascended with the quickest time.

Jayce knew recently though that Caitlyn had been going through something she wouldn’t talk about with him, something that had made her pull away from everyone else in her life.  When Cassandra called him one day to ask if he’d heard from her daughter—he knew.  Charlie was always demanding, requiring all of Caitlyn’s attention, and that, Jayce realized at the time, was a red flag.

But Caitlyn wouldn’t hear any of his concerns.  Her calls simply became less frequent.

“What happened?” Jayce asked with an evident frown in his voice.

Caitlyn ignored his concerned inquiry, “Does the offer still stand?” She asked.

It was a few weeks ago, maybe closer to a month, that her best friend had called her one afternoon while she’d been in the middle of her workout.  He’d just been promoted to Chief Ranger at Shenandoah National Park, an achievement that Jayce dreamed of reaching ever since realizing he wanted to go to Forestry school.

She’d been so ecstatic for him, knowing how hard he worked to make his dreams come true.  Though she couldn’t help the envy that’d bubbled up in her chest at the thought of her best friend being free to chase them while she was stuck following expectations.

Jayce had known it then, too.  It’s why he called her with an offer to bring her on as a climbing ranger for the upcoming season.  Caitlyn was quick to decline, for reasons they both knew were bullshit.

“What…to join my team?” The late-night switch-up had him reeling, especially considering he’d been drinking for the past couple of hours.  His brain wasn’t exactly prepared for this conversation.

Caitlyn nodded even though he couldn’t see her, “Yeah.”

“Of course it does, Cait, but…” Jayce hesitated, seemingly mulling over whether he should prod her further, “What about Charlie?”

She sighed, finally pushing herself up to her feet.  She kicked off her crocs and slid out of her coat, letting it fall to the floor without another thought, “We broke up.”

Jayce released a breath of surprise, “What? When?”

Caitlyn padded into the all-marble bathroom, the grey and white penny tile icy beneath her bare feet.  A bright white light bordered the mirror above the countertop, which stretched the entire length of the wall. 

She caught a glimpse of her reflection and grimaced at what she saw looking back—a broken woman, midnight blue hair damp against her forehead and eyeliner smudged beneath red-rimmed eyes.  Her shoulders sagged from exhaustion, and she could’ve sworn her cheeks looked hollower than they had that morning.

“Cait? Talk to me…” Jayce’s voice brought her back from whatever distracted daze she’d fallen into.

“It happened no more than an hour ago, after I came home and found her fucking someone else in our bed.”

Caitlyn predicted a myriad of reactions from her best friend, but silence hadn’t been one of them.  It sat uncomfortably in the air, thick like smoke.  It wasn’t the loud, angry reaction she’d fully expected from him, and she wasn’t sure whether to be grateful for it or uneasy.

If it hadn’t been for the sound of his breathing coming through the phone, she would’ve thought the call had dropped.

“When can you get here?” He asked eventually.

The question threw her off completely, though she was grateful for it all the same.  It was further evidence that her best friend knew exactly what she needed in that moment, and it wasn’t pity.  Instead, he simply offered her an escape, something else to focus on rather than the whirlwind of emotions spinning in her head.

She exhaled in relief, “I’ll catch a flight in the morning.”

With a new summer season came the long hours of prep work, inspecting rafts that’d spent a year in storage for holes and dry rot, checking each and every bow line for fraying, looking for cracks in the wooden paddles that Vander insisted on using rather than the more modern plastic ones.

Sweat drenched Vi’s tee-shirt under the bright late-May sun.  An old baseball cap kept the golden rays out of her eyes, and the sleeves of her shirt were rolled up past the crest of her shoulders so she could feel its warmth against her freckled skin.

There wasn’t a single cloud in the azure sky, but a breeze drifted lightly through the trees as she worked in field behind the old auto-repair garage they’d converted into the base of operations for the family business. 

The scent of freshly cut grass swirled around her as she inspected the last bow line and coiled it up neatly. 

“You should take a break, Kiddo. You’ve been out here for hours,” Vander’s low, accented voice called to her from a few yards away.

Vi tossed the coiled rope into the pile and glanced over her shoulder just as her adoptive father approached, placing a large hand on her shoulder, “First trip downriver is in a week, one of us has gotta get these equipment checks done and I don’t see you out here doing it.” She teased with a wiggle of her brows, letting the older man know that she was just joking.

Vander huffed a laugh, “You’ve made your point, but that doesn’t change the fact that you need to eat something.”

Her expression softened when she caught a flicker of concern in his blue eyes, “Alright, I need to charge my phone anyways.” She relented. 

They strolled through the tall oat grasses, reveling in the warmth of the sun kissing their faces.  They didn’t say much.  Vi’s hands were shoved in her pockets as she stared at the mountains in the distance, mind wandering to the feeling of Sarah’s fingers raking down her tattooed back the night before.

Vander, seemingly noticing the growing smirk on her face, spoke then, “What are you smiling at?”

Vi chuckled and shrugged her shoulders, “Nothin’, just…looking forward to being out on the water again.” She lied, though it was no use.  Vander always saw through her façade.

He hummed at that, but didn’t push, “Well, as long as you’re focused on your responsibilities, that’s all that matters.”

She knew what he meant by that.  Vander was well aware of her tendency to spend nights warming someone else’s bed.  It wasn’t that she was sleeping around every single night, but she also wasn’t fond of keeping her own company.  There’d been a history of Vi missing early-morning rafting trips because she’d lost track of time in the arms of some woman she’d met at the bar or on the trails.

But this year, Vi was determined to remain focused on the family business, especially now that her younger sister, Powder, was freshly graduated from VCU and focusing on her applications for graduate school.  The only reason they’d been able to send the youngest Lanes sibling to college was because of the family business.

Ever since Vi had gotten certified as a rafting guide, they’d been earning more income, which was a heavy burden to bear but one that she was glad to take on, if it meant giving her younger sister the opportunities she never had.

Vi was six years older than Powder and was only 10 years old when their parents died in a freak accident on the Shenandoah rapids eighteen years ago.  Powder had only been four at the time, unable to understand why their parents hadn’t come back. 

Vander was a close friend of her parents, so it was only natural for him to adopt Vi and Powder after they passed away.  But he was also a busy man, someone that’d taken on a lot of debt to achieve his dreams of running his own Adventure Center in the heart of the Shenandoah Valley.

He always did his best to take care of them, but he worked long days and often left Vi to make sure her little sister got to school on time, had dinner ready for her in the evenings, and helped her with her homework before Vander got home.  And even though she never minded the added responsibility, Vi’s own education suffered because of it.

College had never been in the cards for her, which is why Vi worked so hard to make sure the opportunity was there for Powder. 

Vi sighed, “Don’t worry, Dad. I’ll make sure this is our busiest summer yet.” She promised, straightening her posture under his gaze as they reached the front door of the warehouse.  She pulled the door open, creaking on its hinges and slamming behind them with a thunk.

Inside, the office was neatly decorated.  The counter where tourists could check-in for their scheduled adventure of the day stretched along the back windows.  Large canvas prints of photographs from various hiking, fishing, and rafting trips were hung in rows along the walls.  Racks of rental gear were lined up against the wall on the right.  To the left was the door that led to the back offices and the staff kitchen.

Vander clapped a hand on her shoulder when they reached the kitchen, “That’s good to hear, because there’s something I actually need to talk to you about, Violet.” His voice went low and hesitant, gaining her full attention.

Vi opened the refrigerator door and pulled out a bottle of water, unscrewing the cap while looking at her adoptive father with furrowed brows, “What about?”

He sat in one of the stools by the kitchen island, his frame comically large in contrast to the short countertop, “I need you to run things here for a couple of weeks.”

Her stomach dropped at that.  She leaned her forearms against the counter opposite of him, a frown pulling at the corners of her mouth, “Sure, but..is everything okay? You’re not sick, are you?”

Vander huffed a laugh, “No, gods no. I’m fine, Vi. I just have some business to take care of up in New York. Not sure how long I’ll be gone.”

It wasn’t out of the ordinary for her adoptive father to go on business trips, but something out of the blue like this made her stomach twist, “Anything we need to be concerned about?” She asked, taking a sip of her water.

He shook his head, “No, just focus on running things here and I’ll see you when I get back.” Vander replied, pushing off the barstool and running a hand through his long brown locks.

Vi was surprised by that, “Wait, you’re leaving now?”

Vander nodded, “Yeah, sorry kiddo. Kind of a last-minute thing. I’ll call you when I land, alright? And if you need anything, hit up Benzo.”

Benzo was Vander’s best friend, practically an uncle to Vi and Powder growing up.  He owned a mechanic’s shop in town.  His own adopted son, Ekko, worked on boat motors and was around the same age as Powder.  They used to go fishing together down at the reservoir, on an old john boat they’d found abandoned in the woods and fixed up one summer.

Now, Vi was almost positive that Powder and Ekko had something going on between them, but neither of them would budge on telling her the truth.  That didn’t stop the older woman from teasing them, though.

“Okay, yeah, sure. I’ve got everything under control here, Dad.” Vi nodded, paused for a moment, mulling over whether she should push the matter, then decided against it, “Just…you’d tell me if something was wrong, right?”

Being the eldest daughter had taught her a strange balance between responsibility and trust.  She felt protective of their family, especially considering the loss they’d experienced as children, and sometimes to a fault.  But she also knew that Vander wasn’t the kind of man to let a problem reach their doorstep without doing everything in his power to stop it first.  If he wasn’t telling her something now, there was probably a reason.

Vander sighed, “You’ll hear it from me first, Vi. Now, eat something before I send your sister after you.”

 

Chapter 2: I'll Let Some Light into My Room

Summary:

Caitlyn finally arrives in Shenandoah with a duffle bag and a whole lot of emotional baggage.

Notes:

Hi Everyone!

Really having a blast writing this story so far and I've put hours of research and planning into this so I hope you like it.

Gonna try to post once a week but alas my full time job always gets in the way of my writing.

Enjoy!

(See the end of the chapter for more notes.)

Chapter Text

By the time Caitlyn arrived at her gate in LaGuardia Airport the morning after her breakup, she had twenty-three missed calls.  Fifteen of them were from Charlie, another five were from her mother, and the final three were the customary courtesy calls from her boss, who she hadn’t even bothered notifying about her sudden departure from the city—and her life entirely.

Yeah, she probably should’ve given Mr. Dauphin a call considering she’d left the motion she spent six hours writing back at her apartment, since it was critical for the case they’d been working on, but she couldn’t bring herself to care.

Caitlyn was also well aware of the consequences of withdrawing from the case, and her firm, without notice. 

And that, she figured, was probably the main reason why her mother was trying to reach her.  Nevermind the fact that the woman Cassandra had set her up with had cheated on her.  Cassandra Kiramman would’ve never allowed her daughter to risk losing her bar license.  To put it simply, this behavior was unbecoming of a Kiramman.

So, rather than call anyone back to let them know where she was, Caitlyn simply turned off her phone and boarded her flight.

It was a small jet, since it was only about an hour and forty-five minutes to fly from LaGuardia to Charlottesville Albemarle, which was the closest airport she could get to Luray in such a short notice.  So, Caitlyn put on her headphones and listened to some of her audiobook as she flew toward the next chapter of her life.

She tried not to think too much about what she was leaving behind, packing all those thoughts away in a box and pushing it into the far reaches of her mind for another day.

Instead, Caitlyn’s mind drifted off to dreams of fresh air filling her lungs and watching the sun set over a valley full of lush green trees.  Excitement buzzed beneath her skin at the thought of finally doing something for herself for once.

And of course she couldn’t wait to see Jayce.  It’d been months since they last hung out, and there was something about his presence that always put her at ease.  Her best friend was goofy, smart, and unabashedly himself, no matter what pressure the world tried to place on his shoulders.

She’d been wholeheartedly proud of him when he got promoted to Chief Ranger, and now Caitlyn got to see Jayce in his element, as his employee, no less.

A sudden jolt pulled her from her musings, shaking her in her seat slightly.  Only when she opened her eyes and looked out the window did Caitlyn realize that they were landing already.  Outside, the sprawling grid of endless skyscrapers and asphalt were replaced by swaths of green, luscious and vibrant against the blue sky.

The plane came to a stop on the taxiway, where flight attendants directed she and the few other passengers on her flight toward the door by the cockpit.  A set of stairs led them down to the asphalt, where they were handed their luggage and directed to the small, brick terminal with a few offices and one security line.  It smelled a little musty and some of the ceiling panels were missing.

Outside the double sliding doors, however, Caitlyn could already see more trees than existed in the entirety of New York City.  The air smelled faintly of fuel, but even then, she could already tell that it was crisp and fresh, a feeling she’d have to get used to.

She stood at the curb, anxiously tapping her fingers against the strap of her duffle bag, when she heard the roar of music blaring over speakers.  A grey Toyota rav-4 pulled up to a screeching stop in front of where she stood.

The passenger window was wide open, giving her a clear view of her best friend, grinning widely back at her with his bright smile, a pair of aviator shades covering his hazel eyes.  His brown hair was short but ruffled, like he’d just rolled out of bed to come pick her up.

He probably had.

“Hey, Sprout!” Jayce greeted over the booming speakers, which she’d realized were playing Disturbia by Rihanna, an amusing sight for such a large, built man who worked in the woods for a living.

Caitlyn chuckled as her best friend got out of the car and circled around to pull her into an arm-squeezing hug, “Jayce, I can’t…breathe.” She huffed, wrapping her arms around his wide frame and holding him tighter.

She buried her face into his shoulder, inhaling the familiar, comforting scent of pinesap and sawdust, letting herself melt into him as the emotions came washing over her.

Her tears cascaded down porcelain cheeks as the sobs took over without meaning to, seeping into the fabric of his faded old smokey the bear tee-shirt.

Jayce’s arms held her tight, as if he were protecting Caitlyn from her own heartbreak, “I’m here, Cait. We’ll be okay, I promise.” He whispered, pressing a kiss to the top of her head and pulling back when he felt her shoulders relax.

Caitlyn looked at him with red-rimmed eyes and a small smile, “Sorry,” she apologized sheepishly, “I thought I was holding it together pretty well.”

Jayce shrugged and grabbed her duffle, tossing it in the back seat before opening her passenger door, “You can cry on my shoulder any time, Sprout. Come on, let’s get you home and out of that ridiculous suit.”

Vi sat at a desk in the back offices of Lanes Family Adventures, LLC, her feet propped up on its surface with a casual sort of flair as she flipped through the few pages of notes she’d written about her first guided Whitewater Rafting tour of the summer. 

She only had a few days to memorize the names of her group, their fears and preferences, as well as the route she’d chosen for the run.

“Are you going to hide in here all day or are you going to come help me stack up the yaks?” Powder complained the second she walked in the door.  She stood with crossed arms in front of the desk, scowling down at Vi in a way that said she was tired of working alone.

Vi sighed, “I gotta go over these and then go for a practice run down by the South Fork, Pow.”

“Yeah, yeah, likely story. Dad leaves and suddenly you’re too important to help with the heavy shit.” Powder rolled her eyes, though a smirk toyed with the corners of her lips.  The younger woman loved to push Vi’s buttons whenever the opportunity was available.

“Why don’t you call Ekko? I’m sure he’d love to show off his muscles in front of you.” Vi jeered, smirking wildly at her sister, a knowing expression crossing her face.

Powder shoved Vi’s feet off the desk, the motion making the pink-haired woman fall forward in her chair, “You’re insufferable,” she retorted, rolling her eyes.

Vi took it as a sign to lighten up on her sister, so she pushed to her feet and wrapped an arm around Powder’s shoulders, “Come on, I’ll help you with the yaks.”

Outside, a scattering of yellow, orange, and red kayaks were sprawled across the gravel outside of the garage doors, freshly inspected and ready to be stacked in the outdoor racks near the trailers.  There were single-rider and tandem yaks, the latter of which were way too heavy for one person to stack.

The breeze shifted slightly, brushing the pink strands away from Vi’s forehead.  Clouds hovered in the sky, creeping lazily across the blue expanse like an innertube catching the slow current of the river.

Vi grabbed the yak hauler, which was just a little cart that you could push a kayak onto and then pull across the short distance of grass between the gravel and the yak racks.  As she hauled one up, she turned back to Powder, who seemed perfectly content to supervise the task, “How are applications coming?”

“I submitted the one for Virginia Tech last night.” Powder replied, walking with Vi toward the racks. 

Vi pulled the red tandem yak off the hauler and motioned for Powder to grab one end and help lift it onto the top rack, “Did Jayce write your recommendation?”

Powder huffed, her arms shaking as she pushed the yak above her head, grunting slightly as they slid it over the rails and let it settle.  She caught Vi’s grey eyes and nodded briefly, “Yeah, thanks for that, by the way.”

Vi shook her head, “He was happy to do it, Pow. What about the application for Colorado State?”

“I—” Powder hesitated, a look of uncertainty crossing her features.

“Don’t tell me you’re not applying,” Vi’s steps slowed, her grey eyes raking over her sister’s face, trying to gauge what she was thinking.

Powder sighed, “It’s just…far.” She admitted in a low whisper, her gaze falling to the ground between them.

Vi knew then exactly what her younger sister was thinking.

For most people, leaving home was exciting.  It meant independence and the chance to become whoever they wanted to be.  But for Powder, home had never just been a place, as much as Vander had tried to create stability for them.  Home had always been where Vi was.

When their parents died, Powder was too young to understand the permanence of death.  She remembered only the memory of a laugh, the smell of river water and sunscreen, strong arms lifting her onto smooth shoulders.  Then one day those things were simply gone.

What Powder did remember clearly was Vi.

She remembered waking up from nightmares and crawling into her sister’s bed, crying that a monster beneath her bed was coming to get her.  She remembered Vi learning how to braid her light-blue hair from a youtube video because Vander didn’t know how.  She remembered long nights sitting at the kitchen table while Vi helped her with homework, her own studies left abandoned.

Powder had grown up knowing, with absolute certainty, that if everything else in the world fell apart, Vi would still be there.

But that was the thing about applying to Colorado State.  Sure, they may have one of the best Ecology graduate programs in the country, but it wasn’t just far from Virginia.  It was far from Vi.

And it wasn’t that Powder doubted herself—she knew perfectly well that she could handle the pressure of a competitive program.  But she’d spent twenty-two years measuring safety by her sister’s proximity.  Every major decision, every crisis, every tear she’d shed was shared with her older sister.

The idea of waking up in another state, hundreds of miles away, knowing she couldn’t simply drive home whenever she needed comfort, left a knot in her stomach.

Vi understood that feeling better than anyone.

What Powder didn’t always realize was that the same tragedy that made her so dependent on Vi, had forced her older sister to grow up far too quickly. 

It hadn’t sunk in for her that when they lost their parents, Vi had only been ten years old.  Even though she looked up to her older sister as her big protector, Vi had only been a child herself.

While other kids worried about which snacks they were going to have while watching their after-school cartoons, Vi worried about whether Powder had eaten enough dinner.  While her classmates talked about college applications and prom, Vi was working two jobs just to help Vander cover the bills.

She never complained about it, never once made Powder feel like a burden, but responsibility settled onto her shoulders so early that she sometimes forgot what it felt like not to carry it.

But it wasn’t the so-called burden that exhausted her, it was the constant fear of failing—as a sister, a guardian, Powder’s protector.  Of breaking the promise she’d silently made to herself at ten years old that Powder would never feel as alone as she had felt the day their parents died.

Perhaps that was why the thought of Colorado made both of them uncomfortable.  Even though Vi wanted to push her little sister to do whatever it took to achieve her dreams, including going to graduate school on the other side of the country, she wasn’t sure she was quite ready to let her go.

But that, she realized, was the reality of being an older sister.  Everything she did for Powder growing up had led them to this moment.

Vi pulled Powder in her arms, the shorter girl’s face falling into the crook of her neck, “It is, but no amount of distance can take me away from you. I’m always with you, Sis.”

Powder squeezed her sister’s waist once before pulling away, letting the emotion that’d been clogging her throat be swallowed back into her stomach.  She wasn’t the best at lingering in her feelings.

“Alright, let’s get back to work before you get too soft on me.” She joked, playfully nudging Vi’s arm with a smirk.

Vi watched her sister take the yak hauler back over toward the kayaks, staying put a while longer to let the moment settle over her.  Because yeah, no matter where Powder went for graduate school, she was going to miss her.

Jayce’s cabin was nestled in the woods somewhere between Luray and Lake Arrowhead.  They drove down a winding gravel road until they came upon the small but well-maintained structure, surrounded by white pines and balsam firs. 

The car slowed to a stop on the dirt driveway beside the cabin, between the shed and the side door. 

Caitlyn had fallen asleep somewhere along the fifty-four-mile drive between the airport and Luray, her head resting on the passenger window in a way that couldn’t have been comfortable.  Her eyes fluttered when Jayce turned the car off, a sliver of cerulean peeking out from her heavy eyelids.

He reached over and placed a strong hand on her shoulder, squeezing gently, “Hey, wake up, we’re here.”

She groaned softly and opened her eyes fully, taking in the dense forest outside, and the way the sun streamed through the tree canopy, leaving little dapples of light strewn across the dirt.

“Wow, it’s beautiful out here,” Caitlyn commented, her voice still hoarse from sleep.  She turned to Jayce, smiling softly before opening the passenger door and stepping out into the cool, slightly damp air.

Ferns blanketed the ground beneath the trees, their leaves wild and untamed.  The first thing she noticed when her crocs hit the ground was how quiet it was out there compared to the city.  All she could hear was the breeze rustling through the trees and the birds chirping as they swooped past.

Across the driveway, a red cardinal landed on the edge of Jayce’s birdbath, and Caitlyn nearly cried at the sight of it.

Jayce’s footsteps crunched over the dirt as he walked up behind her, duffle bag in tow, “It’s not quite a penthouse but I hope it’ll be comfortable until we find you a place of your own.” He whispered, slightly self-conscious about his humble living situation.

She whipped her head around and met his gaze, “Don’t ever say that again, Jayce. Your home is beautiful, I— thank you, for letting me stay here.”

He chuckled and nudged her shoulder before walking toward the front door, “Ask me in a week how you feel about living with me.”

Caitlyn frowned but didn’t respond as she followed her best friend into his home.  It was evident really quickly as to why Jayce had said what he did.

The cabin itself was really nice.  The exposed cedar-log walls were beautifully maintained; the fixtures were all updated and she could tell that he'd put a lot of time into upgrading the electrical system. 

But frankly, it smelled like sweaty socks and stale beer.

“I…see what you mean,” Caitlyn muttered as she strolled into the living room, where an old couch sat against the wall.  Its faux leather surface was cracked from years of use and a questionable stain lingered on one of the arms. 

Across from the couch, the fifty-inch television sat precariously on an old slab of wood which rested on two rickety sawhorses.  The cord hung down until it disappeared behind the stacks of books on the floor underneath, a variety of titles that ranged from local field guides to Ecology textbooks.

At the back wall, several large maps of Shenandoah National Park hung in a staggered pattern, following the set of stairs that led up to the upper floor.  Post-it notes with Jayce’s handwriting littered the maps, landmarks were marked with different color-coded labels, and a laminated poster with a list of emergency contacts was taped to the wall next to it.

Caitlyn studied the maps for a while, the muffled sounds of Jayce shuffling through the kitchen drifting from somewhere behind her.

She never realized how large Shenandoah National Park was.  It seemed to stretch the entire length of the state, with hundreds of miles of trails circling the summits of the Blue Ridge Mountains.  Her fingers traced the edges of a marker that was labeled Old Rag Mtn, and she could feel the excitement begin to buzz beneath her skin.

“Are you hungry?” He called over to her, the contents of the fridge rattling as he pulled the door open.  “I’ve got stuff to make sandwiches.”

She dragged her attention from the maps back over her shoulder, crossing her arms as she turned to walk toward the kitchen, “Sure, um, could I shower and change before we eat?”

“Oh!” Jayce exclaimed, closing the refrigerator with a thunk before gesturing toward the stairs, “Sorry, got distracted by my hunger. There’s an extra room up here.” He said, making his way up the stairs as he continued talking, “It has its own bathroom, I just haven’t had the chance to make the bed yet…”

His voice was unintelligible as she followed behind him, everything around her suddenly feeling like she was underwater.  Caitlyn’s lips parted slightly as she tried to draw in a breath, but it felt like her throat had been clogged with cotton.  The edges of her vision faded to black.  Her heart was racing and her hands were beginning to tremble.

She was having a panic attack.  A bad one. 

And she couldn’t get the words out to warn her best friend, who wouldn’t stop rambling about some stack of mail he’d left on the dresser in the corner of the room and that he’d bring up the vacuum after lunch because he’d dropped a bag of pretzels on the carpet a couple of days ago and forgotten to clean it up.

Caitlyn pushed through the door to the guest bathroom before he could even turn around, locked the door behind her, and slid down to the tiled floor just as the body-wracking sobs tore through her throat.

Jayce’s knocks barely broke through her awareness, “Cait? Are you okay? What’s going on?”

He pleaded for a response that didn’t come.

The midday sun beamed brightly against the blue sky, its rays hitting the surface of the water and reflecting off in different directions.  It’d been a few days since the last spot of rain passed through the area, so the river was within its normal levels, making it the perfect day for Vi to scout the run for her upcoming trip. 

This particular trip was scheduled only for Class I and Class II rapids, which would be a good way to ease into the summer season.

She sat comfortably in her river kayak—the orange and yellow ombre one she always used when scouting rapids on her own—paddle resting loosely across her lap as the current pushed her downstream at a leisurely pace.

Vi preferred scouting runs this way rather than on foot.  The river always revealed more of itself when you were sitting directly on its surface, moving with its rhythm rather than observing from shore.  Though, she knew, shore scouting did have its uses.

She dipped her paddle into the water and angled toward river right, passing a cluster of smooth boulders that split the flow into several channels.

Ahead, the sound of rushing water grew louder.  A narrow chute funneled the river between two large rocks, compressing the flow and accelerating it into a playful stretch of Class II whitewater.  Nothing dangerous, but enough to splash rafters and earn a few excited screams from first-timers.

Vi paddled toward it, the front of her yak slipping into the tongue of smooth green water leading into the rapid.  She accelerated quickly without needing to paddle, riding the center line through the chute and bracing lightly as waves slapped against the brightly painted plastic.

The river dropped slightly at the bottom, creating a modest drop that sent her skimming over a burst of white foam.  She laughed as cold water sprayed her arms and soaked the sleeves of her bright blue long-sleeved swim shirt.

The rapid wasn’t particularly technical nor difficult to navigate—guests would need to stay paddling and follow her commands, but the line was obvious.  It was the perfect opening for the tour.

Once through, Vi guided her kayak into a calm eddy (a pocket of slow, upstream-moving water behind a rock or bend) behind a boulder the size of a pickup truck.  She pulled out the waterproof notebook from the pocket of her PFD and scribbled the details of the feature.

The eddy line was clean and visible, marked by a thin crease where the opposing currents collided.  She made a mental note to guide the guests to a good angle before crossing it.  Satisfied with her notes, Vi paddled back into the main flow of the river.

There was a feeling she only ever found on moving water, one she struggled to explain to people who had never sat in the middle of a river and trusted it to carry them.  Out here, everything narrowed into the next paddle stroke, the next wave, or the next line through a rapid.

Bills, responsibilities, grief, loneliness, all of it dissolved beneath the rush of whitewater and the rhythm of her pulse pounding in her ears.

The river demanded her complete attention.  It didn’t care about yesterday’s mistakes or tomorrow’s problems.  It only cared about the present moment, and somehow there was freedom in that.

Vi remembered when she was no older than four years old, sitting between her parents in an old raft and wearing a life jacket that was far too large for her tiny frame.  Her father had laughed every time she splashed him with her paddle, while her mother pointed toward the mountains and named every ridge they passed.

Back then, she hadn’t understood why they loved the river so much.  It had simply been where they were the happiest.

Now, though, she understood.

Every time she dropped into a rapid and felt adrenaline surge through her veins, every time she read the water correctly and threaded a clean line between the rocks, she felt connected to them.  It felt like the river itself had carved the memories of them around every bend, and when Vi was on the water, she never felt quite so alone.

The river curved westward around a forested bend, flowing deeper into the valley.  Songbirds chirped in the trees overhead while a great blue heron glided just above the water, its pectinate toe skimming the water’s surface as it searched for prey.

Vi forward paddled with long, slow strokes through the nearly half-mile stretch of peaceful water before the familiar roar of whitewater reached her eardrums again.  She rounded another bend and immediately sat up straighter, her pulse quickening as she approached the source.

A partially submerged ledge, probably a piece of old rag granite, crossed much of the river’s width.  Water poured over it unevenly, creating a sizable hole near the center channel.  From a distance it didn’t look alarming, but Vi knew better than to underestimate the river’s power.

She paddled closer and watched the feature carefully, backpaddling every few seconds to stay clear of the obstacle.

The recirculation wasn’t particularly strong at the current water level, but it had enough power to stop an inflatable raft if it hit the wrong angle.  She watched leaves and small sticks drift through the feature, several of them disappearing temporarily beneath the surface before being spat back out and held in place rather than flowing downstream.

It was definitely not somewhere she wanted a raft to get stuck.

Vi ferried across the river and found a safter route along river right.  The water there formed a straightforward passage around the edge of the hole, with plenty of room for a guided group to navigate safely.  As soon as she passed the obstacle, another note went into the notebook.

This section of the river was a decent run, considerably mild for her tastes but just right for a group of beginners to be guided through.  She paddled toward the designated take-out point at river left, where the shoreline was a gradual incline rather than a steep drop-off and the water level low enough for her to carefully push out of her kayak and pull it up onto dry land.

Vi had just started to towel herself off when a voice called out to her, “You sure took your time!”

She turned around just as Sarah approached with the yak hauler, her red hair tied back neatly in a waterfall braid.  Her hiking shorts showed off her toned and tattooed quads beneath, the black ink circling her legs like vines.

“Sorry, I forgot you can’t keep yourself occupied for more than twenty minutes,” Vi chirped back with a roll of her eyes. 

Sarah feigned offense, grasping at her chest, “That hurts, Lanes.”

Vi chuckled while pulling the bow line of her yak until it was settled on the hauler, then tossed her towel and life jacket into the seat before taking the handle from Sarah’s hand with a sort of gentleness she reserved only for certain people.

“Thanks for being my ride back,” Vi’s tone turned more appreciative as the women walked back toward the truck, parked about a quarter of a mile through the woods on an old access road.

Sarah hummed, “I like having you owe me.”

Admittedly, so did Vi.  She didn’t have feelings for the bartender, at least, not like that.  But she was having a lot of fun just being in her company.  She was about to ask if Sarah wanted to come back to her place when her ringtone started blaring from the dry bag strapped to her back.

Vi stopped in her tracks, swung the bag off her shoulders, and pulled out her phone, frowning.

“Jayce?”

The tears wouldn’t stop coming no matter how hard Caitlyn tried to pull herself together.  Her suit felt suffocating, the feeling of being trapped in the fabric overwhelming her to the point that she tore off the trousers and the blazer, tossing them across the bathroom with a grunt of frustration.

Cold tile bit into her now bare thighs as she wrapped her arms around her knees again.

Jayce’s concerned voice could still be heard, though it sounded so muffled she would’ve sworn he was a mile away rather than right on the other side of the door.

“Come on, Sprout, just tell me you’re okay,” he said, knuckles still rapping against the door’s wooden surface.  It’d been about thirty minutes since his best friend had locked herself in the bathroom and hadn’t said a word, and Jayce was growing more desperate.

He could hear her cries, though muffled, so he at least knew she was physically okay, but he had no experience dealing with an episode like this, especially since she’d seemed fine just before it happened.

How was he ever going to talk her out of that bathroom?

A thought crossed his mind as he vaguely remembered something that Vi had told him once about her younger sister having panic attacks, and how she’d handled them a lot growing up since Vander wasn’t always around.

Jayce fiddled with his phone and pressed Vi’s contact before stepping out into the hall, lingering just out of Caitlyn’s earshot but close enough that his presence could still be felt.

The line rang a few times before a familiar voice crackled through the receiver, “Jayce?”

A wave of relief crashed over him, “Vi, I need a favor.” He got right to the point, forgoing any small talk.  The sound of wind on the other side of the call ruffled against the receiver, and Jayce realized that his friend was probably out on the river.

“Uh…right now? I’m kind of in the middle of something—”

“I just need a few minutes,” Jayce pleaded, “It’s urgent.”

He heard Vi inhale a breath before responding, “What’s wrong?”

Jayce sighed, peeking back through the bedroom door to make sure Caitlyn hadn’t quietly escaped the bathroom. “What did you do when Powder had her panic attacks?”

There was a pause before Vi answered, “I took her to the doctor and she got diagnosed with anxiety, why?”

“No, like, what did you do to calm her down?” Jayce clarified, voice sharper than it had been a minute ago.

He could feel the confusion emanating from his friend on the other end of the phone, “Well, I—” Vi’s voice turned softer then, “It helped to ground her wherever she was. Usually I’d take her shoes off and tell her to feel the ground beneath her feet, and then I’d tell her to focus on her other senses. You know, what do you see, hear, smell. That kind of thing.” Vi explained.

“And that worked?”

Vi hummed, “Yeah, every time.”

Silence stretched between them, filled only by the sound of their breathing through the receiver.  Jayce didn’t say anything as he let the worry fade, not wanting to go back to Caitlyn in any sort of elevated state that didn’t radiate pure calm.

It was Vi who broke the quiet, “Jayce…why are you asking?”

“I’ll explain later. Thanks, Vi.” He replied, hanging up the call before she could even respond.

He walked back in the room and knocked on the bathroom door again, “Hey, Sprout, I’m here…”

Still, no response came.

Caitlyn wanted to tell him to go away, that she needed some time alone, but the words were jumbled and her lungs felt like she’d inhaled glass.  She was sure that not enough oxygen was flowing through her system, like she was on the verge of collapse.

Her knees were fully moist from where she hid her face between them and cried.

Twenty-four hours ago, she’d still had a life she could use as a mask—a career and a long-term relationship that looked perfectly maintained from the outside, even if they felt increasingly hollow whenever she looked too closely.

Now it was all gone, and she had run away from her problems with an impulsivity she’d never experienced before.  Everything in her life had been so carefully constructed that there was no room for spontaneity or impulsion. 

Caitlyn could only imagine the look on her mother’s face when she’d heard that her daughter had all but skipped down, without so much as a phone call. 

Charlie’s betrayal had cracked something open inside of her.  One awful evening had transformed every uncomfortable truth she’d spent years avoiding into something impossible to ignore.  It felt as though the foundation beneath her entire existence had collapsed overnight.
