“You don’t have to go through this alone,” Jayce’s voice managed to slice through the fog, reaching her ears with a sort of calm composure she wanted to reach out for and take within her grasp.

She let her head fall against the door, trying to focus on the sound of his voice.

“There’s no pressure to come out until you’re ready, but let’s…let’s try something.” He began, letting Vi’s advice guide him, “Tell me something you can see. Anything at all.”

Caitlyn blinked at his words, confused, but lifted her head and let her gaze rake across the bathroom.  The lights were still off, but some streamed in through the gap beneath the door and illuminated it enough for her to just make out the marvel shower curtain.

For the first time since she’d locked herself behind that door, she huffed a laugh and was able to force the words out, though they came as a weak croak, “The shower curtain.” She didn’t have enough energy to gripe about it, but her response had been enough.

Jayce laughed, knowing full well his best friend would have words about his taste for bathroom décor later, “Good. That’s good, Sprout. You can do this,” he answered, voice still soft and low, “Now, what can you smell?”

Caitlyn felt her lungs opening back up slightly, breath still shaky but easier to come by.  She closed her eyes and focused, letting the smell of cedarwood and vanilla waft into her nostrils, “Your soap.”

“Yeah, that’s it. How are you feeling now?” Jayce asked, noticing the slight shift in her voice, steadier.

She sighed and pushed to her feet, feeling a bit wobbly.  Caitlyn inhaled deeply, letting her lungs inflate and then deflate before turning and pulling on the door handle.  It creaked open slightly, revealing Jayce standing there with a soft smile.

“Better, thank you.”  Caitlyn replied, cheeks tinged pink from embarrassment, “I’m sorry.” She apologized partially for the way she’d acted, but also because she was half-undressed, her trousers and blazer still in a heap on the floor. 

Jayce shook his head vehemently, “Don’t apologize, Sprout. Do you want to talk about it?”

They wandered back into the bathroom, where Caitlyn plopped on the unmade bed.  Jayce tossed her a throw blanket, which she accepted happily, snuggling beneath it.

“It’s just a lot all at once,” she admitted, eyes tracking Jayce’s movements while he ambled over to the closet to start hanging up the clothes from the duffle she’d packed in haste the night before.

“Well, you’ve never done well with a change in your routine. What you’re feeling is understandable,” he noted.

Caitlyn stared at the ceiling, letting the feeling of her back pressing into the mattress continue to ground her, “I just…my whole world was built around me instead of for me and then it was taken from me. I just want to feel like I’m in control for once, but I—I don’t know where to start.”

Jayce hummed at that, folding the last of her tee-shirts and placing it on the shelf.  When he finished, he sat in the chair at the desk and found her cerulean gaze, offering a small smile.

“I think you’ve already taken the first step, Cait. Coming here was entirely your decision, no one else’s.”

She sighed, “But wasn’t it a bit rash? I mean…I didn’t even tell anyone I was leaving.”

He couldn’t exactly bring himself to tell her that Cassandra had called him in the early morning hours that morning asking if he’d heard from Caitlyn, sounding worried sick and not at all scathing like he’d expected.  It wasn’t the right time for that.

“You did what you needed to do to protect yourself, Sprout.” Jayce replied, pushing to his feet and leaning over to pat her leg before turning on his heel, “Get some rest. I’ll be downstairs if you need anything.”

Caitlyn didn’t know how long she laid there after he left, staring at the ceiling, wondering what the future had in store for her.  But eventually, she was unable to fight the heaviness of her eyelids, falling asleep in the warm afternoon light streaming in through the blinds.

Vi’s bungalow sat just outside Luray on Stonyman Road, tucked beneath a canopy of yellow poplars and chestnut oaks that shielded it from the brunt of the sun’s UV rays. 

Late afternoon sunlight poured through the leaves overhead, scattering shifting patterns of gold across the weathered cedar siding as the breeze flitted through them.  The house wasn’t large, but every inch of it belonged to her.

She’d rebuilt the porch herself after a storm ripped out the railing and the joists for the stairs dry-rotted after years without proper sealant maintenance.  It took two summers to insulate the walls of the detached garage and hang up the sheet rock, while also running the wiring for the electric so that she could use half the space for her workshop.

Vi had nearly worked herself sick to afford the mortgage.

The house was close enough to Vander and Powder that she still felt like she belonged to something, but far enough away that it held her own independence, like she could close the front door and pretend the rest of the world didn’t exist for a while.

After the scouting run, Sarah had driven her home with the kayak strapped to the roof rack of her Crosstrek and old John Denver songs humming softly through the speakers.  The bartender stayed while she showered, and then somewhere between drying off and getting dressed, they’d fallen into bed and the afternoon slipped away from them entirely.

Now, the house settled around them in comfortable silence. 

The bedroom windows were cracked open, letting in the scent of sun-warmed grass and distant pinesap.  A box fan rattled lazily in the corner, pushing cool air across the room and stirring the sheer curtains.

Somewhere outside, cicadas buzzed in the trees, their droning chorus blending with the occasional high-pitched whistling chirp of a cardinal.

Sarah lay sprawled across Vi’s bed, one bare leg tangled in the sheet, fading sunlight painting her tattooed legs gold.  She was tracing the lines of the abstract, mechanical tattoos of Vi’s bare back, the woman beneath her humming contentedly under the touch.

“I should probably go,” Sarah said eventually, fingertips stilling their movements against Vi’s skin.  Her voice came out soft and tentative, and yet she made absolutely no attempt to move.

Vi smirked when she noticed the lack of movement on the mattress, “Could’ve fooled me.”

Sarah laughed, the sound filling the room like warm Tennessee whiskey, “My shift starts in forty minutes.”

“You say that like it’s my problem,” she replied, flipping over on her back and slipping an arm behind her head to support her neck.

The bartender sighed, “It becomes your problem when I get fired and you lose your watering hole, because we all know if Jericho starts pouring drinks, they’ll come out tasting like battery acid.”

Vi barked out a laugh, “Please. Everybody goes there to stare at you, not because you make the best drinks.”

Sarah propped herself up on one elbow, about to argue that she indeed made the best drinks in town considering she was the only one who actually gave a shit about flavor profiles when the rest of Vi’s words dawned on her.

“Oh?” A grin spread slowly across her face, “And what about you?”

Vi’s smirk faltered and she regretted opening that door.  The look in Sarah’s eyes made her chest tighten in a way she didn’t particularly care for.  

“That’s completely different.” Vi mumbled.

Sarah shook her head, smiling like she knew she caught Vi in her web, “How convenient.”

Gods, that smile was dangerous.

Vi turned away before she could stare too long and grabbed a pair of Levi’s from the floor.  The denim slipped over her Calvin Klein boxer briefs, settling low on her hips.  She tugged on a cropped, forest green Shenandoah University tee, the logo slightly worn at the edges.

Behind her, she heard the rustle of sheets as Sarah adjusted to get a better view of her.

“You’re staring,” Vi noted.

“I’d say I’m…admiring,” The bartender drawled, not even bothering to hide the way her green eyes raked over the exposed skin between the hem of Vi’s sweatshirt and her jeans.

Heat crawled up the back of Vi’s neck.  She hated how easy it was for Sarah to flirt and not even sound serious when she did.  That was the problem—everything came so naturally that she was afraid she’d lead the bartender on.

Vi looked up at the mirror above her dresser and grimaced; damp pink hair was untamed, mussed past the point of brushing it.  She dug through a drawer and emerged with an old, faded ballcap, shoving it onto her head backwards.

When she turned back to the bed, Sarah had gotten up and was pulling on her shorts, “Are you going to come keep me company?” She asked with hopeful eyes.

Vi’s smile faded as another thought surfaced, “I should probably go check on Jayce.”

Sarah tilted her head, “He probably just drank too much last night.”

“Nah, he’s been acting weird ever since he dipped out with that phone call.”

The bartender approached until she was standing right in front of her, placing her small hands on Vi’s shoulders, massaging them gently, “Will you text me when you’re home later?”

“Yeah, sure.” Vi shrugged it off as cool as she could.  She didn’t want this thing between them to become something more than what it had been up to that point.

The bartender narrowed her eyes but didn’t say anything about the way Vi brushed her off, “Alright, don’t get into any trouble.”

Vi huffed a laugh, “Yeah, right. Have a good shift.”

Sarah left the room, the sound of the front door opening came only a few seconds later, and Vi was alone again.

She took a breath, skin still tingling from where the bartender had touched her. 

Jayce’s cabin was only about a fifteen-minute drive down the backroads, over on Valley Burg Road closer to Lake Arrowhead.  Though, the drive took Vi longer than normal that afternoon thanks to a tractor on the road that could only go twenty miles an hour. 

She slowed her Wrangler to a stop on the driveway, pulling up behind Jayce’s Rav-4 and hopping out, her boots crunching against the dirt and gravel.  The sun was setting by then, painting streaks of purple and indigo across the sky. 

Vi could see the lights on inside the cabin, a fair sign that the Chief Ranger was home.  It was always quiet out here, so far deep into the woods.  She knocked softly against the front door, shifting anxiously on her feet as she waited for him to answer.

The door creaked open on its hinges, the blinds thudding softly against the glass inset window with the motion.  Jayce appeared, an expression of surprise and concern flicking across his features.

“Hey Vi, what are you doing here?” He asked, voice a low whisper.  The ranger looked over his shoulder once before turning back to her and stepping aside to let her in.

Vi’s eyebrows furrowed in confusion as she stepped through the threshold, and immediately she knew something was up.  The entire downstairs had been cleaned, the dirty laundry that usually lay scattered across the floor was picked up, and no dishes lingered in the sink.  The house smelled like jasmine and orange nectar from the candle that burned on the kitchen counter.

She smirked, “Dude, are you getting laid right now?”

Jayce’s thick eyebrows shot into his hairline, “What?! No!” He exclaimed, voice still quiet.

“What the fuck are you whispering for, then?!” Vi retorted, keeping her voice a whisper out of respect even though she didn’t know why they were talking like that.

He sighed and gestured for her to follow him to the kitchen, where she plopped down in a barstool as he pulled a couple of beers out from the fridge.  Jayce tossed her the bottle opener, “Okay, so…” he hesitated, took a sip of his beer, “I’ve talked to you about my friend Caitlyn, right?”

Vi huffed a laugh, “Yeah, Jayce. You talk about her all the time. Hotshot lawyer up in New York and bad ass free climber lady?”

Jayce nodded, “Well…long story short, she’s going through something right now and decided to take me up on my offer to come on as one of my Climbing Rangers for the season. Picked her up from the airport this morning.”

Ah, so that explained the Irish goodbye at the bar last night and the strange phone call earlier that afternoon.

Vi leaned back on the stool and rolled the cold beer bottle between her palms.  Truthfully, she knew very little about Caitlyn beyond whatever Jayce had volunteered over the years, which wasn’t nearly as much as people assumed considering how often he talked about her.

Certain details had stuck, though.

Caitlyn was Jayce’s age, somewhere around thirty-six, which made her roughly eight years older than Vi.  The fact that she was a lawyer in New York City made her seem even more adult than Vi was, someone who probably took herself too seriously.  She pictured someone impossibly put-together, all tailored blazers and color-coded planners, the sort of woman who looked like she belonged on the cover of a business magazine.

The image she had of her, though, directly contradicted the stories the ranger had told her about Caitlyn’s ability to free-climb routes with the ease of someone who’d climbed them a hundred times, even if it was her first attempt.  The woman was a complete enigma.

“She’s the one who was having the panic attack,” Vi said, more of a statement than a question.

“Cait’s had a rough twenty-four hours. She’s asleep in the guest room.” He replied, taking another sip of his beer.

Vi hummed, “I thought maybe you’d gotten back together with Viktor.” She laughed, shaking her head at the ridiculousness of the thought.

“Well, that would be the surprise of the century.” Called a soft, accented voice from the other side of the room, followed by the sweetest giggle that Vi was sure she’d ever heard.

She whipped her head around, nearly tumbling off the stool at the sudden appearance of another presence in the room.

There, by the stairs, Caitlyn Kiramman stood with rigid posture, her midnight hair damp against her skin and her tall, lithe frame draped in Jayce’s oversized Virginia Tech sweatshirt, the hem of which came down to her knees.  She looked nothing like the image Vi had conjured in her head of a perfectly put-together woman.

Instead, she looked…well, beautiful.  Even more so that she wasn’t hiding behind the mask of someone who’d argued legal cases for a living.

“Hey, Sprout. I didn’t even hear the shower going. How was your nap?” Jayce’s voice turned softer than Vi had ever heard it, clearly a tone he reserved only for his best friend. 

Caitlyn padded into the room in her socked feet, bringing with her the scent of vanilla and something floral—rose, if Vi had to guess.  The woman leaned her arms on the counter, hands swallowed by the large sleeves of the sweatshirt.

“It was good…I felt like I hadn’t slept in ages,” Caitlyn answered softly, like she was retreating from the attention.

Her cerulean eyes eventually made their way from counting the specks in the granite countertop up toward Vi, “I’m afraid we haven’t met…you’re Violet, right?”

Vi swallowed thickly when those blue eyes met hers, the sudden urge to drown in them so overwhelming that it almost felt like she’d fallen into the ocean for real.  And don’t get her started on the sound of her full name coming from that sweet accent.

“Just Vi, actually. It’s nice to finally meet you, Jayce has told me a lot about you.” She responded once she realized the room had gone quiet and the woman was expecting an answer.

Caitlyn hummed, “Likewise. From what I hear, you’re quite the beast on the river.”

That made Vi laugh, “Oh, I don’t know about that. It’s more like the river is the beast and I’m just trying to tame it."

“She’s being modest,” Jayce cut in, rolling his eyes playfully, “No one knows the Shenandoah River better than Vi Lanes. There’s a reason she’s my first call for any rescue.”

“Well, I think what you do is very admirable. I certainly would be terrified to be out on the water,” Caitlyn admitted, catching Vi’s gaze again.

Vi felt heat creep up her neck, suddenly feeling like she’d been put under the microscope.  She figured it was just the fact that she wanted to make a good impression since this was Jayce’s best friend, but something deeper told Vi that maybe she wanted to be friends with this woman.  Though, she didn’t think Caitlyn would reciprocate that feeling, considering the age difference.

“Not a fan of the water?” Vi asked, pushing the thoughts to the back burner in her mind.

Caitlyn shook her head, “No, I’m afraid not.”

She didn’t offer anything further, and Vi didn’t push.  She hopped off the barstool, figuring that she should probably give them some privacy, “That’s a shame. Let me know if you’re ever interested in changing your mind.”

The offer fell from her lips before she really even had time to think about it.  Vi let it linger there a moment before stepping toward the entryway and opening the front door, “Shenandoah has a lot to offer, I think you’ll like it here.” She said before disappearing outside.

Caitlyn’s gaze lingered where Vi had been standing just moments before, mind reeling over how easily the woman had offered those words of encouragement.  It was like she didn’t even realize how much it meant to Caitlyn.

Jayce caught his best friend’s hesitation, his hazel eyes darting between the door and where Caitlyn still perched against the counter.  He smiled to himself, realizing then that he would make it his summer goal to get those two women together.

“She’s hot, huh?” He chuckled, crossing his arms against his chest.

Caitlyn blinked, shifting her attention from the door to Jayce just in time to catch the mischief in his expression.  She scowled, ignoring the comment no matter how true it had been, “Are we going to eat dinner or am I just expected to starve?”

Jayce laughed at clearly having touched a nerve, shaking his head in amusement, “We don’t have any food here, so I figured we should probably go pick up some groceries. You down?”

“Fine,” Caitlyn mumbled, turning on her heel to head back upstairs and put on something more appropriate for a public appearance.

 

Notes:

Eeek! Our girls have met!!

Let me know what you think in the comments and press that kudos button if you enjoyed the story <3

Thanks for reading, stay tuned!

Chapter 3: Walkin' out the door with your bags

Summary:

A line of storms rolls through the Shenandoah Valley, stirring up emotions, fears, and revelations.

Notes:

This chapter is a little shorter because I've been dealing with a lot at work and my grandma has been in the hospital, but I hope it's enjoyable for you all the same! I had a really fun time writing this chapter and can't wait to share more.

Our girls interact a little more in this chapter, with more to come, so stay tuned! :)

(See the end of the chapter for more notes.)

Chapter Text

Several days passed and Vi had already gotten through the first guided whitewater rafting tour of the season, and still, neither she nor Powder had heard from Vander since he landed in New York.  While it wasn’t unusual for their adoptive father to go radio silent for hours-on-end during his business trips, days without a call from him was certainly out of the ordinary.

Vi was sitting in the front lobby of the LFA building, feet perched up on the counter and leaning back in a chair, precariously balancing her laptop on her elevated legs while sending out cancellation notices and refunds for her upcoming tour.

A front had moved in, bringing with it a stretch of storms that continued to wreak havoc on the Shenandoah Valley, raising water levels to unsafe conditions.  Vi hated cancelling on guests, but she’d prefer to lose out on the cash than have anyone lose their lives because she made the wrong call.

It was Vi’s biggest fear, and for good reason.

She hated storms.  As tough of an exterior she put up, the adventure guide had been afraid of storms ever since she lost her parents.  Any time a thunderstorm rolled through Shenandoah, Vi made it a point to stay put wherever she was—whether that was at the warehouse, the bar, or hell, even the grocery store.  She wouldn’t go outside until she was sure the last bolt of lightning had struck.

The door burst open with a gust of wind, pulling Powder into the room with a sudden jolt, “Ow, fuck!” the younger Lanes sibling exclaimed, struggling to push the door behind her until it shut out the force of the wind outside. 

Vi peered over the top of her laptop at the weather outside and frowned, “Looks like we’ll be stuck here awhile.”

“No way am I staying here all night just because you’re afraid of a thunderstorm, Vi.” Powder argued, reaching for her purse that was hanging on a hook in the corner.  She dug through the contents until she found her car keys and pulled them out, the keychain jingling softly in her hand.

Vi, ever the over-protective big sister, did not like the idea of Powder driving when the sky was about to open up any minute, “If you leave now, you’re going to get caught in the worst of it. Where are you going in such a rush?”

Powder rolled her eyes, “I’ve got plans with Ekko tonight if it’s okay with you, your highness.”

“No, seriously, I don’t want you going out there, Pow. Please?” At some point, Vi must’ve hopped off her stool, because she grabbed Powder’s arm in a firm grip before she could walk out the door.

Powder’s eyes fell to where Vi’s hand was wrapped around her wrist, silence settled thick between them like fog, until she raked her eyes back up to meet her sister’s.  Powder’s eyes were filled with quiet fury.  She ripped her arm away, “Just because you are still afraid because of what happened to Mom and Dad, doesn’t mean you can use your fear to control me. I’m not a little girl anymore, Vi. Let me go.”

The door slammed shut with a gust of wind before Vi could say anything else.  She was struck still, standing like a statue in the middle of the lobby like she’d been slapped across the face.

Powder’s last three words replayed in her head.

Let. Me. Go.

She knew her little sister hadn’t been talking about her arm.  No, it was deeper than that.  It was about their conversation a few days ago.  Powder wanted Vi to let go of her role as her guardian, not because she wasn’t needed anymore, but because she knew it was just as suffocating for Vi as it was for her.  Vi’s only role now that Powder was an adult was to be her sister. 

And Vi knew that her little sister couldn’t be expected to succeed if she was constantly holding her back because she was afraid, because she wanted to keep her close.  Because if Powder was close, then Vi could ensure nothing would ever happen to her.

But in reality, Vi also knew that it was impossible to guarantee Powder would be safe by her side.  She had no control over the universe or anything it could possibly bestow upon them, good or bad.  And Powder deserved this.  She deserved to live her life.

This realization, however, could stop the hurt from blooming in Vi’s chest. 

So, instead of dealing with her emotions, she sauntered through the door to the back offices, toward the fridge in the staff kitchen, where she grabbed a beer, popped the cap open, and chugged it down.

One thing that Caitlyn had noticed in the past couple of days was that Jayce’s cabin was impossibly loud when it stormed.  The torrential rain pounded against the roof, like a symphony of drums echoing directly in her ear canals. 

A small fire crackled lazily in the wood stove in the corner as she sat cross-legged in front of the couch, scribbling furiously on a notepad like a bat out of hell.  It’d been days since she arrived in Shenandoah, and her official orientation was coming up.  Caitlyn was determined to study the maps of the national park with as much fervor as she could muster. 

The last thing she wanted was to show up to orientation with zero knowledge of the park, its most prominent landmarks and its more well-known climbing routes.

Becoming a climbing ranger wasn’t simply a matter of being good at climbing.  Climbing Rangers with the NPS wore a dozen different hats throughout the season.  They assisted with search and rescue ops, inspected fixed anchors on popular climbing routes, educated visitors on climbing safety and wilderness ethics (like Leave No Trace guidelines), and occasionally spent long days patrolling cliff faces simply to make sure everyone came home in one piece.

It was demanding work that required technical expertise, sound judgement, and the ability to stay calm when things went wrong hundreds of feet above the ground.

Fortunately, Caitlyn had spent most of her adult life cultivating those skills.

She climbed all over the country, accumulating hundreds of hours on rock, ice, and alpine terrain.  Denali’s glaciers and brutal weather had tested her endurance.  Mount Rainier taught her the importance of rope management.  In Wyoming, she’d stood atop the Grand Teton at sunrise after a technical ascent, exhausted and exhilarated at the same time.

By any reasonable standard, Caitlyn operated at an expert level.  So, on paper, she shouldn’t have been nervous to take this on.  In reality, however, she was a ball of nerves.  The part that unsettled her wasn’t the climbing; it was the responsibility.

As a climbing ranger, all the skills she’d spent years working to refine would be tested, and it wouldn’t just be her life on the line.  Sure, she’d worked on SAR teams before, but she’d never led operations before.  Out there, on the summits of Shenandoah, Caitlyn would call the shots.  She just wasn’t sure if she was ready.

That’s why, to make up for what she lacked in confidence, she spent hours a day studying the routes and ridges of the Blue Ridge Mountains.

A particularly loud rumble of thunder jolted her attention from the map to the window over the kitchen sink, where everything outside was dark despite it barely being noon.  Caitlyn frowned, her brow creasing slightly as she thought about Jayce, hoping whatever he was out doing today that he was inside somewhere safe.

Her best friend was knee-deep in the beginning of his first summer season as Chief Ranger, which required long days at the station or out in the field, managing and implementing new operational programs, ensuring park visitor safety, training rookie rangers, coordinating with SAR teams, and reviewing conservation efforts within the park.  Needless to say, the man was busy.  Caitlyn had barely seen him at all the past few days.

She sighed, perching her pencil back behind her ear and stretching her long arms above her head.  The map had begun to blur in her eyes from staring at it too long anyway, so Caitlyn decided a break was needed and pushed up from her spot on the floor.

There wasn’t much to do in the cabin aside from watch TV or read a book, which was a stark contrast to her life in NYC, considering there was something to do on every block.  And yet, Caitlyn didn’t mind the boredom.

Actually, she found the quietude relaxing.  She’d gone for walks around Jayce’s property, at least, before it’d started raining the past couple of days.  White pines and dogwood trees filled the forest around her, and she took the time to really familiarize herself with the native plants of Shenandoah.

In the evenings, Jayce would teach her how to cook—a feat that was considerably more dangerous than any route she’d ever climbed.  In fact, Caitlyn was terrible at cooking.  In the city, there was never a need, with long hours in the office, cooking was not a skill she’d ever learned, instead relying on bodega breakfasts and Chinese carry-out.

She’d burnt quite a few chicken breasts the last several nights, much to her dismay and to Jayce’s amusement, but still, she loved learning how to do things for herself. 

Caitlyn was about to find something in the fridge she could attempt to make for lunch when the landline phone rang, the jarring sound echoing against the cabin’s cedar walls.

She ambled over, hesitating briefly, asking herself whether she should answer Jayce’s phone or not, but deciding she probably should since he was Chief Ranger and it could’ve been an emergency.

Pressing the receiver to her ear, Caitlyn cleared her throat, “Talis residence.”

A giggle crackled through the speaker and a vaguely familiar, though slurred, voice came through, mocking her accent, “Talis res-dence.” Followed by a hiccup.

Caitlyn frowned, mind reeling as she tried to remember why she recognized the voice, “Who is this?”

“Ouch, am I…am I really that easy to…to forget, Miss Kiramman?” The voice slurred again, the woman on the other side of the phone clearly drunk, Caitlyn realized.

She was about to respond when the woman interrupted again, “Don’ worry, s’ okay.” It was followed by yet another hiccup, and Caitlyn finally realized who it was.

“Violet?”

A warm chuckle, followed by an audible breath, “I told…you it’s—it’s Vi.”

Caitlyn sighed, wondering if it was a habit for Jayce’s friend to drink like this, “Vi, where are you right now?”

“Hmm, why d’ya wanna know?”

She pinched the bridge of her nose, inhaling a deep breath, “Because, Violet, you’ve rung Jayce’s phone in the middle of the day, clearly drunk. Are you alone?”

The air was thick with silence, the woman on the other line had gone quiet to the point that Caitlyn almost thought the call had dropped. 

But then, Vi’s voice came through, impossibly soft around the slurred edges, “Don’ like thunderstorms.” The wistful admission sounded melancholic to Caitlyn’s ears, her chest aching at the sound of it.

She didn’t even know what made her move, but Caitlyn stretched the corded landline as far as it would go, grabbing her sneakers by the front door and shoved her feet into them as she spoke, “Vi, where are you?” She asked again.

“’m at the adventure center,” Vi answered, sounding more distant this time.

Caitlyn grabbed the keys to Jayce’s old Camry, the car he’d said she could drive for the time being until she sorted her own life out.

“Stay put.” Was the last thing she said to Vi before she bounded out into the barreling winds and the wall of water.

Rain came down in sheets, slamming against the windshield and blocking her view of the road even with the wipers running at full speed.  If it weren’t for the clock on the dashboard reading 12:38pm, Caitlyn would’ve assumed it was well past midnight with how dark it was.

The ominous clouds overhead roared and rumbled, the car almost vibrating with the strength of it.  Every few minutes, bolts of lightning illuminated the road ahead of her as she drove mere single digits down the winding backroads towards the address she’d found on google. 

The Lanes Family Adventure Center was only about fifteen minutes down the road from Jayce’s cabin, but at the rate Caitlyn was driving, she’d get there tomorrow.  She didn’t even have a plan for what she’d do when she got there, but the sound of the younger woman’s voice on the other end of the phone was enough to stir something within her. 

Caitlyn felt almost as if it were her duty to check on Vi while Jayce was at work, like she knew inherently that he would’ve wanted her to go without having to even ask him.  He’d always mentioned how protective he felt of the pink-haired woman, considering her past—though Jayce never clued Caitlyn in on what that past might’ve been.

Thirty minutes later, she carefully pulled into the gravel lot outside of an old warehouse, which she thought looked more like an old auto-body shop than an adventure center that was currently in business, but she’d digress.

When she parked, the engine no longer idling under the din of the raging storm outside, Caitlyn noticed a single car parked over near the garage doors.  Probably Vi’s, she thought. 

She exhaled a breath, steadying herself and grabbing her purse before swinging the door open, slamming it shut with the force of the wind and sprinting toward the front door of the building.

Caitlyn huffed as the chiming door closed behind her, shutting out the roar of the storm.  She was soaked to the bone, huffing a laugh to herself as rainwater dripped from her clothes.  Of course, she’d never been known to carry an umbrella when she really needed it.

Her eyes scanned the lobby, finding the small room empty aside from a backpack hanging from a coat rack in the corner.

Caitlyn frowned, wondering briefly if Vi had left without her car somehow.  Maybe a partner picked her up?  Jayce never mentioned if the woman was single.  She shook that thought from her head almost immediately.

“Violet?” She called out, but no response came.

Cautiously, she checked behind the counter in case Vi was passed out asleep but quickly found that there was no one there.  Caitlyn sighed, about to pull out her cell to call Jayce when the sound of glass shattering echoed from behind a door in the back of the room.

Startled, Caitlyn ran and swung open the door, finding that it led to a hallway lined with a few offices.  She checked each one, peering her head through the doorways but finding the rooms empty. 

At the end of the hall, she could hear the crunch of glass in the last room, as if someone was stepping on broken pieces.  When Caitlyn stepped into the threshold, she realized it was the staff kitchen.

And in the middle of the room, Vi was there, swaying on her feet and staring down at a smashed bottle on the floor.

“Vi?” Caitlyn announced her presence as softly as she could, trying not to spook the pink-haired woman.

Red-rimmed grey eyes found hers, slightly glassy from the alcohol but still somewhat focused, like she was using Caitlyn’s gaze as an anchor.

For reasons she couldn’t explain, Caitlyn felt that look settle somewhere beneath her ribs.

A smirk lifted the corners of Vi’s lips, “Miss Kiramman,” she hiccupped, “I didn’t think you’d actually come.”

Warmth flickered through Caitlyn’s chest at that, the nickname more amusing than anything.  Her shoulders released the tension they’d held through the entire drive through the monsoon, and she was grateful that Vi was still here.

“Why do you keep calling me that?” Caitlyn asked, tilting her head with curiosity, approaching Vi carefully as if one wrong move would scare her away.

Vi shrugged lazily, “Dunno. Just seem like the type.”

Slowly, Caitlyn reached for her, a slender hand finding the spot between Vi’s shoulder blades.  Softly guiding the woman to a chair away from the shattered glass, “The type to what?” Her brows furrowed, trying to understand.

Even through the vintage crewneck she was wearing, Vi felt solid beneath her hand.  Strong with the sort of muscle that came from long days outdoors and years of using one’s body as a tool rather than something to simply exist within. 

“You know, the older authoritative type, ‘ssso mature.” Vi slurred as she was lowered onto the seat of the chair.  Her glassy eyes tracked Caitlyn’s movements, the blue-haired woman searching the room until she found the broom and dustpan.

Caitlyn huffed a laugh, “Older? Is that supposed to be an insult?” She asked, though not lacking in amusement.  She began cleaning up the broken glass while trying to keep her attention on the pink-haired woman.

Truthfully, that part was proving remarkably easy.

Vi shook her head, something she immediately regretted doing because it made her nauseous, “N-No,” she replied emphatically, “Just…hot.” She mumbled.

The broom stilled.

Heat rushed up Caitlyn’s neck so quickly it nearly startled her.  Of all the things she’d expected Vi to say, that certainly hadn’t been one of them.  Her gaze lifted from where she’d been tidying up the floor, finding Vi staring determinedly at the floor now, cheeks faintly flushed, looking considerably less smug than usual and far more vulnerable.

Caitlyn’s stomach flipped, which she immediately blamed on embarrassment and certainly not anything else.

Gods above, she’d known the woman for less than a week.

The thoughts were pushed to the back of her mind when she noticed that the other woman’s pallor had gone noticeably greener, like she was trying and failing to hide that she might be sick at any moment.  That made ignoring the comment significantly easier.

Caitlyn chose to say nothing, scooping up the last of the glass before grabbing a mop bucket from the same corner where she’d found the broom and dustpan and placing it by Vi’s feet.

“Do you drink alone very often?” She asked instead, moving through the staff kitchen like wind through the trees, with a sort of calm stillness that contrasted well with Vi’s inner turmoil. 

Caitlyn searched through the cabinets, finding a package of stale saltines that were still mostly edible, and pulled a bottle of water out from the fridge.

Behind her, she could feel Vi watching, oddly aware of the younger woman’s attention.

Vi grunted, grey eyes locking onto Caitlyn’s again as the blue-haired woman kneeled in front of her, “I told you…I hate thunderstorms.”

Being this close made it difficult not to notice things, like the constellations of freckles that scattered across Vi’s nose, the matching pair of scars that cut across her eyebrow and her top lip.  The small VI tattoo high on her cheekbone.

Caitlyn promptly reminded herself that she had recently discovered her girlfriend of four years cheating on her and was in absolutely no position to be cataloguing another woman’s features.

“Will you tell me why?” Caitlyn knew there was some deeper meaning to the sentiment, something that floated in the undercurrent, just beneath the surface.  She patted Vi’s knee, offering the crackers and glass of water.

Vi accepted both with a curious expression, then shook her head, “No.”

The answer was firm, but her grey eyes softened when she explained further, “I don’t talk about it.”

There it was again, the sudden glimpse beneath Vi’s bravado.

“So, you just…drink about it instead, I reckon?” Caitlyn stood, smoothing the invisible wrinkles of her sweatshirt.

“Did you come here to lecture me, Miss Kiramman?” Vi asked with the same melancholic voice that Caitlyn had heard on the phone earlier.

She shook her head, “No, Violet. I—I thought you might need a friend, and as it turns out, so do I.” The admission feeling painfully honest, far more so than she usually allowed herself to be.  Her voice came out low and timid, fingers fidgeting together as she swallowed thickly.  Because that was the real reason she’d come, wasn’t it?

“You want to be friends?” A smirk grew on Vi’s face, a warmth washing over her expression.

The sight pulled an answering smile from Caitlyn before she could stop it.  It was unfair, really.  Vi smiled like sunshine breaking through the densest of storm clouds.

“Don’t make me say it again, I’m already utterly mortified.”

Vi chuckled, “Oh, come on. Being friends with me can’t be that embarrassing.” She teased.

“No! Gods, no that’s not what I—I only meant that…well, I—” Caitlyn stumbled over her words, exasperated and horrified.  She stopped mid-sentence when Vi laughed again, the sound warm like honey to Caitlyn’s ears, a feeling she definitely wouldn’t acknowledge right now.

“Chill, Caitlyn, I know what you meant.” Vi admitted, wiggling her eyebrows to let the blue-haired woman know she’d only been kidding.

Caitlyn exhaled a breath of relief, nodding slightly, “Okay, friends.”

“Friends,” Vi repeated with a mischievous grin. “Now, uh, would you mind driving me home?”

Caitlyn laughed.  A loud, unbidden sound that came deep from her diaphragm, “Yeah, sure.”

As it turned out, Vi wasn’t exactly the best at giving directions while drunk.

Somehow, Caitlyn had managed to guide a stumbling Vi out to the Camry, only once the storm had fully stopped raging at the woman’s fervent insistence. 

But when it came time to drive Vi home, the pink-haired woman simply couldn’t remember how to get there.  Her eyebrows pinched together and her freckled nose scrunched up in thought, trying to figure out which turns to take.  Caitlyn thought she looked cute like that.

It became evident relatively quickly that there was no way they were making it to Vi’s bungalow anytime soon, and the longer this went on, the sicker Vi started to look.  So, Caitlyn made the executive decision to take the woman back to Jayce’s.

That’s where Vi lay now, passed out with her face smushed in a pillow on the Chief Ranger’s couch, an arm hanging loosely off the side of it.

Slowly, the stench of something burning began to seep into her consciousness, stirring her awake much sooner than she probably would’ve wanted.  But the smell only got stronger, and Vi couldn’t ignore the survival instinct that made her open her eyes to make sure she wasn’t caught in the middle of a fire.

She lifted her head off the pillow, orienting herself.  Her head felt heavy as a rock, and everything felt like it was spinning.  It didn’t take long for Vi to recognize that she was in Jayce’s cabin, but how the hell did she get there?

There wasn’t enough time to answer that question before she felt that familiar tightening in her chest, the way her mouth gathered saliva, a sign of what was to come at any moment.  Thankfully, Vi spotted the bucket on the floor just in time for her stomach to release its contents with a rough, hacking sound.

Well, if it wasn’t the consequences of her own actions.

Vi didn’t register the presence of someone else in the room; not until a soft hand was rubbing soothing circles on her back.

“Do you think you can manage a glass of water?” That newly familiar accented voice came from somewhere next to her.

When she looked up, she found those cerulean eyes looking back at her, full of concern and something else Vi couldn’t name.  She was close, probably close enough to smell her nasty breath, which left her feeling embarrassed and a bit horrified.  Pushing herself up, trying to put distance between them, Vi nodded.

“Yeah,” her voice hoarse, “please.”

Caitlyn frowned at the way the woman pulled away from her but didn’t move to acknowledge it.  Instead, she simply stood and crossed the room, filling up a glass and bringing it back over and kneeling in front of Vi again.

“Try this,” Caitlyn kept her voice low as to not agitate Vi’s hangover, “small sips.”

Their fingers brushed when Vi accepted the glass, the contact brief but sparking a flame within her all the same, “Thanks,” she croaked, cheeks flushing with either heat or embarrassment.  Vi chose to blame it on the latter, considering she’d just vomited in front of the woman.

She took a small sip, enough to wash down the acidic taste in her mouth.  A beat passed and the stench that’d woke her up seeped in again, “Is something burning?”

Caitlyn sighed, worrying her bottom lip between her teeth, “I...was trying to make you some eggs and rice to help your hangover but I’m afraid I never learned how to cook, and the rice is sort of…stuck…to the bottom of the pot.”

Vi chuckled, “Wow, Miss Kiramman, I would’ve assumed a woman as mature as yourself would at least know to turn the heat down to a simmer.”

She wouldn’t acknowledge the fact that she teased the woman with that nickname just to get a reaction out of her, nor would she admit that watching Caitlyn roll her eyes and grin at her made her stomach flutter, and not from nausea.

“You ought to figure out something else to call me,” Caitlyn retorted, though she couldn’t help but smile at the sound of the nickname.

Vi scrolled through her mental rolodex for a list of names.  Your Highness, Councilor, Mommy… Okay, so she definitely couldn’t say the latter without completely crossing a line.  But she had to admit, it sounded nice in her head…

“Hmm, I’m sure I’ll come up with something.” Vi replied instead.

A comfortable silence fell between them, their gazes lingering for a beat too long than what was probably necessary.  Vi could feel her heart beating beneath her ribs.  They were so close that she could just make out the tiniest freckle on Caitlyn’s jawline and the cutest gap between her two front teeth she hadn’t noticed before.

Vi cleared her throat, “I should probably go.”

Caitlyn swallowed the disappointment that bubbled up.  She didn’t want Vi to go yet, having gotten her hopes up that maybe she’d stay and watch a movie or something.  Instead, she stood, giving the woman some space.

“Right, of course. I’ll just grab the keys…” Caitlyn’s posture went perfectly rigid, voice polite and neutral, not giving anything away.

Staring at something on her phone, her thumbs typing quickly, Vi mumbled, “I have a ride.”

When she finally looked up from her screen, she caught the flicker of disappointment that crossed Caitlyn’s face before the woman smoothed out her expression, nodding, “Oh, well, get home safe, Violet. And do try to keep the drinking to a minimum.”

Vi frowned, replaying the last few minutes of their interaction in her mind, wondering what she said wrong to make the older woman turn cold as ice.  Her phone screen illuminated with a text notification, a confirmation that her ride was a minute away.

She walked toward the front door, hands in her pockets, when she stopped and looked over her shoulder, “Do…” Vi hesitated, mulling the words over in her mind before continuing, “Do you want to hang out tomorrow? My hiking tour got cancelled because of the rain, so, I could give you a tour of the caverns, if you want.”

Caitlyn’s breath caught in her throat, her cerulean eyes widening at the invitation.  No amount of self-preservation could’ve kept her from reacting.  She smiled softly at Vi, nodding, “Y-yes! I mean, that sounds great, Vi.”

Vi grinned, feeling warmth spread in her chest as she took one last look at Caitlyn, lingering only a second before leaving.

Still stunned at how her day had turned out, Caitlyn watched out the window above the kitchen sink as Vi hopped into the passenger seat of a Subaru Crosstrek.

The rain slowed to a soft mist by the time the sun went down, the moon breaking the horizon and covering everything in a subdued silver light.  Low jazz music was playing from the YouTube app on the television, a welcome replacement for the raucous torrential downpour.

Cinnamon, turmeric, and coriander filled the air, wafting from the sizzling pots on the stove.  Jayce had returned to the cabin about an hour ago, his nose scrunching as he walked in the door and smelled the lingering stench of burnt rice.

Now, with Caitlyn permanently banned from cooking in his kitchen unsupervised, the Chief Ranger was making his favorite meal: Moroccan spiced chicken and mesfouf. 

His best friend watched from where she perched on a barstool at the kitchen island, arms swallowed by Jayce’s oversized sweatshirt again, a pair of his sweatpants hanging loosely from her hips.  Her blue hair was pulled back in a messy ponytail, and she leaned on one elbow as she listened to the man talk about his day.

“Oh, and get this!” Jayce exclaimed, emphasizing his words with a wave of the tongs, “Steb, I think I’ve mentioned him before…he’s my deputy ranger? Anyways, he saw Viktor out by Dark Hollow Falls with some lab equipment and a new lab partner the other day.” He scoffed, shaking his head.

Okay, so Jayce and Viktor had dated for just under a year before breaking up a couple months ago.  Apparently, Viktor was too “married” to his work to pursue a long-term relationship and kind of left Jayce out to dry, so-to-speak.

But Caitlyn always had a hunch that there was more to it.  She didn’t tell Jayce that, though.

“Did Steb say what he looked like?” Caitlyn asked, letting herself get lost in the gossip about her best friend’s personal life, rather than her own.

Jayce scoffed, “I’m sure he looks the same. Sometimes I imagine if he let his hair grow long. Or if he has stubble now…” His thoughts trailed off, voice wistful, like he was actually trying to picture it in his head.

Caitlyn shook her head, “Not Viktor, you idiot! The new lab partner.” She chuckled.

He turned over his shoulder and glared at her, “Why do you want to know?” He asked with a level of suspicion that could only make her laugh.

“Because I need to know what he looks like so that when I’m out patrolling the cliffs, I know who to shove off of them.”

That made Jayce laugh.  A real, boisterous, bellowing laugh.

“You’re scary sometimes, Sprout.” Jayce replied, his abs sore from laughing.

Caitlyn shrugged, grinning widely at him.

He turned around again and slipped the meat thermometer into the chicken, nodding when it read 165F.  It took only a few more minutes for him to plate everything and carry it to the dining room table, where they sat in comfortable silence.

The clinking of cutlery and the occasional hums of pleasure were the only sounds that could be heard for several minutes, until Caitlyn felt Jayce’s eyes burrowing into her soul from across the table.

She placed her fork down quietly, dabbing her lips gently with the napkin before acknowledging him, “What is it?”

“I stopped by earlier to eat a quick sandwich for lunch and you weren’t here,” he replied simply, one of his eyebrows quirking upward like he was asking a question but didn’t need to ask it out loud.

Caitlyn breathed a sigh of relief, however, “Oh, that,” she scooped another bite of the mesfouf onto her fork, “Violet called the landline and sounded like she needed someone…So, when I drove to her family’s business, I found her drunk out of her mind.” She shrugged, continuing to eat like it was enough of an explanation. 

Jayce’s eyes widened, “Fuck, I totally forgot about the storms. I really appreciate you going to check on her, Cait. That was the best thing you could’ve done in that scenario.”

Of course, he was concerned about his friend considering he knew Vi’s history with thunderstorms and how she was afraid of them, and it truly was a good thing that Caitlyn had been there.  But he also couldn’t help but smile to himself at the thought of his two friends hanging out more.

Caitlyn sighed, “Are you going to tell me whatever this…thing is with Vi about thunderstorms?”

He shook his head, shoving a forkful of chicken in his mouth and talking with his mouth full, “Nah, I can’t. Not my place.”

She didn’t argue with that, considering she was fighting her own demons, Caitlyn could respect someone else’s.

“Is it normal for her to get that drunk in the middle of the workday?” She asked instead.

Jayce’s movements slowed, he took his time swallowing the mouthful of food before answering, “It’s not as common as you’d expect. Caitlyn hummed at that, pushing the last bite of food around on her plate, deep in thought.

“She offered to take me on a tour of the caverns tomorrow,” she mumbled, barely audible, like she hadn’t really meant to say it out loud.

Jayce stilled, “Oh?”

She looked up, catching his mischievous grin and narrowing her eyes at him with suspicion, “What?”

He chuckled, shaking his head, “Nothing. Are you gonna go?”

“Y-yes, I think so. We um…talked about being…friends. It would be nice to have someone else to talk to while I’m here.” Caitlyn explained with a small, shy smile.

Caitlyn couldn’t help the warmth that spread in her chest nor the slight pink tinge in her cheeks, “I’m…looking forward to it.” She replied.

At thirty-six years old, she’d thought her life was settled.  The sort of life that became more difficult to change with every passing year.

And yet, here she was, living out of a duffle bag in her best friend’s cabin.  Learning how to cook at an age when most people seemed to have already figured that sort of thing out.  Starting an entirely new career, studying maps late into the night like a college student cramming for finals.  Making plans to spend tomorrow wandering through caverns with a woman she’d only known for a few days.

If someone had described this version of her life a month ago, Caitlyn would’ve laughed in their face.

Her heartbreak still lingered beneath everything.  It followed her into the quiet moments and surfaced unexpectedly whenever she thought too long about New York.  Some mornings, she still woke up confused, reaching instinctively for a life that no longer existed. 

But alongside the grief, something else had begun to take root.  Despite having lost so much, it felt as though her world was expanding again.

Jayce sat with her every evening, droning on with ridiculous stories from the field and giving her an endless supply of encouragement.  Then, of course, there was Vi’s stubborn kindness wrapped around her like a hug. 

It felt like the beginnings of community, somewhere she felt at ease because they wanted her there, and not because she fulfilled some sort of expectation or role.

“Your mother called again this afternoon.”

Caitlyn groaned, “What did you tell her?”

He sighed, “I told her that you call me once a day to check in, but that I don’t know where you’ve gone.”

But all Caitlyn knew was that she wanted to establish herself in Shenandoah before calling them.  She wanted to sink her roots into the soil here so that no one could try to uproot her.

“Thank you,” she murmured, worrying her bottom lip between her teeth.

Jayce seemed to soften then, “Listen, Cait. I’m here for you and I wouldn’t do anything against your wishes.”

She eyed him, “But…?”

“But,” Jayce continued, rubbing the back of his neck, “I don’t know how much longer I can keep lying to her. You know how your mother is.” He pleaded.

Caitlyn stared down into her wine glass, watching the deep burgundy liquid shift as she turned it slowly. 

Of course she knew how her mother was.

Cassandra Kiramman had spent Caitlyn’s entire life steering her toward what she believed was success.  Every decision had been made with love, she knew that much, but that love often arrived tangled in the heaviest of expectations.

If she called her mother, Caitlyn was afraid that she’d fold beneath that familiar pressure.  Cassandra would hear uncertainty in her voice, hear the heartbreak and impulsiveness and vulnerability.  She might get on the next flight back to New York simply because it is easier than disappointing the people who cared about her—and that would destroy her.

All Caitlyn wanted was the chance to call her mother when she had developed a sense of independence, once she had built something to be proud of.  When she could finally say, this is my life, I chose it, and I’m staying.

“I know,” Caitlyn admitted at last, “I know I can’t avoid her forever.”

Their lips smacked together, warm and wet and full of wanton desire that could only be satiated by no-strings-attached sex.

“F-fuck,” Sarah huffed when Vi’s lips traced the shell of her ear.

Vi grinned against her flushed skin, “You like that, Fortune?”

“Shut up, Lanes,” the bartender fisted the hair at Vi’s nape, pulling her head away from her neck so that she could kiss her lips again.

Vi felt herself get lost in the push and pull of it, the way Sarah’s soft lips collided with her own, the way she could feel the heat radiating between their hips.  It was a perfect distraction from a certain smooth, accented voice and cerulean eyes that felt deeper than an ocean.  Midnight blue hair that looked so, so soft.

The bartender’s face twisted into a look of confusion and concern, her kiss-swollen lips still parted like Vi’s would find hers again any second.

Vi was pacing the living room, occupying the space between the couch and the coffee table, muttering something unintelligible to herself while Sarah replayed what just happened in her head, trying to figure out what went wrong.

Cautiously, she approached Vi, placing a hand on her shoulder, “Vi? What happened?”

She sighed, shoulders sagging as she looked into those familiar green eyes, which held so much patience that Vi didn’t think she deserved, “I’m sorry, I think it was just too emotional of a day and I’m in my head.”

Sarah shook her head, “Don’t apologize, I…I don’t want just sex from you, Vi.”

“What?” Vi’s brow furrowed, anxiety bubbling up in her stomach at the soft expression on the bartender’s face.

“Well, I was sort of hoping we could, I don’t know, put a name to this.” Sarah began, voice low and hesitant, timid in a way that was self-conscious, which was something Vi had never heard from the woman before.

She was about to ask what the bartender meant when Sarah held up a hand, stopping Vi in her tracks, “We already hang out almost everyday and the sex is amazing, but…I don’t know, I guess I want more. I want you to be my comfort and I want to be the same for you.”

Vi felt like her heart was going to stop at any second.  Her biggest fear was coming true, and with the one person she thought she didn’t have to worry about catching feelings or wanting something more. 

Especially now, when all Vi could think about was the Kiramman woman.

Sarah was staring at her now, eyes glimmering in the low lamplight of her living room, a look of hope on her features.  She was beautiful, there was no doubt about it.  The bartender deserved to be with someone that wanted more with her, someone that could make her happy.  But Vi knew it wasn’t her.

She inhaled a deep breath, steadying her nerves, “I—”

Chapter 4: Wanna hear your history until you don't wanna talk

Summary:

In the aftermath of a decision, Vi takes Caitlyn on a tour of Luray Caverns.

Notes:

It turns out writing is easy when you're sitting in a hospital room with nothing else to do!

In all seriousness, thank you all for the kind words and support for this fic so far, it means the world to me. Especially now with everything going on with my grandma, the distraction is welcomed <3

That said, I hope you all enjoy this chapter! We get to know our girls a little bit more and explore their dynamic. This was a fun one to write and I hope it makes up for last chapter's cliffhanger ;)

(See the end of the chapter for more notes.)

Chapter Text

“I think we should stop seeing each other.”

The words fell from Vi’s lips like a waterfall over a cliff—quick and inexorable.

She felt a stone sink in her stomach as she watched the confession register in Sarah’s expression.  The bartender looked like she’d been slapped, her lips parted slightly in surprise and her green eyes shimmered with tears.

Vi wanted to reach for her, to comfort her with a hand on her shoulder, but she kept her arms glued to her sides, fists trembling slightly.  No amount of physical comfort would do them any good right now.

Whatever heat had been between them just froze over in a sheet of ice.  Vi could almost see her breath with how cold the air had gotten.

“What?” Sarah’s voice was so small and shaky, like the earth itself had collapsed beneath her.

Vi bit her cheek, trying to keep her composure, “I don’t want more, Sarah. I’m sorry.”

“W-wait, then let’s just…let’s just go back to the way things were! I—I can handle that, Vi. We don’t have to stop.” The bartender rushed forward, arms falling to Vi’s hips.  Desperate to hold onto something she knew was slipping away.

Breaking someone’s heart like this was never easy, and it was exactly why Vi vowed never to pursue a true, committed relationship.  She just didn’t have it in her to let anyone get that close, for her own sake and theirs.

Vi sighed and reached up, placing a hand on Sarah’s cheek, the pad of her thumb rubbing circles against her skin. “We can’t go back, Fortune. Not when I know now that I’d be leading you on.”

Tears slowly dripped down the bartender’s face then, quickly wiped away by an attentive thumb.  Sarah didn’t say anything else, just buried her face against the crook of Vi’s neck, inhaling the scent of her.

“I’m sorry. I’m so sorry. I never meant to hurt you,” Vi apologized, wrapping an arm around the bartender’s shoulders.

Sarah pulled away, wiping away the moisture on her cheeks with the back of her hand.  She shook her head, green eyes downcast, unable to meet Vi’s, “You were clear about what you wanted, I just…I let my feelings blind me and got my hopes up.”

“We can still be friends, though, right?” Vi felt sick to her stomach.

The silence that stretched between them lingered like a shadow, the absence of words a quiet presence that they shouldn’t have been able to sense but did.

Sarah’s eyes found Vi’s then, and they said more than words ever could, yet her voice came all the same, “I—I think I just need some time…” she whispered, with a solemn finality that crushed Vi’s insides, like she’d gotten stuck in a winch.

Vi stepped back, accepting that the distance between them was only growing further as time went on, “Okay, yeah. As much time as you need.” She croaked, biting her cheek even harder so that the physical pain drowned out the emotional.

“Bye, Lanes,” Sarah muttered as she turned and opened the front door, looking over her shoulder once before letting the door close behind her.

“Bye, Fortune.” Vi mumbled when the door closed, sealing the decision she’d made with a soft thunk.

It wasn’t just that she’d rejected the prospect of a relationship with someone that genuinely had made Vi perfectly happy.  It was the fact that she was also losing the friendship she’d valued so much.

Sure, Sarah had just said she needed time.  But Vi saw that look in her green eyes, she saw the hurt there that she knew wouldn’t simply go away at the drop of a hat.  Vi knew that rejection would drive a wedge between them that she’d never be able to pry loose again.

Vi couldn’t help but question herself.  Had she honestly ended whatever it was that she had with the bartender for the simple fact that she found Caitlyn Kiramman attractive?  Or was she truly simply afraid of the commitment that a relationship required?

She flopped on the couch in the living room, feeling dizzy under the constant whirlwind of questions that kept spinning through her head.  It felt like she’d been sucked into a whirlpool and was just waiting to be spat back up to the surface.

She stared at herself in the mirror, unblinking, for what seemed like an hour before Jayce appeared behind her, grinning at her reflection, mischief lining his features.

Caitlyn had barely slept the night before, her body buzzing with excitement and anxiety for her day out with Vi.  Jayce had talked about the natural grandeur of Luray Caverns and what to expect over dinner the night before, but she’d stayed up late reading everything she possibly could about them, increasing her knowledge infinitesimally.

It’s not that she didn’t trust Vi to give her a proper tour or to explain things, she just liked to go into new situations as prepared as possible.  It helped her feel at ease.

Which is definitely not how she felt now, standing over the guest vanity and staring at the bags under her eyes from the lack of sleep.

“Nervous for your date?” Jayce chuckled.

Caitlyn’s eyebrows shot up into her hairline, “It’s not a date!” She exclaimed.

Her pulse pounded in her eardrums at the thought of it.  There were several reasons why she wouldn’t be caught dead going on a date with anyone, let alone Vi, someone who was younger and cooler and probably not single.  The first being the fact that she’d just broken up with Charlie.

Caitlyn was on a mission to find herself and rediscover her independence.  Any sort of romantic involvement would certainly jeopardize that.  Plus, she and Vi had clearly said they were friends, and it wasn’t like the other woman showed interest in being anything other than that.

“Right,” Jayce replied with a sarcastic edge in his tone, unable to keep from smirking.

She rolled her eyes, ignoring his antics as she finally started to put on her makeup.  She never laid it on too thick, just dabbed some light concealer under her eyes, brushed the tiniest hint of blush on her cheeks, and painted on the faintest of smokey eyes.

Her routine took maybe fifteen minutes, if that.

When she walked back into the bedroom to get dressed, Jayce had plopped down in the chair in the corner, “Seriously, Cait, are you ever going to date again?”  He asked, still in that teasing tone.

“Jayce, I just got cheated on. Why do you think I would want anything to do with dating right now?” She asked, slipping her sleep shirt over her head and tossing it in the direction of the bed, but not caring whether it actually landed there or not.

Ever since moving in with her best friend, Caitlyn found herself caring less and less about cleanliness or being perfectly organized.  It was something that Jayce had noticed, a bit concerned about his friend’s mental wellbeing but hadn’t commented on yet.  He’d simply keep a closer eye on her for any other worrying developments in the wake of her breakup.

“You seem to be doing that well enough without my help,” he jeered.

“She’s here,” Jayce noted, “Take your time, I’ll keep her busy.”

“Yeah,” her voice cracked, causing her to blush with embarrassment before she cleared her throat, “Let’s do this, it’ll be fun.”

Exhaustion lingered beneath Vi’s grey eyes and her smile appeared a fraction too quickly, like she was simply hiding behind a mask to appease those around her.  Caitlyn also recognized it because she herself has put on that same charade.

It reminded her of the version of herself that existed back in New York.  The one who showed up to work every morning looking perfectly composed while quietly falling apart beneath the surface.

Part of her wanted to ask, to gently prod at Vi’s walls and figure out what she kept hidden inside.  But they had only just become friends, and she knew all too well how uncomfortable it felt when someone tried to pry open wounds you weren’t ready to share.

She raised an eyebrow, “What makes you think I have intentions?”

Jayce chuckled, shaking his head, “Because I know you, Vi, and I know you’re not blind. She’s beautiful, but she’s been hurt enough. I’m not saying you shouldn’t go for it, I’m just asking for you to be careful with her.”

Vi rolled her eyes, “We’re just friends, Jayce. I’m not thinking about that.”

Deep down, she knew it was a lie.  She was thinking about it.  But Vi was also realistic—she knew Caitlyn was going through something, she knew that the woman probably wasn’t interested in her, or in any sort of relationship that was anything more than friendship.  Especially considering their age difference.

If Caitlyn Kiramman wanted to date someone, then she’d probably want someone older and more sophisticated than Vi.  She was too brash, too rough around the edges.  Vi was like a wild horse roaming free in the vast wilderness.  She didn’t bring stability or commitment.

Soft, golden lights illuminated the formations from below, making them glow against the darkness of the caverns.  The sound of steady dripping water somewhere in the distance seemed to echo forever.

Caitlyn stared upward, utterly transfixed while the rest of the world vanished from her periphery.  Beside her, Vi watched the wonder spread across Caitlyn’s face and found herself smiling despite everything weighing on her shoulders ever since the night before.

“So?” Vi asked softly.

Vi’s attention, however, remained keyed in on the older woman rather than the surrounding rock.  While most tourists simply looked around briefly awestruck, Caitlyn was clearly studying the caverns.

“This whole place started forming millions of years ago,” Vi explained as they followed the winding path, “back when this area was covered by a shallow inland sea.”

Caitlyn tore her eyes away from a massive column that reminded her of the flying buttresses of Notre Dame, “A sea?” she asked, eyes wide with disbelief.

“Yep,” Vi smirked, amused by the older woman’s expression. “Hard to picture, isn’t it?”

“A little.” Caitlyn giggled, suddenly feeling flustered at just how much Vi seemed to enjoy talking about this place.

Vi gestured to the walls around them, “All this limestone came from the remains of marine organisms. Tiny shells, coral, all sorts of things settled on the seafloor over millions of years.”

Caitlyn hummed thoughtfully, “So, the rock itself is essentially…compressed biological matter?”

The younger woman chuckled, “See? This is why I like talking to you.”

She blinked, “What? Why?” Caitlyn tried to ignore the butterflies that fluttered in her stomach when Vi complimented her.

“Most people just nod politely and spend the whole tour wondering where the gift shop is,” Vi replied with a genuine smile.

A laugh escaped Caitlyn’s lips, completely unabashed, “Sorry for being interested.”

Vi shook her head, chuckling softly with her, “Oh, no, it’s weirdly refreshing.”

They continued walking, a few tourists passed them going the opposite direction, but neither woman paid much attention.  Caitlyn stopped beside a particularly peculiar curtain-shaped formation hanging from the ceiling.

“The water is still shaping it, isn’t it?” She asked, fingers perched on her chin in thought.

Vi grinned, “It is,” she answered, stepping beside her and leaning her elbows against the railing, which overlooked the rest of the caverns below, “The cave isn’t finished growing. Every drop of water that comes through the limestone above absorbs a tiny amount of carbon dioxide from the soil.”

Caitlyn nodded, “Forming carbonic acid.”

Vi blinked, “You…already knew that.”

The older woman chuckled, “Maybe…”

“Maybe?” Vi repeated with a quirked brow and an amused smirk.

Caitlyn tried and failed to hide her smile, “I may have spent some time reading about the caverns last night…” she admitted.

Vi barked out a laugh, the sound echoing against the cavern walls, “You studied before a casual cave tour?”

In Caitlyn’s defense, it had sounded perfectly reasonable when she’d done it, “Preparation is important.”

“Gods, you’re such a nerd,” Vi teased, nudging Caitlyn’s shoulder with her own. 

From anyone else, the words would’ve sounded insulting, but from Vi they came out oddly affectionate, bringing heat to Caitlyn’s cheeks.  Fortunately, Vi had already turned toward the next formation before noticing.

“The carbonic acid dissolves tiny amounts of limestone,” Vi continued. “Over enough time, it creates cracks, tunnels, entire chambers…”

Caitlyn cut in, “And the stalactites?”

“Minerals get deposited when the water drips from the ceiling,” Vi answered in kind.

She watched the older woman as she studied a cluster hanging overhead, “So each drip leaves behind a microscopic amount?” Caitlyn asked.

Vi nodded, “Exactly.”

“How long does it take them to grow?” Their eyes met as Caitlyn looked back over to the younger woman, cerulean and grey colliding in mutual fascination.

“Now that’s the cool part,” Vi pushed the feelings that’d bubbled up in her throat back down her esophagus, instead focusing on explaining the science behind the caverns.  It was a good distraction, “Most only grow about an inch every hundred years.”

Caitlyn’s eyes widened in astonishment, “A hundred years?!” she exclaimed.

Vi shrugged, “Give or take.”

“That’s extraordinary.” The older woman replied wistfully.

Vi thought that was true, but not of the stalactites.  No, because in all honesty, she couldn’t care less about the stupid rocks or the compressed biologic matter or the vast expanse of caverns.  All she could focus on was the way Caitlyn’s eyes lit up even down here beneath the earth, where the sun’s rays never even reached.  The way her breath caught each time she stumbled across something else that caught her attention.  Caitlyn Kiramman herself was extraordinary.

And Vi was so fucked.

Eventually, they crossed a small bridge spanning one of the deeper pools.  The surface reflected the formations above like black glass, making it difficult to tell where the cave ended and the water began.

“You know,” Vi hummed casually, “some people say these caverns have a really rock-solid history.”

Caitlyn groaned, “Oh, Violet. That was terrible.”  Though she couldn’t stop the laughter from breaking the silence of the pool chamber they were standing in, just the two of them—it seemed as though the rest of the tourists had all but disappeared.

Vi joined the fit of laughter, her cheeks hurting from smiling so much today, “There’s more where that came from.”

Ignoring her, Caitlyn walked further, past the pools and through a narrow tunnel that led to the biggest chamber that they’d seen on the entire tour.  The ceiling soared overhead, taller than any grand foyer she’d ever seen.  She stopped dead in her tracks, mouth agape as she stared upward, feeling so inconsequentially small in that moment.

For a beat, they simply stood in the middle of the cavern, silence settling over them.  Their breathing seemed to sync up, standing shoulder to shoulder under the soft amber glow.  Vi thought she could almost see tears forming on Caitlyn’s waterline.

“You keep doing that,” she noted, voice soft rather than accusatory.

Caitlyn slowly found Vi’s grey eyes, “Doing what?”

“Looking like someone just showed you actual magic.” The younger woman replied, her voice suddenly breathless, like she herself had found magic in simply watching her.

Caitlyn fell quiet, then smiled and said, “This feels like magic.”

The admission came so softly that Vi almost missed it.  Neither of them spoke after that, the chamber humming with distant echoes and dripping water.  This felt easy for them, like being in each other’s company was as natural as the caverns themselves.

Eventually, Caitlyn looked toward a massive column that connected floor to ceiling, “How does one of those form?” She asked, pointing toward it.

Vi’s eyes lingered on Caitlyn for a second before following the gesture, clearing her throat, “When a stalactite and a stalagmite finally meet,” she answered.

The older woman tilted her head, “So they’ve been growing toward each other for thousands of years?”

“Yep,” Vi’s lips popped to emphasize the ‘P’ and her smile turned mischievous, “You could say it’s a very long-distance relationship.”

Caitlyn laughed so hard that the stalactites seemed to vibrate above them.

As they continued deeper into the caverns, the conversation never seemed to falter.  Vi explained geology, cave ecology, groundwater systems, and local history and how the culture intertwined with the existence of the caverns.  Caitlyn continued to ask thoughtful questions that made Vi actually push the boundaries of her own knowledge, until eventually the tour became less a tour and more like two curious minds exploring something together.

And neither of them seemed eager for the day to end.

By the time they drove back from the caverns, the rain was hammering heavily against the windshield, as if it were trying to break through the glass to reach them.  Vi’s attention remained steeled on the road ahead, while Caitlyn’s gaze drifted off somewhere toward the tree line in the distance.

The sun had already slipped beneath the horizon, the world existing in that strange in-between hour where everything seemed suspended between day and night.  Deep blues and muted violets blanketed everything around them, while the headlights cut through the sheets of falling rain in slices of orange.

When Vi eventually pulled into the driveway of Jayce’s cabin and shifted the Subaru into park, the rain didn’t slow. 

“Gods, the sky has been so angry these past couple of days,” Caitlyn commented, her nose scrunched in dismay.  A slender hand lingered on the door handle, the older woman hesitant to step out into the downpour, lest she get absolutely soaked.

Vi shifted slightly, unclicking her seatbelt so that it was no longer sitting uncomfortably across her collarbone, “Well, I don’t have anywhere I need to be, so…we could just, sit here? Until the rain clears?”

Caitlyn turned, looking at Vi with a surprised expression, “Oh? Are you sure I wouldn’t be overstaying my welcome?”

Vi shook her head, “Not at all, actually. This has been…fun.” She admitted, rubbing the back of her neck with slight embarrassment.

The older woman relaxed in her seat, turning her shoulders slightly so that she was facing Vi more directly, “Hmm, yes. I admit, I was quite impressed with your knowledge of the caverns.”

“What? Did you think I wasn’t smart?” Vi teased.

Caitlyn’s eyebrows raised slightly, “Well I—”

The younger woman cut her off, letting out a soft, breathless chuckle that eased the tension in Caitlyn’s shoulders, “Everyone always thinks I’m a dumb jock when they meet me.”

“I didn’t think you were dumb,” Caitlyn countered quickly, reassuring Vi, “You just don’t come off as the type to care about geology.” She laughed.

Vi hummed at that, “Geology plays a large role in what I do, if you think about it.  River erosion, sediment deposition, all that stuff.  It’s a central element in how I read the water or classify different sections of rapids.”

“I…hadn’t thought about it like that.” Caitlyn admitted, “Sorry, Vi, I didn’t mean to offend.” She whispered, cerulean eyes slightly downcast as she placed a hand on Vi’s shoulder.

The younger woman shook her head, “Don’t be. I promise you didn’t hurt my feelings.”

Caitlyn let Vi’s reassurance wash over her, pulling her lower lip between her teeth for a moment before asking, “I think you’re brave for what you do.”

“What? Whitewater rafting?” Vi asked with a tilt of her head.

Outside, the wind howled briefly, making its presence known as it slammed the rain against the side of the car.

The older woman nodded, brushing a strand of blue behind her ear, “Yes, I mean, isn’t it incredibly dangerous? And Jayce mentioned you’re also certified for rescue operations?”

Vi shrugged nonchalantly, playing off the compliment, “Oh, yeah, but I mean, I’ve gone through rigorous training, so it doesn’t seem as scary for me. But it can be dangerous guiding first-timers. That’s why I focus so heavily on instructing them on commands before we even get in the water.”

When Caitlyn didn’t respond, Vi figured she’d try her hand at asking the question that’d been on her mind since they met a few days ago, “Why are you afraid of the water?”

The older woman hadn’t been expecting the question.  For a moment, she simply watched raindrops race each other down the windshield, before turning back to Vi.

Caitlyn drew in a slow breath, “I almost drowned when I was seven.”

Vi’s expression softened but she kept quiet, not wanting to interrupt what was clearly something vulnerable that Caitlyn had decided to share with her.

The older woman stared through the windshield at the warm glow of the cabin lights as she spoke, her voice sounding more distant than it had all day, “There was a pond at my family’s estate in Cambridge. I used to spend hours outside with my Doberman, her name was Dottie.” A smile touched her lips as she remembered her favorite dog, “She was my best friend.”

Caitlyn sighed, “We were playing fetch one afternoon.  The weather was beautiful and I was wearing this awful pink dress covered in little flowers.”

Vi chuckled, which pulled a smile from the older woman, “My mother thought it was adorable.”

“I would’ve paid money to see that.” Vi teased.

Caitlyn found herself smiling before continuing, “We were playing fetch by the pond, which in hindsight probably wasn’t the best idea.” She murmured, “I threw the tennis ball a little too far to the left and watched it roll right into the water.”

She glanced over toward the younger woman, who was only watching her with the softest, kindest grey eyes she’d ever had the pleasure of seeing in person.  Caitlyn swallowed, clearing her throat, “I remember leaning over…just a little.”

Her fingers began to fidget within the sleeves of Vi’s sweatshirt that she has yet to take off, “The next thing I knew, I was underwater and I couldn’t tell which direction was up.”

Vi felt her chest ache imagining just how scared a young Caitlyn Kiramman must’ve been, “How did you make it out?” She asked before she could stop herself.

A small, fond smile returned to Caitlyn’s lips, “Dottie.”

“Dottie?” Vi’s eyes widened in surprise.
