Caitlyn nodded, “My brave girl. She jumped in after me. Grabbed the back of my dress and dragged me to shore.”

“Good dog,” Vi smiled, genuine relief flooding her body at the fact that the pup had been there.

The older woman laughed, the subtle tears that’d been gathering on her waterline falling quietly down her cheeks, “The best dog.” She replied, the affection in her voice making something warm bloom inside Vi.

“It probably sounds stupid,” Caitlyn muttered, “But I’ve been too afraid to get near the water ever since.”

Vi shook her head, “No, it doesn’t.” Her voice quiet but certain, “Everyone’s afraid of something.”

Something flickered across the younger woman’s expression, gone almost as quickly as it appeared, like a shadow passing in your periphery.  But Caitlyn caught it.

She hesitated, “Why are you afraid of thunderstorms?” The question hung in the air between them.

Vi inhaled slowly, then looked away, “I…” she shook her head, “I can’t.”

The admission sounded hollow to her own ears, and she felt ashamed of the fact that this amazing woman sitting next to her had just shared something so vulnerable with her, only for Vi to shut down when asked in return. “I’m sorry,” she mumbled, voice barely above a whisper.

“Vi.”

The younger woman lifted her gaze from the rain-streaked windshield.  The dashboard lights painted faint amber shadows across her features, catching the tension lingering in her jaw.  Outside, the wind whispered against the car like spirits back from the dead.

Caitlyn watched her for a moment before speaking, “We both have things we don’t want to talk about.”

The words were simple, but there was something immeasurably gentle in them.  There was no expectation or subtle attempt to pry beneath the walls Vi had spent years building around herself.  Instead, there was simple understanding.

Caitlyn wasn’t holding it against her; she was giving her grace.

“Yeah,” Vi murmured after a beat, voice rough.

The rain continued its steady percussion overhead while neither woman rushed to fill the silence that followed.  Instead, Vi found herself drifting further into thought about the older woman she’d spent the better part of the day with.  She thought about the sadness that still lingered behind Caitlyn’s eyes sometimes, surfacing only when she thought Vi hadn’t been paying attention.

Whatever brought her here, it had been enough to make her abandon her entire life.  People didn’t leave entire careers or families because they were bored.  The question settled at the tip of Vi’s tongue.  What happened to you?

Eventually, Caitlyn glanced down at her phone.  The screen illuminated her face in soft blue light before she sighed quietly, “I should probably go. I have orientation tomorrow morning.” She explained, “I’d prefer not to fall asleep during my first official day as a climbing ranger.”

Vi huffed a tired laugh, “That’s probably wise. Wouldn’t want anyone falling from a cliff because you couldn’t keep your eyes open.”

Caitlyn’s laughter filled the space between them, tooth gap making an appearance.  She reached over, a slender hand resting on top of Vi’s without saying a word.  The contact was featherlight and warm, and suddenly Vi forgot how to breathe.

All she could feel was the warmth of Caitlyn’s hand resting over hers and the steady pulse suddenly thundering in her chest.

“Thank you for today,” Caitlyn said quietly, her accent thicker the more exhausted she was.

Vi’s grey eyes dropped to their hands before slowly lifting to meet Caitlyn’s gaze, “Anytime.”

The older woman smiled, small and real and somehow all the more devastating because of it, and withdrew her hand, “Goodnight, Violet.”

“Night, Miss Kiramman.” Vi muttered as the passenger door opened and cool, rain-scented air rushed inside.  She watched Caitlyn’s every step as the older woman hurried through the rain, the cabin lights catching in her midnight-blue hair.

She sat motionless in the driver’s seat, hands wrapped loosely around the steering wheel and her lips still slightly parted, the ghost of Caitlyn’s touch still lingering against her skin.  She cast one last glance toward the cabin, then shifted the car into gear and drove back down the driveway, the memory of that brief touch sticking with her all the way home.

 

Notes:

Does the yearner even yearn?!

Forreal, I feel like the tension between them is building like crazy after this one. Where will this go? Stay tuned! Lots of excitement, angst, yearning, romance, etc., to come!

As always, please let me know what you think in the comments below, and hit that kudos button if you're enjoying this story so far. Chat me up on twitter @Laur2d2_ if you want to engage with my fic writing process or get chapter sneak peeks. I post on there regularly!

Chapter 5: If I Keep Running, Will You Follow Me?

Summary:

It's orientation day at Shenandoah National Park, and Caitlyn's first day as Climbing Ranger. Will her first day go smoothly, or will it all come crumbling down?

Vi spends the day leading a hiking tour up Hawksbill Mountain.

Notes:

Woo, this chapter is a doozy. After asking my twitter followers, everyone said they'd rather I keep this chapter long than split it into two, so here you go: 12k words of pure angst and yearning!

Thank you for all of your support and love on this fic so far, it's really been such a joy to write these first few chapters and I really feel like we are heading in such a great direction! This story has been thoroughly outlined and researched from day one, so it's been exciting to continue to expand on the themes and ideas and settings I imagined in my head when I first started writing.

CW: Maddie jumpscare, descriptions of blood and injury, discussions of mental health.

(See the end of the chapter for more notes.)

Chapter Text

The first day jitters were nothing new to Caitlyn.  The anxiety had gotten worse after the Kirammans moved to the U.S. from England.  Not only was it a completely new environment for her, but it had also been a wildly different schooling system to get used to.  Her first day in an American high school was nothing like the movies—she’d walked in that day to a crowd of students walking down the hallway with barely an inch of space between them.

And no one had talked to her.  Or seemed to notice her.  At all.

Ever since then, whenever she entered the courtroom for the first day of a trial, or starting a new job, like she was now, she couldn’t help feeling like her veins were made of live wires.

Caitlyn and Jayce carpooled to the ranger station that morning, her best friend bouncing with an energy she didn’t have.  He went on and on talking about his deputy ranger, Steb, or the Supervisory Wildland Firefighter, Loris, and how essential they were to his day-to-day operations, and that Caitlyn would love them.

She had no doubt, but she also wished she could’ve had the car ride to sit in silence and gather her nerves.

Now, she stood awkwardly under the bright morning sun, its rays unobstructed in the cloudless blue sky.  The Blue Ridge Mountains surrounded her, not as tall as the Rockies, but certainly older.  She could almost feel the history here.

Beside her was a group of no more than fifteen people, all brand new NPS employees of different departments, including wildland firefighters, archaeologists, and junior rangers.  There was only one other person in the group that was joining as a climbing ranger.  A shorter woman with choppy orange hair and a face speckled with freckles.

The younger woman, Maddie, reminded her a little bit of Vi, but a whole lot more annoying.  As soon as she and Caitlyn were introduced at the beginning of orientation, she’d practically latched herself to Caitlyn’s hip.

She tried to ignore her, focusing on what Jayce was saying at the front of the group, his hands moving erratically to emphasize whatever point he was making.

“Once you’ve all received your uniforms, please make sure to check in with Steb to grab your radios.  You’ll be assigned two, one to keep on hand and one for a back-up.  And I cannot reiterate it enough, always have your radios on-hand. They are the difference between life and death out here.”

Caitlyn swallowed thickly, understanding then just how serious this job was.  Not that she hadn’t really known it before, but now it was sinking in.  She was going to oversee visitor safety on the climbing routes, and that was a pressure she would take on with a sense of pride and duty. 

“Have you climbed Everest yet?” Maddie asked from the side of her mouth, low enough that those around them couldn’t hear her talking.

Caitlyn sighed internally, not wanting to offend the young woman on her first day of the job, “No, but do you mind if I answer your questions later? I’d like to focus on what the Chief Ranger is saying.” She replied as politely as possible.

“Oh, of course!” Maddie said cheerfully, probably because Caitlyn had implied that they’d talk later.

Great.

Her mind drifted to Vi and how, despite being younger than Caitlyn was, she didn’t completely annoy her.  To be completely honest, she didn’t annoy her at all.  Even her constant teasing or inability to call Caitlyn by her name, she found the pink-haired woman endearing.  Why was it so different with her?

Caitlyn had even awoken earlier that morning to a text from Vi, which said: “Good luck at orientation!”

She couldn’t ignore the warmth that’d filled her chest reading that first thing, and that scared her shitless.  Caitlyn hadn’t been able to respond yet, unable to find the words for just how grateful she’d been.

“Cait?” Jayce’s voice broke through her wayward thoughts.  She blinked, and suddenly her best friend was standing in front of her, his face a mixture of concern and amusement.

“Hmm?” She hummed, still trying to orient herself back in the present.

Jayce chuckled, “Everyone is in line to get their uniforms. Are you okay?”

When she looked past his shoulder, she could see everyone standing in front of a folding table, a bunch of khaki ranger uniforms stacked across it in piles, organized by size.  Maddie was last in line, looking over at Caitlyn with that too-chipper smile.  When their eyes met, the younger woman waved her over.

Caitlyn sighed, “Yes, I’m fine.”

“You sure? It looked like you were dissociating,” Jayce huffed a laugh, patting her on the back as they walked toward the rest of the group.

She smirked, “Well, you were droning on for quite a while,” Caitlyn giggled, teasing on purpose to turn his attention from her. 

“Oh, please, you were probably just daydreaming about your date.” Jayce retorted, quirking a brow as he crossed his arms.

They stood in line together, despite the Chief Ranger not needing a new uniform.

Caitlyn rolled her eyes, “When are you going to lay off about that? I told you it wasn’t a date.”

She must’ve said it too loud, because the short, orange-haired woman turned around with a wide grin, interjecting herself into the conversation, “Who’d you go on a date with?!” she exclaimed excitedly.

Jayce turned to her, smile gleaming in the morning sunlight, “She went to the caverns yesterday with—Oof!”

Caitlyn elbowed Jayce in the ribs before he could say Vi’s name, “It wasn’t a date, Maddie.”

The younger woman just laughed, completely oblivious to Caitlyn’s scowl.

“You gotta lighten up, Sprout.” Jayce commented, nudging her arm as the line moved again. 

She huffed, shaking her head, “I just want to focus on the job today, okay? Is that too much to ask?”

Before her best friend could respond, it was Caitlyn’s turn to pick out her uniform.  Deputy Ranger Steb, a tall, slender man with a slicked back haircut and a quiet demeanor, greeted her with a soft hello.  She wondered how on earth he ever got a word in working with Jayce.

While Caitlyn would be required to wear the same standard ranger uniform—army green trousers, a khaki button-down, and a campaign hat—there were additional gear requirements that she needed to carry with her for her specialized work.  So, after retrieving her uniform, Jayce led her and Maddie inside the Ranger Station while Steb continued the orientation for the rest of the group.

Inside was a small bullpen, a few small desks scattered in haphazard rows with stacks of papers and miscellaneous supplies.  Safety posters and old certificates hung in frames on the walls.  The room smelled of sweat and cedar, a stench that Caitlyn didn’t particularly want to linger in.

“You’ll both need to inspect your gear daily, so get in the habit of logging those checks in this binder,” Jayce instructed, lifting a binder from its slot on the wall, which was clearly labeled, ‘Daily Equipment Check Log’.

“Will we be able to take our gear home with us at all?” Maddie asked with a tilt of her head.

Jayce shook his head, “No, everything must be turned in at the end of your shift so that we can ensure safekeeping. That’s why this log is so important in the mornings.”

“But what if we want to go climbing on our off-hours?” she asked with a frown.

Caitlyn caught the look of annoyance that crossed Jayce’s features, though she knew the other woman wouldn’t catch on, “You’ll need to use your own personal gear for recreational purposes. Any unsanctioned use of NPS climbing gear will result in suspension.”

When it was clear that Maddie had no further interruptions, Jayce continued, “Now, I know it’ll get hot this summer climbing on the cliff faces that face the sun, but it’s imperative you wear your uniform trousers. They’ll protect your legs from abrasions as you climb.” He explained, grabbing a bunch of gear from the closet.

Once he was sure he’d collected everything, the Chief Ranger spread out the gear on an empty desk.

“Harnesses, climbing rope, extra cams, climbing helmets, chalk bags, quickdraws…” he rambled, double-checking the items against his list.

Caitlyn cleared her throat, getting his attention.  When their eyes met, she reminded him, “Climbing shoes?”

“Oh! Right!” Jayce chuckled, striding across the room in quick beats and picking up a couple of shoe boxes from a shelf by his desk, “I was able to find your sizes at the local outfitter’s outlet, try these out today and let me know if they hurt at all. I can exchange them.”

Maddie squealed with excitement as he handed her the box, looking up at him expectantly until he realized exactly what she was thinking.

“These you are welcome to take home with you,” he clarified, side-eyeing Caitlyn with an exasperated look.

She couldn’t help but snicker at that, reaching a hand up to cover her mouth so that the younger woman didn’t hear her by mistake.

Jayce left the two women alone to complete their equipment checks, opting to go check in with the rest of the group while they did so.  Caitlyn was slightly annoyed at having been left alone to deal with Maddie, but she realized they’d be spending a lot of time together, so she should probably try to make an effort to get along with the younger woman.

She cleared her throat as she inspected her climbing rope for signs of fraying, “So, Maddie. What experience do you have with free climbing?”

Maddie smiled, pleased to finally have a real conversation with Caitlyn, “Oh, well actually I’ve only been top-roping and always had a belayer, but I’ve been practicing sport climbing lately at the climbing gym back home.”

Caitlyn’s hands stilled midway through inspecting her rope, “So you haven’t gone trad climbing at all?” She inquired, eyeing the younger woman with a look that said she wasn’t impressed.  Though Maddie didn’t notice.

The shorter woman shrugged, “No, not really. Just some outdoor bouldering with the landing pads. Jayce said you’ve climbed all over, right?”

Momentarily stunned, she gained her bearings, clearing her throat, “Y-yes. I’ve done some sport climbs at Denali and El Capitan.” Among others, but she didn’t exactly feel like going through her entire resume considering she was much more qualified than Maddie was.  They could’ve been there for an hour and she wanted to get to work sooner rather than later.

“Woah! So, you’re like, totally professional!”

Caitlyn stopped herself from rolling her eyes, “It’s more of a hobby than anything, but yes, I have a lot of experience with outdoor climbing.” She replied politely.

Maddie beamed and both women finished inspecting their ropes in silence, for which the older woman was eternally grateful.

It wasn’t until they were packing their climbing bags with all their equipment that Jayce returned, “Alright, how are things looking in here?”

“Ready for my assignment, Sir!” Maddie announced, huffing her bag over her shoulders like she was going to her first day of school. 

Caitlyn grimaced, internally trying to figure out why Jayce hired this woman, “Yeah, me too.”

“Great! I have you both assigned to a few sport routes over at Reflector Oven, East of Old Rag Mountain. It’s a popular area for climbers and we have a lot of routes to maintain. Here’s the list of your routes and their coordinates.” Jayce grinned, handing Caitlyn a piece of paper he’d clearly just typed up that morning.

She turned to Maddie, saying a curt “Excuse us,” before grabbing Jayce’s arm and pulling him in the opposite direction, not looking back at the young woman they’d left standing in the middle of the bullpen.  Caitlyn found an empty office, pulled Jayce in, and closed the door.

“What on Earth are you thinking?!” She scowled, crossing her arms in that familiar, bossy way he always found partially endearing and partially terrifying.

Jayce sighed, “Listen, Cait, please just go along with this. I’m already in a tough enough spot with her as it is.”

Her blue eyes narrowed, “What do you mean?”

“She’s the senator’s daughter. I didn’t really have a choice in hiring her.” He admitted, rubbing the back of his neck in silent frustration.

“Fuck, Jayce.” Caitlyn muttered, “I didn’t sign up to be a babysitter. What if she gets hurt? I don’t want to be responsible for that!”

“You’re the best climber I know,” He pleaded, “and the safest. It’s better if she learns from you than someone else!”

She pressed her fingertips against her temple and exhaled sharply, wanting to argue.  When she accepted Jayce’s offer to come to Shenandoah, she’d imagined long days spent alone on the cliffs, just her, the rock, and the wind.  No responsibility beyond keeping herself alive and doing the job well.

Now, somehow, she’d ended up right back where she started—with everyone seeming to need something from her.

Caitlyn’s gaze drifted toward the window, where sunlight spilled across the ranger station parking lot.  She could almost picture herself on some quiet route high above the valley, alone with the stone beneath her fingertips, the way it dug into her skin and reminded her that she was alive.

Then, her thoughts wandered inevitably toward Vi.  The younger woman spent every day taking complete strangers out onto the river.  She could’ve kept those experiences to herself, but because she loved the water so much, Vi had decided to share it.  To teach others.

Caitlyn remembered the way Vi’s entire face had lit up while explaining groundwater erosion in the caverns.  The enthusiasm in her voice when she talked about reading currents and guiding rafts through whitewater.  The obvious pride she took in helping people experience a world she cared deeply about.

Maybe that was part of loving something, not just doing it, but sharing it.  That fact irritated her far more than it should have.

“Gods,” Caitlyn groaned, rubbing a hand down her face, “I hate that you’re making sense.”

Jayce grinned widely, “You know I’m right.”

“Don’t push your luck,” she glanced toward the bullpen, where Maddie was enthusiastically sorting gear and nearly vibrating with excitement.  The woman would drive her absolutely insane, without question.  But maybe that wasn’t the worst thing in the world.

Caitlyn released a long, suffering sigh, “Fine.”

“Fine?” He repeated, a tinge of teasing to his tone.

“I’ll do it,” she pointed a warning finger at him, “but if she throws herself off a cliff because she watched one too many climbing videos on the internet, that’s on you.”

Jayce barked out a laugh, “Deal.”

Caitlyn shook her head and headed for the door, “Congratulations, Chief Ranger,” she muttered, “you’ve successfully tricked me into becoming a mentor.” Then she pulled the office door open and strode back into the bullpen.

“Come on, Maddie,” she called, “Let’s go climb something.”

While the weather was clear, with the sun shining brightly against an azure, blue sky, the water levels in the Shenandoah River had not yet receded.  Therefore, once again, Vi had to cancel her whitewater rafting tours for the day.

It left her completely frustrated and buzzing with untapped energy.  All she wanted to do was be on the water.  Now, however, she was sitting at her desk in one of the back offices at Lanes Family Adventures, her phone pressed to her ear as she tried, again, to call Vander.

“Come on,” she mumbled to herself, “Pick up, pick up, pick up.”

A knock thudded against the doorframe, and when she looked up, Powder was standing there with pinched brows, “Still nothing?”

Vi shook her head, “He’s not answering.”

Powder sighed and perched on the edge of her sister’s desk, “I know he does this sometimes, but I have a bad feeling.”

“Try not to worry.” Vi replied, putting on the mask of the unmovable big sister, trying to ease Powder’s concerns.

It was a front, and they both knew it.

Vander had always been difficult to pin down.  Half the time he forgot where he’d left his phone, and the other half he was knee-deep in a project, usually in the workshop at the house, but he always called back eventually.

Something about this business trip felt different.  Vi couldn’t point to any specific thing and say that’s why.  There had been something in his voice when he’d told her he was leaving the week before, a strange hesitation before answering even the simplest questions about his trip.  Maybe she was reading too much into it, maybe she was just annoyed he hadn’t called.

Still, every time she reached his voicemail, a quiet uneasiness settled a little deeper between her ribs.

“Did you talk to Benzo?” Vi asked. 

Powder had been spending more time with Ekko at Benzo’s Autobody shop than anywhere else lately.  She claimed it was simply a desire to hang out with her best friend as much as possible before she left for grad school, but Vi knew there was more to it.  She just didn’t push her little sister on what that was.  At least, not yet.

“He hasn’t heard from him either,” Powder answered, chewing her inner cheek. “But he did say Vander told him he’d be going ‘off grid’ for a bit, so…”

Well, that was…odd.

“Off grid?” Vi’s brows furrowed.  She tapped her pen against the surface of the desk, then shrugged, “New York City isn’t exactly ‘Off Grid’ though. It’s like, the most connected city on the planet.”

Powder shrugged, “Just what he said.”

Vi hummed at that, tossing her pen down and pushing up from her chair, “I’ve got guests arriving soon for the hike up Hawksbill. Are you going to be okay here by yourself?”

“Why wouldn’t I be?” Powder retorted with that same old bratty attitude that Vi always said she hated but would never admit she’d miss whenever her sister left for grad school.

All she could do was laugh, “I’ll take that as a yes.”

Powder followed Vi down the hallway and through the Staff Only door back to the main lobby, “Actually, Ekko is going to come help me fix the motor on the bass boat. Said he managed to find one of the parts we needed on eBay.”

Outside, cars began rolling into the gravel parking lot and forming half-crooked aisles.

Vi chuckled, “Okay, just don’t get grease all over yourselves again. You looked like some kind of oil monsters the last time you ‘fixed’ the boat.”

Powder crossed her arms and stuck her tongue out before shooting her sister a lopsided grin and turning tail to head out the back door toward the back lot, where the bass boat was tarped and hitched on the trailer.

The lobby of Lanes Family Adventures was beginning to fill by the time Vi returned from the back offices with a clipboard tucked beneath one arm and a coffee mug balanced in her other hand.  This was the best kind of morning: The kind where she got to meet like-minded people who enjoyed the fresh air as much as she did.

Morning sunlight streamed through the large front windows, warm rectangles blanketing the industrial floor.  Outside, the Blue Ridge Mountains sat hazy in the short distance, beneath a blue sky that hadn’t shown itself in a few days.  Inside, six guests had gathered near the check-in counter, shifting awkwardly in that way strangers always did when thrown together before one of her excursions.

Vi took one look at the group and grinned wildly.  Show time.

“Well, this is either gonna be the best hike of the season or the opening scene of a true-crime podcast.” She announced, confident, charming, and comfortable in front of an audience.

Six pairs of eyes turned toward her.  The younger brunette nearest the counter barked out a laugh, “Good morning to you too,” she replied.

Vi pointed at her with the tip of her pencil, “See? That’s exactly the kind of enthusiasm I like to hear.”

The woman laughed again and stepped forward, “I’m Addie.”

“Addie. Excellent. You look like you’d survive a horror movie.” Vi noted.

The man beside her snorted, his sandy hair peeking through his backwards ballcap, “What does that say about me?” He asked.

Vi looked him over dramatically, “You?” she winced, “You’d investigate the weird noise in the basement.”

The group erupted into a chorus of laughter while the man, Chris, placed a hand over his chest, “That’s incredibly accurate, actually.”

Vi grinned again, letting the warmth and ease of the conversation wash over her, rinsing her mind of earlier concerns over Vander’s whereabouts.  She needed this. “Yeah, I know my people.” She replied. 

She could feel the awkward tension between strangers loosen.  Beside Addie and Chris stood another couple, one older woman with shorter red hair, looking effortlessly styled and poised.  The woman crossed her arms with amusement, “I’m Genevieve.” She introduced herself as Vi’s gaze shifted toward her.

Vi nodded toward the younger, shorter brunette woman standing beside her, long hair tied back in a braid, “And?”

“Kleya,” the brunette replied with a shy smile, almost melting into Genevieve when the older woman’s arm came around her shoulders protectively.

The movement sparked curiosity somewhere in the back of Vi’s mind.  She wondered how much older Genevieve was than her girlfriend, and how they met and fell in love.  The sudden impulse to ask them a whole bunch of questions was so strong that Vi had to suppress the urge.

“Fantastic,” Vi swallowed, continuing with her introductory bit, “Which one of you readds all the trail signs?”

A grin grew on Kleya’s face as she immediately pointed at Genevieve, while the other woman rolled her eyes. “I’m an archaeologist.” She explained.

Vi tucked her pencil behind her ear and clapped enthusiastically, “See!”

More laughter rippled through the room.  The final couple stood slightly apart from the others, comfortably settled like long-married people tended to be.  Patrick was tall, greying slightly at his temples, wearing glasses and a bucket hat intended to shield his face from the sun.  He was buzzing with energy while his partner, Sam, was slightly shorter, though only by an inch or so.  His sunglasses were perched atop his head, and his hiking clothes looked so pristine that it was if he’d steamed them before dressing.

“And you two?” Vi gestured toward them, feeling a sense of admiration in her bones at seeing a couple their age still active enough to join a hiking group.

“Patrick and Sam.” They greeted, short and sweet.

Vi nodded thoughtfully, “You both have the vibe of people who brought snacks.”

Patrick immediately pulled a granola bar from his pocket, a wicked grin on his face.  The rest of the group burst out laughing.

“No way,” Addie said, cheeks burning.

Vi fist-pumped the air triumphantly, “I’ve been doing this too long.”  She was satisfied now that everyone looked considerably less tense, taking that as her cue to set down her coffee. 

“Alright, everybody. Before we head out and go hiking up a mountain, let’s do the boring part so nobody dies.”

Chris raised a hand, “That’s not reassuring.” He teased.

“It wasn’t supposed to be.” Vi replied seriously, though she was only able to hold her neutral expression for a brief second before the smirk forced its way through.  The group laughed in response, and she stepped behind the counter, setting her clipboard on the surface.

“Hawksbill Mountain is one of the most beautiful hikes in Shenandoah National Park. It’s also one of the most popular. That means we’re going to share the trail with other hikers, photographers, families, and approximately seventeen people who somehow forgot they were hiking and wore flip-flops.”

Genevieve sighed audibly, “Every trail has at least one.” She replied, rolling her eyes.

Vi hummed, “Exactly.” She took a second to glance around the room to ensure everyone was listening.

“First thing. Stay on the designated trails.  NPS spends a lot of time protecting vegetation and preventing erosion. If a trail curves one direction and you think you’ve found a shortcut, you haven’t. Absolutely no cutting the switchbacks.”

She held up a green and orange Owala bottle, “Second. Water. Even though this isn’t an all-day hike, dehydration sneaks up on people fast, especially in the summer. Drink before you’re thirsty.”

Sam raised his own Owala, blue and grey, with a sheepish grin, “Already ahead of you.”

Vi smirked, “Third. Watch your footing. The trail has sections with exposed roots, loose rocks, and uneven ground. If you’re at the front of the group and you identify a tripping hazard, call it out.”

She leaned against the counter, taking on a more serious tone, “Fourth. Wildlife.”

The room quieted, obviously recognizing the importance of this point, “If we see deer, that’s great. If we see a black bear, that’s also great. That said, if we happen to see one, stay calm, stay together, don’t run, and definitely don’t’ try to get closer for a selfie.” She added with a hint of annoyance.

Vi pointed a finger around the room, “I mean it. If I catch anyone trying to become an influencer today, I’m leaving you for the squirrels.” 

That got the biggest laugh of the morning.  Proud of herself, Vi hooked her thumbs into the pockets of her jeans, smiling widely as her gaze raked over the group, “This hike is supposed to be fun. Ask questions, take photos, enjoy yourselves. You’re going to be hiking one of the oldest mountain ranges on Earth.” Her smile softened slightly, “And trust me, the view from the summit is worth every step.”

The hike into Reflector Oven led them through a stretch of hardwood forest so dense that it seemed to swallow the outside world whole.  Sunlight filtered through the canopy in scattered flecks of gold, illuminating patches of moss that covered ancient granite boulders in blankets of viridian.

The forest floor smelled of damp earth and last autumn’s leaves, rich with decay and new growth alike.  Somewhere deeper in the trees, a wood thrush sang its haunting, flute-like melody, the notes flitting between the trees until it was carried away with the breeze.

Caitlyn found herself slowing more than once during the approach, much to Maddie’s apparent confusion. 

There was simply too much to look at.  New York had taught her to move through the world without really seeing it, rushing from subway platforms to the office, from conference rooms to courtrooms, keeping her attention fixed on deadlines and obligations.  Entire seasons had passed without her truly noticing. 

But Shenandoah would not allow that kind of blindness.  Everything here demanded to be noticed, whether it was the mountain ridge extending across the horizon like a giant wall or a birdcall reverberating through the valley. 

Even now, standing at the base of Reflector Oven with a harness hanging from her hips and a coil of climbing rope draped over one shoulder, Caitlyn felt more present than she had in years.

It was a large, sunlit, 100ft tall granite crag that stretched the east-facing side of Old Rag, with several popular trad and sport climbing routes ranging from 5.9 to 5.13 in difficulty.  While there were over twelve routes on this section of the mountain, Jayce had assigned three to Caitlyn and Maddie: The Parker Route, Loki, and Strawberry Fields, which was the most popular route.

The morning chill had begun giving way to the warmth of early summer, the granite of The Parker Route rising above them through breaks in the trees, pale stone split by weathered cracks and interrupted here and there by the metal gleam of fixed bolts.

They were there to inspect the routes, specifically every hanger, bolt, and anchor, to ensure everything was sturdy enough to handle the expected load of a climber and their gear.  If something failed, it wouldn’t just be an inconvenience—people could get hurt.  That responsibility settled heavily across Caitlyn’s shoulders.

Once tied into the rope, however, the anxiety faded.  The moment Caitlyn placed her hands against the rock, the rest of the world seemed to fall away.  Climbing had always done that to her.

There was no room for overthinking six moves above the ground.  No space for spiraling thoughts about Charlie, the image of her Ex in bed with a man burned into corneas, or her mother, who was probably fuming with rage at not being able to reach her.  Or the legal career she’d abandoned, the consequences of which she couldn’t, or wouldn’t think about.

The wall demanded her complete attention.  One missed foothold or one sloppy move and she’d go tumbling down in a flailing heap of limbs.  She had one job, and that was to climb, inspect, climb, inspect.  That singularity of purpose felt oddly liberating.

Caitlyn moved upward in smooth, controlled motions, smearing one foot against the slab while her fingertips settled onto a shallow crimp.  A quickdraw clipped cleanly into the first bolt, and she slid her rope through it, tugging once to test its strength before moving on.  Falling into a rhythm that felt as natural as breathing.

Fifty feet below, Maddie maintained the belay and was doing a good job at it so far.  Much to Caitlyn’s pleasant surprise.  As much of a chatterbox the younger woman was, it seemed she knew when to focus.

The forest spread beneath her in a sea of green.  A hawk circled somewhere above the canopy, her eyes tracking its path for a moment before her attention flickered back to the rock, where she was reaching toward the next sequence.

Her left foot was secure against a small ledge, while her right foot rested confidently against the slightly angled slab.  The next move appeared relatively straightforward—a weathered handhold protruding just enough from the wall to support the transfer of her weight.

Just as she did before each move, Caitlyn drew in a long, steadying breath.  Her mind narrowed to the ledge, her body buzzing with an energy that was like she’d been holding one of those plasma ball toys. 

She pushed upward with her left foot, her right foot dragging across the granite as her fingers reached for the ledge and settled against it.

There was something only someone with a decade or more experience climbing outdoors could recognize—a subtle shift in the rock, a sensation that traveled through her fingertips before her conscious mind even recognized it.

Her pulse skipped as every instinct she possessed screamed at her to pull away.  Her muscles contracted tightly as she began transferring her weight back toward her other arm, but it was too late.

A sharp cracking noise echoed through the cliff face, the sound low at first, like the rumble of distant thunder.  Then it grew louder.  Tiny fissures spread across the granite like veins beneath skin, grains of stone broke loose and scattered into the air.  The entire section shifted several inches outward.

For one suspended heartbeat, Caitlyn found herself staring directly at the collapsing hold.

“Rock!”

The Hawksbill Loop trail was a moderate, 2.9-mile hiking trail that circuited up to the Hawksbill summit and back down the mountain.  Since the summit is the highest peak in Shenandoah National Park, it meant that it was also the most popular for park visitors and tourists. 

Which meant, even for a Wednesday, the trail was pretty crowded.  Not ideal, but Vi knew how to work with the crowd, rather than against it.  Besides, everyone was out there for the same reasons, right? 

It couldn’t have been a more beautiful day, either.  The sun was shining high in the sky, but the breeze cooled the air enough that it didn’t feel like they were cooking beneath it.

Vi led the group, setting a pace that was quick enough that they’d finish within the two-and-a-half hours allotted for the tour, while not going too fast that her group couldn’t keep up.  While the oldest members, Patrick and Sam, were rather athletic for their age, she still worried about either one of them twisting an ankle.

Meanwhile, Addie and Chris were right behind her, their strides long and steady.

“How often do you take tours up here?” Addie asked, her voice showing no sign of strain from the physical exertion.

Vi huffed, focusing on her breathing as she answered, “I don’t really know, once a week, maybe? It really depends on the rain and how many rafting tours I have to cancel in a season.”

“You raft too?” Chris replied, “No wonder you’re so buff.”

That made Vi laugh, “Someone’s gotta make up for your noodle arms.” She jeered, smirking at the man.

Addie joined in, “Oh, you should see him try to open a jar of pickles. It’s pathetic!”

“Hey!” Chris exclaimed, placing his palm against his chest in feigned offense, “Take that back.”

Somewhere behind them, Kleya whispered, “They argue like they’ve been married forty years.”

Genevieve chuckled, “I’m afraid that’s quite normal, darling.”

“We don’t argue,” Kleya pointed out, poking her partner’s arm.

The older woman scoffed, “Surely you’re joking. You were just arguing with me this morning about wearing those shoes.”

“That…was just a discussion.”

“No, it wasn’t.”

“Ladies!” Vi’s voice echoed over the group, cutting through the argument like scissors, “We are fifteen minutes from reaching the summit, let’s put some smiles on our faces and enjoy the moment!”

Kleya rolled her eyes and Genevieve couldn’t help but giggle, throwing an arm around her partner’s shoulder and pulling her in tighter, “She’s right, darling. Let’s not spoil the fun.”

Satisfied that her group would remain on their best behavior, Vi turned back around, leading them up switchback after switchback through dense trees and rocky outcroppings.

While everyone behind her continued to talk amongst themselves, boots crunching against the dirt path, Vi let herself drift back into the rhythm of the hike.  Despite the higher altitude, each breath came easier beneath the tree canopy.  The air smelled of pine needles and moss, the breeze stirring through the leaves overhead and carrying away the lingering anxieties that had followed her all morning.

Between worrying about Vander and dealing with the fallout of her and Sarah’s (conversation? Break up? She didn’t even know what to call it) situation, the uncertainty gnawing at her chest left her mind feeling hopelessly cluttered.

Out here, though, amongst the trees and the birds, nature seemed to scrub all that noise away.

Well, except for one remaining person who seemed to have made a home in her head—Caitlyn Kiramman.

Her lips twitched into a smirk before she could stop them.  At that exact moment, Caitlyn was at her orientation, and she found herself wondering what the older woman was doing.  Was she buried beneath a stack of paperwork?  Or was she getting issued one of those ridiculous ranger hats Jayce wore?  Maybe she was already halfway up a cliff face, the muscles of her long legs flexing as she climbed.

Vi wondered whether she’d made any friends yet.  Though, considering Caitlyn looked uncomfortable whenever attention lingered on her for more than ten seconds, Vi figured that was probably unlikely.  Then again, Caitlyn was also kind, funny, and somehow managed to make geology sound like the most interesting thing on earth simply because she cared enough to ask questions.

She felt a warmth spread in her chest as she thought of her.  Her gaze drifted toward the trail ahead, and she wondered if Caitlyn was thinking about her too.  Gods, she wished the woman was here.

“How much farther? I’ve got a cramp in my calf,” Chris’ voice shattered Vi’s thoughts, bringing her back to the present.

She chuckled, glancing over her shoulder at her group.  Vi grew truly fond of these folks—the connections she made doing this job always brought her so much joy and reminded her why she loved doing this in the first place.

“Look alive, everyone! The summit is just over this next switchback.” Vi announced, grinning widely as the six of them traversed the path, the trees beginning to thin out the closer they got to the peak of the mountain.

Granite crumbled beneath her hand, falling into a cascade of debris.  Chunks of stone sheared away from the wall and tumbled downward in a rockslide, fragments shattering against the slab and ricocheting to the ground below.

Suddenly deprived of one of her primary points of contact, Caitlyn’s weight swung violently away from the wall, her feet skating across smooth granite as the world dropped out from beneath her.  Gravity took her in its grasp, pulling her down with the broken stone raining around her like shrapnel.

“Caitlyn!” She vaguely registered Maddie’s voice calling to her as she fell.  It felt like everything had slowed, with time being just as suspended as she was.  All she could think of was how much it was going to hurt when she landed.

But then her rope came tight.  Her harness jerked upward, stopping the fall abruptly enough to wrench her shoulders.  The force of the catch was so sudden it swung her back toward the cliff face.  Her hip slammed into granite with bone-jarring force.  A split second later, her knee scraped harshly across rough stone as her body bounced against the slab.  White-hot pain flashed through her leg as she felt fabric tear and skin split open beneath it.

The impact momentarily drove every bit of air from her lungs; the entire world narrowed to a blur of pain and adrenaline.

Beneath her, Maddie hadn’t moved, her hands still clutching the rope in a white-knuckled grip.  In any other instance, Caitlyn would’ve been impressed and relieved.  But now, the younger woman stood directly beneath the route, frozen in shock as debris continued tumbling toward the base of the wall.

Somehow, she gathered herself enough to scream down to her, “Move!”

She didn’t think about what would happen if Maddie, her belayer, would let go.  Yes, she would fall again, from a height that was still much too high to survive from, but Caitlyn could hold on, try to catch a ledge and hang on.  Maddie, however, was surely about to get squashed by heavy rock.

The command seemingly broke Maddie out of it, because she stumbled backward just as a chunk of granite crashed into the ground where she’d been standing seconds earlier.  The sound echoed through Reflector Oven before gradually fading away to silence.

Even the birds seemed to have stopped singing.

Maddie, in the chaos, had stumbled over on her ass, the palms of her hands scraped up on the sharp rock beneath her.  Her chest heaved with shaky breaths as she took stock of her surroundings, her eyes landing on the granite that now rested where she’d stood.

Above, Caitlyn hung motionless, suspended between earth and sky while the last traces of adrenaline coursed through her veins.  Her fingers clenched around the rope so tightly that her knuckles had gone white.  She hadn’t yet opened her eyes, unable to work up the courage to do so.

Her knee burned with a raw, fiery intensity that told her there was almost certainly blood running down her shin beneath her uniform trousers.  So much for long pants protecting her from abrasions, she mused.

She forced herself to stay calm.  Years of mountaineering had taught her how to take stock of her injuries, even in a state of shock.  Her hip ached and her knee was torn up, but everything else felt fine.

When Caitlyn finally opened her eyes again, the wall remained exactly where it had been before the fall, vast and indifferent beneath the Virginia sun.  She swallowed against the lingering adrenaline, shifted her weight experimentally, and felt another sharp throb radiate through her hip.  While it was painful, it was manageable, and that was good enough for her.

But how was she still hanging there?

Caitlyn looked down, her cerulean eyes finding Maddie staring back up at her from her spot on the ground, tears falling down her cheeks.

She cleared her throat, “Are you alright?!” Caitlyn called, voice hoarse like it too had been scraped against the rough stone.

Maddie, still in shock, couldn’t speak.  She could only nod slightly, but the older woman took that as enough to know she was okay, at least physically.

“Maddie, how am I still suspended?” She found the courage to ask, unable to see where the rope ended below her.

The younger woman only lifted a finger, pointing in the direction of where the coil of rope that she’d been holding onto had been.  A large, shattered piece of granite was sitting right on top of the end of the rope, serving as a sort of anchor, holding Caitlyn in mid-air like her own guardian angel.

She was quite literally suspended in disbelief.

That’s when the wave of relief washed over her, the pure joy of still being alive flooding her bloodstream and mixing with the waning adrenaline to form a high that she originally thought could’ve only been achieved from drugs.

Caitlyn huffed a laugh, and then another, then another.  Until soon enough, she was cackling out loud like she was front row at a comedy show.

It was enough to draw concern from the younger woman, who finally found her voice and pushed herself back up to her feet, rushing over to the rope that was now caught beneath the rock.

“Uh, Caitlyn? Are you okay?” Maddie asked, brows pinched together as her hands came around the rope and pulled it tight.

Caitlyn, unable to stop laughing, just nodded vehemently, her cheeks bright red now.  She knew this was just the shock, the adrenaline would soon leave her bloodstream, and the searing pain would come, but it still seemed like the funniest thing in the world to her.

Maddie, seemingly understanding what was happening, reached into her pocket and pulled out her knife, making quick work to free the rope with one hand while the other stayed clenched around it.

The harness jerked slightly as the rope was freed, but the younger woman was quick to grab it, lowering Caitlyn slowly and cautiously toward the ground.

As soon as her feet touched the ground, Maddie noticed the blood that’d pooled around Caitlyn’s knee, “You’re bleeding!”

“It’s just a scratch, it doesn’t hurt,” Caitlyn lied, the laughter dying out on her tongue.  She tried to take a step, but her hip screamed in protest.  Wincing, she held onto Maddie’s arm for support, “We need to radio Jayce and have this route shut down while we inspect our other routes.”

Maddie huffed, “You’re not seriously considering climbing with a busted knee, are you?”

Caitlyn frowned, “Well, who else is going to do it? We have a job to do, Maddie. I told you, it doesn’t hurt.” She steeled herself, taking a couple of steps and gritting through the pain, though she was doing a much better job of masking her expression to hide the fact that it hurt more than she was letting on. “Come on, Loki is next.”

The final switchback that they ascended toward Hawksbill Summit was steeper than the rest of the trail, the path winding upward through scattered trees and jagged outcrops of stone.  Vi led the way, occasionally glancing over her shoulder to make sure everyone was still keeping pace.

“Good news,” she called back as they rounded the final bend, “you’re all about thirty seconds away from earning the right to complain about this hike!”

A chorus of tired groans rang out.

“Best incentive I’ve heard all day,” Chris wheezed sarcastically.

The summit emerged in massive slabs of weathered quartzite, sun-bleached and warm beneath the afternoon light.  Beyond the rocky overlook, the Shenandoah Valley unfolded in every direction, an endless sea of rolling green ridges fading into layers of hazy blue mountains on the horizon.

Patches of farmland stitched the valley floor together like a quilt, while winding roads and the Shenandoah River glimmered beneath the early-summer sun. 

For what seemed like hours but was probably only a minute, the six hikers simply stood staring, mouths agape.  This was always Vi’s favorite part of the hiking tours—watching everyone’s reactions to seeing a view that most people took for granted.

“Woah,” Sam breathed.

Patrick slowly lowered his camera, “Okay. Pictures officially do not do this justice.”

Addie walked forward until she reached the edge of the overlook, “Oh my god.”

Chris stepped up beside her, equally speechless for nearly three whole seconds before recovering, “I can see Russia from my house.”

The joke made Addie roll her eyes, though she couldn’t suppress the amused grin that grew despite her annoyance, “Seriously, babe?”

He chuckled, “What? I climbed a mountain for that joke!”

Vi snorted, shaking her head at their easy banter.

Genevieve drifted toward the western edge of the overlook with Kleya beside her.  Neither woman spoke much.  They simply stood shoulder-to-shoulder, gazing out across the valley.

As Vi watched from a respectful distance, Genevieve reached for Kleya’s hand.  The gesture was small and unassuming, the younger woman’s fingers intertwining with her partner’s with a certain familiarity.

Something about it made Vi smile.  The mountains had a way of stripping life down to its essentials.  No expectations, just a handful of people standing above the world with the ones who mattered most.

Her chest ached slightly, because Vi had made the conscious choice that she’d never commit to a serious relationship, because she knew she was afraid of being abandoned.  But at the same time, the loneliness of that choice made her feel that way anyways.  She couldn’t help but wish that she had someone to share this moment with.

When she looked back toward the group, Patrick was already digging through his backpack. “Trail snack time!” He announced, his husband laughing as he produced an entire box of granola bars with the triumphant expression of a magician revealing his greatest trick.

A collective cheer rose from the group. 

“Do summit calories count?” Chris asked as he studied the wrapper thoughtfully.

Addie shook her head, “No.”

“Good,” he mumbled around a bite, the bar already almost halfway gone.

Vi laughed and wandered among the group, answering questions and pointing out visible landmarks.  She even insisted on taking each couple’s photo whenever they needed a picture.

It wasn’t until thirty minutes later that she checked the time and sighed dramatically, “Alright, everyone.” She called out, pointing toward the trail behind them. “We should start heading back if we want to make it back to the bus before sunset.”

And once again, a collective groan echoed across the summit.  Because, as beautiful of a view it was, the hike down, unfortunately, still existed.

The sun was setting by the time Caitlyn and Maddie returned to the ranger station.  The pink and golden light blanketed the gravel lot and turned the surrounding mountains into deep indigo silhouettes against the colorful sky.

Most of the vehicles were gone already, orientation clearly ended hours ago for everyone else.  Only Jayce’s Rav4 and Maddie’s bike remained parked there. 

Jayce sat on the station steps waiting for them, his ranger hat balanced on one knee.  Even from a distance, Caitlyn recognized the look on his face—concern.  Everyone was concerned these days.

She parked the camry beside his car and shut off the engine, the sudden silence feeling like she’d stepped into deep water, cold and isolating.  A few hours ago, she’d been hanging sixty feet above the ground wondering if she was about to die, and now she was somehow expected to return to normal.  She didn’t have the energy for any of it.

“I’ll take the equipment and do the report,” Maddie offered, voice noticeably quieter than it had been that morning.

Caitlyn turned toward her and managed a tired smile, “Thank you. I’ll see you tomorrow.”

Maddie nodded, lingering just a second to assess if the older woman was truly okay before opening the passenger door and circling to the trunk, hauling out their equipment and lugging it across the lot and toward the station.

The moment she vanished from sight, Caitlyn let her head fall back against the seat.  Everything hurt—the adrenaline had finally worn off somewhere during the drive back from Reflector Oven.  The throbbing ache in her hip seemed to pulse in time with her heartbeat.

Okay, so maybe continuing to climb after the fall had been reckless, but she’d been determined to finish the job.  After she’d spent the last week feeling like her entire life had become one long series of bad decisions, climbing had been the one thing that gave her something straightforward to focus on.

A knock sounded softly against the door, “Sprout?”

She suppressed a groan.  For one selfish moment, Caitlyn wished he would’ve gone home without her.  She wished everyone would stop worrying about her long enough for her to figure out how she felt. 

But that was the problem.  She didn’t know how she felt, because her brain seemed incapable of settling on one single feeling.  Every thought pulled her in a different direction; one moment she felt liberated and the next she felt guilty.  Then angry, then relieved, then terrified.  Like some invisible hand kept reaching inside her mind and spinning a compass that refused to point north.

She opened her eyes, finding Jayce standing beside the door with furrowed brows and a growing frown.

Caitlyn sighed and pushed the door open slightly, “Great first day.”

He took one look at her and shook his head, “You almost died, Cait.”

“Almost being the operative word,” she retorted, shifting her head slightly to look at him, “I’m fine.”

Jayce hesitated, evaluating his next move like he was afraid of how she might react, “You’re aware I’m going to have to fill out a report, right?”

She nodded, “Maddie is filling one out as we speak.”

“Good. That’s good…” a pause, then that subtle twitch of his lips, like he was trying to decide what to say next.  And Caitlyn knew then that he was about to push the conversation somewhere she didn’t want it to go.

“Listen, Sprout. I know you’ve got a lot…going on. And you have every reason to be upset, but it’s just…well, I’ve noticed a change in you, and I guess I’m just concerned.”

Ah, there it was again.  Concern.  It sparked a familiar defensive instinct within her as her stomach tightened.  He hadn’t done anything wrong, not really.  But she was afraid that if she let him in, she might have to look honestly at all the things she’d been running from.

“What do you mean?” she asked quietly.

“Cait, you ran away. You haven’t called your mother. Today you had a complete disregard for your own wellbeing and safety after experiencing a fall that could’ve been catastrophic. Don’t get me started on the state of your room…”

They weren’t accusations, realistically she knew that.  But Caitlyn couldn’t help feeling like he was attacking her in a vulnerable state.  No matter how true his observations were.  Hearing it aloud felt like having floodlights pointed directly at parts of herself she was desperately trying not to examine.

She held up a hand, cutting him off, “What are you getting at, Jayce?”

“Look, Cait, you’re my best friend. But I’m also your boss. I have to make sure you are mentally well enough to handle this job.” He came right out and said it, ripping the band-aid off in one quick beat.

The words caught on nerves already frayed from a week of sleepless nights and constantly feeling on edge.  She simply stared at the steering wheel, unmoving.  Ever since leaving New York, her thoughts had felt tangled together like climbing ropes thrown carelessly into a duffle bag.

“You…think I’m depressed.” Caitlyn replied, exhaling in disbelief.  She turned away from him, gaze settling somewhere between the steering wheel and the dashboard.

Jayce sighed, “Why don’t we talk about this at home after we get you cleaned up? Come on, I’ll drive.” He started to open her door fully so that he could help her out of the camry and into his rav4.

But Caitlyn, in her stubborn haze, refused. “No, you’re the one that started this conversation now, Jayce. So, finish it.”

Frustrated, Jayce tapped the roof of the car with his fist, “Okay, fine. If you won’t tell me how you’re actually feeling and let me help you, I’ll be forced to put you on suspension.”

That snapped something inside of her resolve.  Caitlyn suddenly felt trapped, like the walls of the vehicle were closing around her and the air had become too thick to breathe.  Jayce was coming from a place of love, she knew that.  Rationally, she understood every word coming out of his mouth.

Emotionally, however, it felt as though everyone wanted something from her.  They wanted her to stop running, but it was the only thing she was good at these days.  Before she could think better of it, Caitlyn slammed the door shut.  The engine roared to life beneath her trembling hands, and before Jayce could stop her, she sped off in a cloud of dust.

It was well after nine when the last of the cars left the gravel lot outside of Lanes Family Adventures, leaving Vi feeling bittersweet.  She’d had such a good day that she almost wished it wouldn’t end.

A few of her hikers lingered a couple of hours after they’d returned from the hike, deciding to join Vi around the campfire with a couple of beers and some smores that were certainly well earned after a long day of hiking.

It was the sort of camaraderie she needed after such an emotional week, especially with Vander being gone and Powder out doing whatever it was that she was doing in her last summer before grad school. 

Now, she was sitting at the counter in the dim lamplight, the orange glow softening her features as she stared at her laptop screen.  New inquiries were flooding her inbox, from bachelorette parties to family reunions, it seemed like everyone wanted to book a guided rafting tour this summer.

How the hell was she going to manage all these rafting trips while also keeping the business running while Vander was away?  She really needed him to answer his fucking phone one of these days.

Vi sighed, pinching the bridge of her nose as she felt a slight headache coming on, when the telltale sound of vehicle tires crunching over gravel could be heard outside.  She looked up in time to see a pair of headlights illuminating the parking lot.

Her stomach dropped.  She was out here alone, and the warehouse was isolated from civilization, so if someone was coming to rob the place…

Vi pushed the thought away as soon as she recognized the car.  She’d ridden passenger in Jayce’s camry so many times that she could spot it right away.  But the last person she’d seen driving it was Caitlyn.  Why was she here?

Before Vi even got to the glass front door, the headlights switched off.  She stepped outside cautiously, still weary of being out here alone, and waited a few beats for the driver, assumingly Caitlyn, to exit the car.  But no one got out.

She felt the gravel through her thin flip flops as she walked closer, just mere inches from the driver’s side window, when she could see the figure hunched over the steering wheel.  Her heartbeat sped up in her chest as she raised a fist and knocked on the glass with her knuckles.

“Caitlyn?”

Vi watched the woman lift her head, their eyes meeting through the glass.  Caitlyn fidgeted with something on the inside of the car door, and a few seconds later the window rolled down, revealing the older woman and the exhaustion lingering on her features.

“Vi,” Caitlyn croaked, surprise lacing her tone, “What are you doing here?”

That earned a chuckle from the younger woman, “Me? I happen to work here. Shouldn’t I be asking you that?”

“Oh,” the woman replied, her cerulean eyes dropping to stare at the steering wheel, “I don’t really know. I guess I just needed some air…” she admitted quietly.

Despite her stomach doing flips at the fact that the older woman had seemingly found enough solace in her to drive to wherever she was, Vi frowned.  Did something happen to Caitlyn at orientation?

Vi pulled on the door handle, letting it swing open.  That’s when she was able to fully take in the state of Caitlyn—still wearing her dirtied uniform, her shredded trousers still bloodied, and her knee not attended to.

“Cait!” she exclaimed, throwing herself forward.  Vi reached over the woman, unlocking her seatbelt, “What happened?!”

The older woman only shrugged, seemingly gone mute.  Vi could see the tears welling up in her eyes, and it pulled at her heart so tightly that she swore it would burst at any second.  She didn’t want to push her, but she also needed to take a proper look at Caitlyn’s leg.  So, she slowly, carefully, slid her strong arms behind Caitlyn’s back and beneath her knees without bumping the injured one, and lifted her up.

When the older woman didn’t make a complaint, Vi walked her back inside and carried her through the staff only door, finding the ratty old couch in the back room and set her down gently.

“I’m going to grab the first aid kit, okay?”

Caitlyn nodded, though she was still unable to get any words out.

Vi had just stepped into the storeroom closet to grab the first aid kit off one of the shelves when her phone started to ring in her back pocket.  She pulled it out, seeing Jayce’s contact illuminate the screen.

“Jayce, if you’re—” she was cut off mid-explanation by his anxious rambling.

“Vi! I need your help. Caitlyn disappeared and we need to find her.” He sounded so lost, his voice shaky from the other line.

“I was just about to tell you she showed up here. I have her.” Vi replied, trying to calm her friend down.

Jayce audibly exhaled, “She needs to get her knee looked at, I’ve been looking for her for hours.”

Hours?  How long had Caitlyn been driving around for?  Where has she been?

“I’m already on it, don’t worry.” Vi replied instead.

“Okay, okay. Just keep an eye on her. I’ll be there as soon as I can.”

The line clicked dead before she could say anything else.  With a sigh, she grabbed the first aid kit and walked back out to the room, where she found Caitlyn already fast asleep, her cheek smushed against the couch cushion.

Vi kneeled on the floor, her deft fingers coming up to inspect the older woman’s knee carefully.  The green fabric was shredded where the injury was, a piece of it hanging down loosely like it’d been peeled away.  Caitlyn’s skin was covered in dried blood, and the gaping wound looked viciously angry. 

But there was no way to clean it or get a full assessment with these trousers in the way.  She didn’t want to wake the older woman up, but if she didn’t clean the wound soon, it would certainly get infected.

Vi reached up, a callused hand brushing away an errant blue strand from Caitlyn’s smooth cheek, “Caitlyn?”

Two cerulean eyes appeared in slits beneath heavy eyelids as a hum left the woman’s throat.

“Can you sit up for me?” Vi asked, voice low and patient as, for some reason, her thumb still lingered on the woman’s cheek, rubbing circles on her skin.

Caitlyn pushed herself up with a groan, wincing slightly as the pain in her hip reared its ugly head.  Vi’s hand had retreated from her cheek, and she found that she missed its warmth.

“It’s not as bad as it looks,” her tired voice came out just barely above a whisper and lacked that usual spark that Vi was used to hearing.

The pink-haired woman hummed at that, glancing at the wound again with furrowed brows, “It’s bad enough.” She replied.  Vi cleared her throat, “Are you comfortable with taking these off so I can patch you up?” She asked, tugging slightly on the loose fabric of the torn trousers.

“Oh, sure.” Caitlyn replied, trying not to draw attention to the heat that flushed her cheeks.  She swallowed, undoing the button and zipper and slowly pulling the waistband of her trousers down her legs, careful to avoid irritating her knee.

“Here,” Vi whispered, their fingers brushed she grabbed the fabric, helping the woman lift her legs with gentle hands while pulling it off and tossing it over the arm of the couch.

It was only when the trousers were removed that Vi could really get the full picture of Caitlyn’s injuries.  The knee wasn’t the only problem—a large, black and purple bruise bloomed over her hip.

Unable to stifle her grimace, Vi sucked in a breath, “Gods, Cait. What happened today?”

Caitlyn stared at the floor for a moment.  The silence left from her lack of response was filled with the soft rattle of the refrigerator and the buzz of the industrial lights hanging above them.

“I fell,” she answered eventually.

“No shit,” Vi deadpanned.

Despite the pain, a tired laugh escaped the older woman.  She watched in quiet reverence as Vi popped open the first aid kit and began digging through the supplies. 

Their eyes met again when Vi returned holding an ice pack. “Here,” she shook it until it activated and gently pressed it into Caitlyn’s hands, “For the bruise.”

The older woman shifted slightly and pressed the cold pack against the swollen purple mark on her hip.  The sudden chill made her flinch.

“I don’t like seeing you hurt,” the words slipped out from Vi’s lips too casually, like she hadn’t realized she was saying them out loud.  Her attention snapped back to the first aid kit, suddenly very interested in a pair of nitrile gloves.

Caitlyn’s stomach performed a somersault, the tips of her ears going hot in embarrassment at having the younger woman tending to her like their ages flipped.  She watched quietly as Vi snapped the gloves on and grabbed a steel bowl, filling it with clean water.

“Okay,” Vi said softly, settling back onto her knees in front of the couch.

There was something about having the pink-haired woman kneeling in front of her with such focused concern that made Caitlyn’s heart skip a beat.  Vi dipped a clean cloth into the water and carefully began washing away the dried blood.

Caitlyn sucked in a breath when the damp cloth touched her knee, “Fuck.”

“Sorry,” Vi whispered, ducking her head.

The older woman shook her head, “Nothing to be sorry for.”

Vi’s grey eyes flicked upward, locking onto hers like an arrow finding its target.  Caitlyn felt heat gather in her stomach at that penetrating gaze, suddenly aware that she was sitting on a couch wearing nothing but an oversized ranger shirt and her underwear while Vi sat between her knees looking far too respectful about the entire situation.

It probably would’ve been easier if Vi had stared, or looked even a little bit flustered.  Instead, she treated Caitlyn’s vulnerability like it was something precious and deserving of dignity.

“You’re thinking too hard.” Vi’s voice broke the silence.

Caitlyn blinked, “What?”

Vi smiled faintly while continuing to clean around the wound, “Your face gets all serious and you get these little dimples,” she pointed between her own eyebrows, “right here.”

“Well,” Caitlyn huffed a laugh, “excuse me for having thoughts.”

Vi hummed as she finished rinsing the worst of the dried blood away, “Dangerous hobby.”

Their eyes met once again, the silence feeling more comfortable the more time they spent in each other’s presence.

“It was a loose rock.” Caitlyn said out of the blue.

“What?” Vi asked, setting aside the bowl of dirty water and the copper-stained rag, grabbing the alcohol pads from the medical kit.

The older woman cleared her throat again, “I was reaching for the next hold, but the ledge I grabbed hold of sheared away as soon as I touched it.”

Vi’s stomach dropped, her movements slowed as she listened without interrupting.

“Another new ranger was holding the rope when I fell.  I feel like such an asshole because I was kind of dismissive of her.  She didn’t have any experience with outdoor climbing and she was just so…perky.”

That made Vi snort, “And you’re not into that sort of thing?”

Caitlyn smirked, unable to stop it, “No,” she whispered, “not my type.”

Their eyes met yet again, a collision of ocean and steel, like the titanic meeting the Atlantic for the first time.  All that was left to do was sink into each other.  Vi’s gaze flicked between Caitlyn’s eyes, her lips parted slightly, like she had a question on the tip of her tongue.  But at the last minute, she decided not to ask it.

“You tell Jayce everything? I’m sure he probably had to write a report, right?” Vi asked, wiping another alcohol pad over the wound, making the older woman hiss out in pain.

There was a lengthy pause before Caitlyn answered, “Um, most of it.”

Vi narrowed her eyes, “Most of it?” she quirked a brow in suspicion, eyeing the older woman with a look that clearly meant that she wanted to hear whatever it was that Caitlyn hadn’t told the Chief Ranger about the accident.

Caitlyn sighed, “Maddie may have caught me at first, but she was standing right in the path of the falling rock, so…I—I had to make a split-second decision…”

“Which was…?” Vi pushed for her to continue, though her mind began to race as she realized what the older woman was about to admit.

“I told her to move so she wouldn’t get hurt.” She confessed much to Vi’s growing horror.

The younger woman knew what that meant.  She’d been climbing at those very routes herself a few times.  Vi knew what happened when a belayer let go of the rope.

Caitlyn could see the fear that flickered in Vi’s expression, the way her eyebrows lifted toward her hairline and the way her lips parted slightly.  She could’ve sworn that her grey eyes had turned misty as well, until all those emotions shifted into confusion, her eyebrows pinched.  It emphasized that scar that cut through one of them.

“You could’ve died,” Vi croaked eventually.

All the older woman could do was nod, and suddenly neither of them seemed entirely sure what to do with the silence that followed.  Caitlyn glanced away first, her grip tightening slightly around the ice pack resting against her bruised hip. 

Eventually, Vi finished cleaning the wound and reached for fresh gauze.  As she worked, her gaze drifted upward again.  She chewed on her cheek before finally speaking, “Can I ask you something?”

“Anything.” Caitlyn’s response came out a little more breathless than she’d intended.

“Why’d you come here? I mean…you said yourself that you hadn’t expected me to be here.”

The older woman let her head fall back against the backrest of the couch, her cerulean eyes staring at the ceiling tiles.  The question lingered between them, because the answer wasn’t rational.  Caitlyn wasn’t entirely sure she understood it herself.

After several seconds, she released a slow breath, “Jayce and I fought.”

Vi’s expression softened, “A bad one?”

“Bad enough.” The older woman rubbed at her brow with her free hand, “He was worried, and I don’t blame him. I’ve just…been going through a lot and he wants me to be okay. But I don’t know how to stop running.” The last word was said so quietly that Vi wasn’t sure she’d heard it correctly.

“I don’t remember leaving, honestly.” Caitlyn continued, “I wasn’t really thinking. I just…needed to get away.” She confessed, her gaze drifting toward the dark window on the far side of the room.

Vi looked down at the finished bandage and smoothed one final piece of tape into place, “There.”

Satisfied, she finally leaned back onto her heels and pulled off her gloves.

The room suddenly felt much larger with the extra distance between them.  Caitlyn disliked that realization far more than she should’ve.  Meanwhile, Vi found herself staring for just a second too long before looking away.

She wanted to ask what was going through Caitlyn’s mind, what exactly she had been running from, but they’d both made it clear that they had things they didn’t want to talk about, so instead, she pushed herself to her feet.  Across the room, she found the merch stock room and pulled out a navy pair of Lanes Family Adventures sweatpants for Caitlyn to wear.

“Jayce is on his way,” She admitted, rubbing the back of her neck as she offered the woman the pair of sweatpants.

Caitlyn’s stomach dropped.  For reasons she couldn’t name, she didn’t want to leave.  But Vi was right, it was late and the older woman didn’t want to overstay her welcome.  She accepted the sweatpants with a soft smile, “Thank you, Violet.”

“Anytime, Miss Kiramman.”
