Chapter 6: Gods, I love the way you look at me

Summary:

Caitlyn wants to stop running, but can she truly confront everything she's been avoiding?

Vi has fun out on the river.

Notes:

Happy International Lesbian Day!

Let's celebrate with a chapter full of yearning :)

This is my favorite chapter I've written so far - Slowly developing these two characters and getting inside of their heads has been such a source of joy, creativity, and catharsis for me personally. I'm really looking forward to sharing where this goes, and I'm so grateful that, if you're reading this, you've decided to join me on this wonderous ride.

Enjoy! :) <3

CW: Brief mention of past unwanted physical touch (kiss).

(See the end of the chapter for more notes.)

Chapter Text

The ride home was awkward, to say the least.  It was nearly midnight, the roads dark and winding as the headlights cut through the trees, casting haunting shadows that Caitlyn couldn’t keep her eyes off of. 

She hadn’t said a word since Jayce picked her up from Vi’s workplace, instead opting to sit quietly in the passenger seat, her forehead resting on the window.  The glass fogged up with each breath she took, and she watched as it slowly receded until the next breath came.

Jayce also hadn’t said anything.  Not that he was mad that she’d essentially run away from her problems, again.  The man was simply afraid to say the wrong thing and cause his best friend to jump out of his moving car.

Vi had helped Caitlyn into the seat, the younger woman’s muscles had felt firm and warm under her fingertips.  She thought about those grey eyes, how they sparkled even in the black of night.  How Vi had whispered goodnight to her before closing the car door.

She thought about how attentive those callused hands were while bandaging her knee.  Gods, she wanted those hands on her more than she’d like to admit.

Eventually, the familiar orange glow of the cabin lights came into view, and the car rolled to a slow stop in the driveway, crunching over twigs and pinecones.  Everything went still, the tension in the air hanging heavily between them like thick fog.

Of course, Jayce was the first to break, “I shouldn’t have threatened to suspend you.” He sighed, disappointed that he’d failed as a best friend. “That wasn’t helpful.”

It took Caitlyn a few seconds longer to respond than it should’ve, but when she did, she looked at Jayce with the sincerest expression she could muster, considering how exhausted and in pain she was, “Well, maybe not. But you were right.”

His eyebrows practically shot off his head in surprise.  Jayce hadn’t been expecting that response, “I was?”

Caitlyn rolled her eyes, though couldn’t help but grin in amusement, “Yes, Jayce. I shouldn’t have lashed out like that when you were just expressing a concern.”

“I really am worried, Cait. I don’t like seeing you hurting like this and trying to hide it.”

The words echoed Vi’s earlier sentiment, and it made her heart clench.  Guilt bubbled in her gut, and Caitlyn realized that she was being selfish.  It wasn’t wrong for her to want to put herself first for once, but she couldn’t cut the people she loved most out of her life.  Most of all her mother.

She sighed, “I’ll call my mum, okay? I promise.”

Relief washed over him.  Jayce combed his fingers through his hair, releasing a breath, “Okay.”

Caitlyn smiled softly at him before pushing her door open, the cool, early-summer air rushing in and cleansing the tension from between them.  She didn’t even notice that her best friend had already exited the car, and was at her side seconds later, offering his arm to help her stand.

She accepted the gesture, letting Jayce guide her from the driveway to the cabin.  The lights flicked on and welcomed her home, her shoulders sagging from the weight of the day as she realized just how close she was to being able to sleep.

“Do you need help going up the stairs?” Jayce asked hesitantly.

Caitlyn nodded with a meek smile, “I’m sorry. I just don’t want to mess up Vi’s handiwork.”

He chuckled, the sound a deep rumble, “She’s good with her hands, huh?”

She choked at that, coughing as they ascended the stairs to the guest room, “Stop it!”

“What?” Jayce replied, mischief in his voice, “I saw the way she looked at you tonight, Cait.”

They were at her bedroom door before she extricated herself from his grip, hobbling over to the bed and sitting on the edge of the mattress, reveling in the relief it provided to her aching hip.

“I’ve got enough going on without you trying to push this…thing with Violet.” Caitlyn grumbled, slowly kicking off her shoes. 

“No one calls her that,” he pointed out with a smirk, leaning his shoulder against the doorframe.

She groaned, flopping her body back on the mattress and staring up at the ceiling, “I do.” She murmured.

Jayce sighed, pushing off the doorframe and letting his arms fall to his sides, “it’s been a long day, I’ll let you get some rest.”

He was gone before she could respond, the bedroom door closing with a soft click, followed by his footsteps echoing from the stairwell.

Caitlyn waited until she heard the familiar drone of the television speakers downstairs before digging into her pocket to pull out her phone.

Her mother was sure to have been long asleep by then, the woman a serious creature of habit.  She should really wait until the morning to call, but Caitlyn also knew that if she waited, she would sooner lose her nerve.

So, she thumbed her mother’s contact before she could talk herself out of it, and pressed the phone to her ear.

Cassandra answered on the second ring, her voice breathless and still clearly half asleep, “Caitlyn, dear, is that you?”

She wasn’t entirely sure why the woman was asking, considering she certainly had caller ID.  But perhaps she shouldn’t have been surprised by her mother’s disbelief, since Caitlyn had been dodging her calls for over a week now.

She swallowed, “Sorry to call so late, mother.”

Caitlyn prepared herself for the lecture, the stern words that were bound to follow.  Her heart raced beneath her ribs, pulse thundering in her eardrums.

Her mother would have choice words about her behavior, about abandoning her career and forsaking her responsibilities.  And worse, for damaging the reputation that came with the Kiramman name.

She sucked in a breath and bit her bottom lip—

“I’m so relieved to hear your voice, darling. Where are you? Are you safe?”

Caitlyn’s thoughts halted with a record scratch.  What?

There was no screaming?  No lecture?  No telling Caitlyn how much of a disappointment she was? 

Surely she was dreaming.  It was the only thing that made sense.  Except she wasn’t dreaming, the pain in her hip and her knee was enough to remind her of her lucidity.

“Caitlyn dear, please say something.” Cassandra’s pleading voice caught Caitlyn’s attention, the woman blinking back to reality.

“Y-yes, mother. I’m quite alright,” she croaked eventually, unable to stop the tears from welling up in her eyes.  She felt like a kid again, that brief time in her life when her mother had been more focused on Caitlyn’s emotions than her expectations of her.

She heard Cassandra release a breath of relief, one so strong that her mother must’ve been holding in since she first disappeared. “Your father and I have been so worried, darling. When Charlie first called, I didn’t know what to think.”

Caitlyn sighed, the moisture falling down her cheeks in rivulets, “I wasn’t even thinking, I just…I needed to get as far away from her as I could.” She sobbed, “It hurts so much, Mum.”

The hiccups escaped her lips before she could stop them.  Caitlyn was finally allowing herself to break in front of someone else.  She could only hope that her mother would help her pick up the pieces.

“Oh, dearest, I am so sorry.” Cassandra whispered, her own emotions clear in her voice. “Where are you? Your father and I will come to pick you up and bring you home. There is no reason for you to go through this alone.”

Oh, right.  Caitlyn had forgotten that Jayce didn’t tell her mother that she was staying with him, let alone the fact that she’d been hired on with the National Park Service.

She cleared her throat, “Well, actually, I’m afraid Jayce and I haven’t been exactly truthful with you, mother.”

“Is that so?” Cassandra’s tone shifted to something more serious, an undercurrent of sternness that Caitlyn recognized easily.

“I’ve accepted a new job in Virginia,” she confessed, keeping her voice as level as humanly possible, conjuring up every ounce of confidence. “Jayce has opened his home to me until I find my own place.”

The silence that followed was deafening.  Hell, she couldn’t even hear her mother’s breathing from the other line it was so quiet.  Caitlyn’s mind was running a mile a minute, coming up with arguments and explanations to counter anything her mother said.  Because she knew how this would go down.  She knew her mother would send the private plane for her to return to New York.

The response came quieter than she’d ever expected, “Okay.”

Caitlyn blinked, “Just okay?”

“Well, what more did you want me to say, darling?” Cassandra asked, genuinely curious.

That you expected more from me?  That I’m a disappointment?

When it was clear that Caitlyn was too stunned to respond, her mother filled the silence, taking the pressure off her daughter, “Darling, I realize that we’ve put a lot of weight on your shoulders in the last several years. We’ve always wanted what was best for you, but never at the expense of your happiness. I think I lost sight of that these last few years.”

Caitlyn didn’t realize just how much she needed to hear this from her mother.  She never would’ve expected these words to come from her in the face of one of the most impulsive and irresponsible decisions she’s ever made. 

“Your father wanted to say something when you missed Chinese New Year. He insisted that no matter how busy you were, you’d never miss it. But I convinced him that you and Charlie were probably doing your own thing for the holidays and…”

To Caitlyn’s horror, Cassandra began to choke up, her words full of the emotion she swore she’d never heard from her, “Gods, my Caity bug, I didn’t know how bad it had gotten for you. I understand why you left.”

She could hear her father’s voice in the background, probably having woken up from the sound of his wife’s tears.  Her mother’s voice was distant as she responded to whatever he had said to her.

“Mum,” Caitlyn croaked, unable to accept her mother’s supportive demeanor without pointing out the consequences of her own actions, “Aren’t you angry that I left the firm? In the middle of a case, no less? I’ll probably lose my bar license. My career is surely over…everything you’ve worked so hard to help me build…”

Cassandra interjected with a fierce determination, “Quite frankly, darling, Mr. Dauphin would be a fool to file for disciplinary action with the board, but should things come to that, I’ll handle it.”

“But—”

Her mother wouldn’t hear any further arguments, “We will retrieve your things from the apartment and have them sent to storage until you find a place of your own, Caitlyn. But let’s discuss this all at a more reasonable hour, hm?”

Caitlyn sat with her back against the headboard, utterly speechless by how this conversation had gone.  She’d spent a week avoiding this very moment, just for her anxieties to be all for naught.

“Your father wants me to tell you he loves you. We both do.”

Caitlyn finally responded, her voice wet and shaky, “I love you, too.”

The Shenandoah River was finally herself again.  Days of relentless rain had come and gone, leaving behind nothing but emerald-green mountains, fat white clouds drifting lazily across the sky, and water levels that had settled back into their perfect summer range.

The river still carried a little extra volume beneath its surface, enough to make the rapids lively, but not enough to make them dangerous.  In other words, it was a damn good day to be on the water.

Vi stood ankle-deep at the boat ramp, tightening the bow line of a fourteen-foot raft while five grown men loudly argued behind her.

“Gentleman,” she called over her shoulder, to which she received no response.

Apparently, bachelor parties had the collective attention span of a squirrel on an espresso binge.

“This is why you don’t get married,” one of the men declared as he struggled with the straps of his lifejacket.

“You’re literally the groom!”

“Exactly! Learn from my mistakes!”

The group erupted into laughter, and Vi rolled her eyes.

The groom, a broad-shouldered mechanic named Tyler, stood with a cheap plastic crown balanced on his head.  Beside him were four lifelong friends who looked like they’d spent the entire drive trying to out-roast one another.

A physician from Richmond, a high school football coach from Winchester, a contractor from Harrisonburg, and a software engineer from Charlottesville.  All of them had entirely different careers, and yet, the exact same maturity level.  Zero.

“Alright!” she barked, watching as all five pairs of eyes snapped toward her. “I know this is a bachelor party, but if any of you idiots fall out of my raft today, I will personally drag you back into it and make fun of you for the rest of the trip.”

They gave her blank stares and wide eyes, until finally they all burst out into a chorus of laughter.

It wasn’t until twenty minutes later that they finally pushed off, after she had to spend an extra several minutes explaining the list of commands again since they weren’t paying attention the first time.

The raft slid from the shoreline and into the current, catching the flow of the river instantaneously.  Sunlight glittered across the water while tree-covered mountains rose on both sides of the valley.  The breeze brought the scent of pine and wet moss, while a red-tailed hawk circled overhead.

Vi stood high in the stern of the raft, one foot wedged beneath a thwart, paddle resting against her shoulder as the current slid beneath them, pushing against submerged boulders and curling around gravel bars before reforming itself a hundred feet downstream.

To most people it was just a river, but to Vi, it was a book that she was constantly reading.  Her eyes scanned the water’s tells for every hidden hazard, and she knew exactly where they were heading.

“Alright, boys!” she shouted above the din of the trickling water and the raucous conversation.

The bachelor party roared back as the raft surged through a smooth section of current, gliding between towering sycamores whose branches reached over the water like arms, ready to pluck them right out of the boat.

“See those darker lines out there?” Vi pointed downstream.

The men squinted.

“Looks like normal water.” The software engineer pointed out.

“That’s because you’re blind,” Vi retorted, smirking as the rest of the group howled in laughter. 

She always rode this line between entertainment and safety when it came to her whitewater rafting trips.  While she was professional and focused, ensuring she got each and every one of her group members home without a scratch, she also wanted them to have fun.  Because wasn’t that the point?

“The darker water marks the strongest current, where the river’s deepest. The river wants to pull you there.” She explained.

Ahead, the tranquil section ended, and the horizon suddenly tilted downward.  The water compressed between two enormous boulders, which at one time had probably existed somewhere up on the mountains themselves.  The current accelerated as water funneled into a narrow chute.  The surface transformed from smooth green glass into foamy whitewater.

Standing waves roared downstream like an avalanche of rushing water, each one taller than the last.  The roaring sound of them made every guy in the raft sit up straighter.

Vi laughed, “Forward paddle!”

Their paddles dug into the rushing water, pushing the raft forward with increasing speed as the bow dropped into the tongue of the rapid.  The first standing wave crashed against the raft, cold river water exploding over the bow and taking Tyler’s plastic crown with it.

The group shouted as the second wave launched them even higher, as if they were riding an elevator made of water, until eventually gravity took over and the raft slammed back into the trough.

The warmth of the late-morning sun caressed her cheek like the hands of a certain pink-haired woman.  Cerulean eyes stirred as the bright, golden rays tickled at her eyelashes.  She blinked awake, the edges of her vision coming into focus until she could count the dust motes flitting in the summer sun.

Strands of midnight blue cascaded over her forehead and a small, moist puddle seeped into the pillowcase.  It’d been months since Caitlyn had slept well enough to wake up drooling.  Her brows furrowed when she realized that the sun wasn’t supposed to be up when her alarm went off for her second day.

She pushed herself up onto an elbow and leaned over to the nightstand, turning the alarm clock just enough so that she could read the time.

“Ten-thirty?!” Caitlyn panicked and threw the comforter off her body as she scrambled to her feet.  She should’ve been at work three hours ago.

The moment her feet hit the cold hardwood floor, a sharp pain echoed from her hip and down to her knee, reminding her of everything that had happened the day before.  She grunted and hobbled toward the bathroom, using the vanity’s surface to keep herself upright as she reached for her toothbrush.

It wasn’t until she looked up at herself in the mirror that she noticed a note taped to its surface, Jayce’s scratchy handwriting scrabbled in haphazard lines.

 

Hey Sprout,

Wanted to let you sleep.  Take the day off to rest and we’ll get you back out in the field tomorrow.  I’ll pick you up in the afternoon so we can retrieve the camry from LFA.

 

Great.  She’d only been on the job for a day and she was already placed on temporary leave.  Well, she supposed it was better than a long-term suspension.

Her shoulders sank and she sighed.  Maybe this would be good for her.  She could take the day to let the emotions settle and collapse into the couch cushions with a mindless show playing on the television.  Hell, Caitlyn couldn’t even remember the last time she’d been able to be a couch potato for a day.

Downstairs, she brewed a cup off coffee in the Keurig, the familiar scent of the grounds doing a much better job at waking her up than her body was.  In the fridge, there were a few eggs, but not much else—they should probably pick up groceries again if they wanted to bring lunch to work this week.

The gas stove clicked on, she spread some butter in a pan and cracked the eggs, frying them over-easy and flipping them onto her plate.  She found one slice of bread left in the bag and toasted it, then took her plate to the couch and curled her feet underneath her as she ate.

Caitlyn was still getting used to just how quiet it was out here in the valley.  All she could hear was the birds chirping in the canopy above the cabin and the soft rustle of leaves brushing the roof as the breeze swept through them.

She was about to turn on The Great British Bake Off when her cellphone rang, the sound of R2D2’s trilling scream jarring in contrast to the quietude.  Caitlyn startled and grabbed her phone, thumbing the answer button out of habit before even looking at who was calling.

“Hi mum, I meant to ask you last night if you have a key—”

“Baby?” 

Caitlyn’s heart stopped at the sound of the voice, which was clearly not her mother’s.  She pulled the phone from her ear, staring at the contact at the screen with wide eyes and a thrumming pulse. 

Charlie.

Fuck.  She really shouldn’t have been so careless as to not look at who was freaking calling.  Caitlyn was well aware that several people had been trying to reach her over the past week.  She couldn’t exactly hang up now, though, could she?

Caitlyn brought the phone back up to her ear and took a deep breath, “I thought you were my mother. I don’t want to speak to you.” She replied, cold as ice.

“Wait! Just give me a minute to explain, hun—”

She shook her head, even though the woman on the other line couldn’t see her, “You don’t get to call me that anymore, Charlotte. I don’t want to hear your explanation or your excuses. I’m done.”

A bitter laugh echoed from the receiver, “Oh, that’s rich. I make one mistake and suddenly miss perfect can’t handle it. Need I remind you of the mistakes you’ve made?”

“Don’t.” Caitlyn practically growled in response, the pressure in her chest felt like an oxygen tank ready to blow.  Her ears went hot and her chest heaved with heavy breaths.

It was a familiar argument.  One that her Ex had never been able to let Caitlyn forget.  One single unwanted kiss in the club, just months before they broke up, and Charlie had blamed Caitlyn for it.  She hadn’t even known the person who did it, nor did she even return the gesture.  One second, she’d been standing at the bar waiting for the bartender to make their drinks and the next, some stranger’s lips were on hers.

Of course, Charlie didn’t see it that way.  She never had.  She’d stomped over without even asking a question and grabbed Caitlyn’s wrist, pulling her away from the bar and out of the club, onto the dark street to berate her.

Caitlyn had been reduced to tears, unable to argue back. 

That’s when everything started—the withdrawal from her social life, from her parents, from any activities that existed outside of their little bubble.

Her ears had started ringing and she wasn’t paying attention to anything her Ex was saying, “Fuck you, Charlotte. You never gave a shit about me or how I felt. You only cared how I looked on your arm, like a fucking accessory for your deluded ego.” Caitlyn breathed hot fury, letting the words she’d been holding back for months finally fall from her lips, “I was assaulted and somehow, you made me the villain. And then what? You go and fuck a dude in our bed because you wanted to hurt me? To get revenge? You don’t get to lecture me anymore.”

There was only silence from the other line.  Good.

“My mother and father will be stopping by to pick up the rest of my things, and you will never see me again.” Her words were calmer then, shifting back to something glacial.  It felt good to get everything out, to finally have the courage to say what she’s been feeling for months.

Caitlyn didn’t wait for a response.  She hung up the phone and tossed it to the end of the couch, watching as it bounced off the cushion and hit the rug with a thud.

Her pulse was still pounding in her eardrums, but it was easier to breathe now.  A wave of relief washed over her, and then all she could do was laugh.

It was loud and unbidden, cathartic in a way that crying hadn’t been.  She was well aware of how crazy she’d look if Jayce walked in, cackling by herself in the cabin with not even a funny television show to blame for it.

She needed this.  To release the tension she’d been holding within herself not only for the past week, but for the past several months that she’d been so miserable.

Caitlyn had the sudden, overwhelming urge to tell Vi all about it, too.  That thought came rather unannounced, and she found herself wanting to spend more time in the pink-haired woman’s presence.  Simply being around her was enough to make her skin vibrate and her stomach flutter.

Then she remembered Jayce’s note—they’d be picking up the camry this afternoon.  Maybe she’d run into Vi and she could hear that cute laugh again.  She hummed at that possibility, picking up the remote and flicking on her favourite show, a smile plastered on her face that hadn’t been there just a few minutes ago.

Later that afternoon, after the sun had reached its peak and the temperatures soared, heating their skin despite the chill of the water, they approached the largest rapid of the run.

Vi guided the raft into a broad eddy on river right, letting the calm water catch them behind a large boulder, the river quieter there.  The roaring current slid past only a few feet away, past the eddy line while they floated peacefully in the protected pocket.

It was the perfect place to scout the rapid; Vi read the current with poised focus.

“Oh,” Tyler said as he looked downstream with wide eyes.

Ahead of them, the Shenandoah crashed through a maze of rocks and granite boulders.  A powerful tongue split the center of the rapid, a large hydraulic churned farther downstream, folding back on itself in a violent recirculation.  Massive standing waves towered beyond it.

“This one is called Devil’s Elbow,” Vi announced, turning back toward the group of men with a grin spreading from ear to ear.

The contractor from Harrisonburg chirped up, “Well, that’s certainly not comforting.”

Vi chuckled, “Relax. I’ve run this rapid more times than I can count.” She pointed with her paddle, “We enter through that tongue, then we’re going to ferry across the current toward river right to avoid that mess.”

Their eyes followed her gesture to where she pointed at the large hydraulic.  While Vi could probably manage to get herself out of that mess, she wanted to avoid it at all costs when she had other lives in her hands.

“We’ll have to paddle pretty hard, you think y’all are up for that?”

It was a challenge, one that she knew this group would accept with puffed up chests.

“Fuck yeah!” The teacher yelled, a battle roar echoing through the valley as the rest of the group joined in with raucous cheers.

Yeah, this is exactly why Vi wanted to become a guide in the first place.  She smirked and steered the raft in the right angle, “Forward paddle!”

Once again, their paddles dug into the water, scooping backward until they crossed the eddy line.  The current seized them as the eddy vanished behind them, the rapid thundering beneath their feet.  Water rushed from every direction as the raft shot toward the head of the rapid.

The tongue carried them straight down the center of the rapid, the first standing wave rising before them like a wall.  Vi felt her stomach leap with anticipation.  Gods, she loved this part.  The raft climbed, seemingly high above the surface of the river, until it dropped back down, the impact strong enough to send spray flying twenty feet into the air.

Laughter and shouting rang out from the group as the next wave came, soaking everyone to the bone with river water.

Now came time to paddle hard, the hydraulic churned angrily just ahead of them.

“Right side forward! Left side back!” She called out the commands like it was second nature.

The raft rotated perfectly, the group following her instructions to the letter, “Okay, both sides, forward paddle!”

They ferried across the current exactly as she’d planned, safely slipping past the hydraulic and toward the safe side of the river.  Not a single swimmer and not a single mistake—a perfect line that led them toward one last drop.  The bow tipped downward and the raft plunged through the cascade, sending cold water raining over everyone as they burst into the calm water below.

“That was fucking awesome. I think I’d follow you into war!” Tyler called out, voice slightly breathless from laughter and pure adrenaline.

Vi laughed quietly and rested her paddle across her shoulders, her cheeks burning from how much she smiled today. 

The river mellowed out as they left the rapids behind, whitewater fading into longer stretches of emerald-green.  The lazy current led them gently toward the designated take-out spot, no longer charging downhill with reckless abandon.

The bachelor party had finally settled down.  Well, as much as a bachelor party could settle down.

She listened as they debated whether any man should legally be allowed to own more than three grills, then argued about whether a bear or a gorilla would win in a fight.  Of course, it would be the gorilla, but Tyler was insisting it was the bear and Vi didn’t have the energy to join in the argument, so she kept her opinion to herself. 

She let them talk as she sat at the stern of the raft, occasionally making small corrections with her paddle to keep them centered in the current.  Her gaze drifted downstream while the voices of the bachelor party faded into background noise.

Her thoughts led her to Caitlyn, and she wondered how the older woman was doing.  Had the edges of her bruise started to turn yellow yet?  Did she stay off her leg like Jayce undoubtedly ordered her to?

A small smile tugged at the corner of Vi’s mouth when she thought that the latter seemed particularly unlikely.  Caitlyn didn’t come off as someone that could stay still for any amount of time.

Gods, she hoped she was alright.  It hadn’t even been twenty-four hours since she’d last seen her, and yet there was this ache beneath her ribs that refused to go away.  An irritating, persistent feeling of homesickness.  Except, it wasn’t a sickness from missing her bungalow.

No, she missed Caitlyn.

Vi groaned internally, because the feeling wasn’t getting better with time.  The more time she spent with the woman, it became harder to imagine not doing so.  It was becoming a serious problem, because she knew exactly where this road led.

She needed to be smart about this.  They’d already established that they were friends, and honestly?  That should be enough.  Because even if Caitlyn somehow did return those feelings, what then?  Vi wasn’t built for relationships.  At least, that was what she’d spent years telling herself.

Eventually, people wanted more—a future, a commitment with promises, and every time she imagined giving someone those things, panic crept into her chest like floodwater.

What if Caitlyn ended up looking at her the way Sarah had looked at her the other night?  Vi wasn’t sure she could survive seeing that expression on Caitlyn’s face.  The last thing the older woman needed was somebody making her life more complicated.

And yet…

Vi watched the current swirl past the raft, the sunlight shimmering across the surface.  And she found herself wishing Caitlyn wasn’t afraid of the water.  Because fuck, she wanted to share this with her.  She wanted to see those blue eyes light up when the raft dropped into a wave train.  Vi wanted to sit beside her in a quiet stretch like this one and listen to her ask a thousand questions about currents and eddies and river hydraulics.  She just wanted her here.

The wanting itself had become almost painful.  She sighed heavily and tipped her face toward the sun, letting its heat wash over her freckled cheeks and illuminate one simple truth: She wanted to see her again.

The sun had begun to set by the time the bus returned the group to Lanes Family Adventures.  Thankfully, Powder had parked the bus near the take-out spot, something the younger Lanes sibling had offered earlier that morning.

They’d loaded up the raft, paddles, and lifejackets in the trailer hitched to the back of the bus—a task that Vi normally found herself doing alone most trips.  So, she was incredibly grateful that her younger sister was there to help her this time around.

Her suspicions flared when Powder offered to drive back as well, but Vi chose not to comment on it, instead sitting behind the driver’s seat and staring out the window in contented silence.

The bus swayed as it drove over one of many dips in the gravel lot, “Sorry!” Powder called, laughing as she watched one of the guys, the software engineer, tip out of his seat, through the rearview.

“All good!” He replied.

Vi stood as the bus came to a halt, turning to face the bachelor party with a fond smile, “Thanks for a great day, gentlemen. It was a pleasure guiding you. Now, go get drunk!”

Each man patted her shoulder in thanks as they filed out of the bus.  Vi shook her head and grabbed the clipboard from its slot above the dash, about to head outside when Powder grabbed her wrist.

“Toots, who is that?”

Vi glanced back at her sister with furrowed brows, “What?”

Powder nodded in the general direction of the parking lot, “Her.”

Vi turned her head, grey eyes tracking the direction her sister had pointed out until they landed on the exact woman she’d been thinking about all day.  Her breath caught in her throat, like her lungs were suddenly incapable of inflating.

At the far edge of the parking lot, Caitlyn leaned casually against the hood of Jayce’s camry, the same spot where she’d left it parked the night before.  Even from this distance, she looked beautiful under the fading sunlight.  Her midnight hair shifted into hues of orange and purple, the strands blowing in the soft breeze.  Those impossibly blue eyes were locked in Vi’s direction.

“Uh, Earth to Vi?” Powder’s voice broke through whatever trance held Vi in its grip, “Do you know her?”

Vi snapped back to reality, clearing her throat, “Y-yeah, she’s a friend of Jayce’s.” she replied without offering any further explanation.  She climbed down the steps, feet landing in the gravel, and headed in that direction.

Powder watched her go with a tilt of her head, though her curiosity of the woman in the parking lot wasn’t enough to make her linger.  Besides, Ekko was waiting for her at Jericho’s, and she wanted very much to get drunk with him.

As Vi crossed the lot, she realized that she’d just spent all day on the river and certainly smelled like sweat and algae.  Hell, her wetsuit was still uncomfortably damp, and her hair was a mess.  Caitlyn would probably get one whiff of her and regret waiting for her here.

Someone nearby called her name.  When she turned her head, she found Tyler waving at her, “I’m totally going to convince my fiancée to come back for our honeymoon!” He chirped.

Vi rolled her eyes, though she couldn’t help breaking into a grin, “Careful! She may want to marry me instead!”

That earned her another round of laughter.

When she turned her gaze back toward Caitlyn, having walked much closer now, she could see the woman grinning back at her.  That shy, closed-lip smile that softened her angular features.  Her cheeks were kissed by the pink sunset, which made Vi’s stomach flip.

“Hi,” Caitlyn greeted when Vi was only a few feet away, her accented voice warm like honey.

Vi grinned, feeling almost relieved at being in the older woman’s company again, “Hey.” She breathed, adrenaline coursing through her veins, though not from the day’s adventure.

Her eyes fell to Caitlyn’s injured knee, where it was wrapped tightly in a fresh set of bandages.  She was definitely leaning most of her weight on her other leg.  It probably still hurt, she supposed.  When she looked back up, the older woman was watching her fondly.

“It looks like you had quite the day today,” Caitlyn said eventually, filling the comfortable silence that’d settled between them.

Vi nodded, “Yeah, bachelor parties are always a good time. They can get a bit out of hand if you don’t reel them in, though.”

Caitlyn hummed, though she didn’t say anything else.  Her cerulean eyes seemed to take Vi in, like she was assessing her, but she couldn’t figure out why.

Vi bristled under the attention, suddenly feeling heat at the tips of her ears.  Then, out of nowhere, the older woman pushed off the hood of the car and stepped into her personal space, so close that Vi could smell the vanilla scent of perfume on her neck.  Caitlyn reached up, Vi’s eyes tracking the movement as the woman brushed her slender fingers through pink strands, pulling something out that’d been tangled between them.

Caitlyn shifted her weight back, just enough to look into Vi’s grey eyes, “You had a leaf.”

Vi’s lips parted and her mouth went dry.  She tried to keep her breathing steady, though it was incredibly difficult with only a paper-thin distance between them. “Thanks,” she breathed.

“I was hoping we could talk,” the older woman announced, retreating to the car hood, this time hoisting herself up to lean back against the windshield.  She patted the spot next to her, gesturing for Vi to join her.

The younger woman hesitated, her heart racing beneath her ribs as she wrapped her head around what Caitlyn could possibly want to talk about.  Eventually, she acquiesced, carefully sliding up onto the hood, their shoulders brushing when she leaned back.

Neither woman said a word for a long stretch, instead settling into each other’s comforting presence with the ease of water sinking into sand.  Above them, the clouds were painted pink and gold, the sun making its slow descent behind the hazy blue mountains.

Caitlyn wasn’t sure why she was so nervous to talk to Vi.  When Jayce dropped her off an hour ago, she’d been insistent that she stay and wait for the younger woman to return, determined to open up to her about what she’d been running from.

She pulled her bottom lip between her teeth, rolled it between her canines before releasing a breath, “I came home to my girlfriend shagging some random wanker in our bed.” She whispered, her eyes locked on the darkening sky.  The moon was visible now, the waxing gibbous growing brighter as the sun disappeared.

Vi’s brain short-circuited.  She wasn’t sure what she had been expecting the woman to say, but it certainly wasn’t that.  She turned her head, taking in the sight of Caitlyn lying there next to her, biting her bottom lip.  She could see the way her throat bobbed as she swallowed.

“That’s…” She wasn’t sure what to say.  

Sad? Horrible? Annoying? 

Nothing seemed appropriate enough to describe what Caitlyn had surely been feeling.

“Disgusting,” was the word she settled on.

Next to her, the older woman snorted a laugh, releasing a breath she’d clearly been holding in.

“Yes, I suppose you’re quite right.” Caitlyn murmured, glancing at Vi briefly before her eyes found the sky again.

“Was it serious?”

The older woman shrugged, “Four years, lived together for two.”

Yeah, Vi would consider that pretty serious.  Sadness flickered in Caitlyn’s expression, and she watched the woman blink it away. 

“I should’ve seen it coming.”

Vi shook her head, “You can’t blame yourself. It’s not wrong for you to want to see the best in people.”

Caitlyn sighed, “Yes, well. She didn’t exactly treat me well the last few months, and I just let her.”

She choked on the last few words, biting her cheek to keep the tears at bay.  Vi was right, she shouldn’t blame herself, but she couldn’t help but wonder if she’d be any better off if she just ended things sooner.

Vi’s stomach dropped.  She pushed herself up on an elbow, looking down at the older woman with concern in her grey eyes, “Did she hurt you? I mean…”

Caitlyn shook her head, interjecting, “No, not physically.”

“Just emotionally.” It wasn’t a question.

The older woman swallowed and nodded, her blue eyes flicking up to meet Vi’s for a breath before retreating again.

Vi wished they’d linger.  But she understood.  She lowered herself back down, her back pressing against the cool glass of the windshield.  The breeze picked up as the sun went down and the stars started to fill the sky.

Caitlyn shivered and instinctively scooted closer to the younger woman, their shoulders pressed together more firmly now.

“Will you help me get comfortable with being in the water again?”

The question came out of nowhere, striking Vi right in the gut.  The fact that the older woman trusted her enough to help her face her fear made her blush. “Yeah, of course. But…why now, if you don’t mind me asking?”

“I’m tired of running. I want to take my life back, and I think it’ll be good for me.”

Vi hummed, “Must’ve been quite the revelation you had last night to change your mind like this.”

Caitlyn chuckled, the sound like a breath of fresh air, “Let’s just say I had someone patch me up so well that it fixed more than just my knee.”

“Ah, well, could you get me their number? I’ve got this pain in my lower back…”

She was cut off by the older woman’s deep laugh, the corners of her eyes crinkling with joy as her hands covered her stomach, trying to catch her breath.  Vi watched her in pure reverence, her own stupid grin spreading from ear to ear.

Caitlyn’s laughter faded and she turned her head, meeting Vi’s eyes again.  There was something about those grey eyes that she found comforting, like every time she caught their gaze, she got lost in them.  Maybe lost wasn’t the right word.  Maybe instead, it was as if she found herself in them.  Like Vi saw her for who she was even when she didn’t even know it herself.

It was crazy, wasn’t it?  They barely knew each other.  And yet, she was so drawn to the younger woman, like they were being pulled into each other’s orbit.

Without thinking too much about it, she reached up, brushing away a river-matted strand from Vi’s forehead.  The touch was featherlight, barely more than a whisper of the wind, but it was enough to make her stomach feel like a merry-go-round.

“Does that mean I’ll see you tomorrow then?” She asked when the moment passed.

Vi’s lips parted, but it seemed like she struggled with words.  It wasn’t until the breeze tickled her nose that she spoke, “Y-yeah. After your shift?”

“I’ll be here.” Caitlyn replied before pushing herself up and off the car again, “I should go. Early morning,” she shrugged, hesitated, then giggled, “And you need a shower.”

That made the younger woman cackle, “Are you saying I stink?!” She exclaimed, hopping to the ground with a thud.

“Oh, no, of course not. I’m saying you reek, Violet.” Caitlyn smirked, mischief sparkling in her blue eyes.

Vi scoffed, “Incredibly rude. And here I thought you posh types were supposed to be polite.”

The older woman shook her head, “We are, darling, but you’re forgetting that I spent my entire adult life in New York City.”

“The best of both worlds, then.” Vi hummed.

Caitlyn smiled softly, “I suppose so.”

She pulled the driver side door open, lingering outside it for just a moment, “Goodnight, Violet.”

“Goodnight, Miss Kiramman.” Vi replied, a teasing lilt to her voice. 

She watched Caitlyn shake her head in amusement and step into the car, the door closing behind her with a thunk.  The engine buzzed to life, the tires crunching over the gravel and the headlights illuminating the now dark parking lot as the older woman drove away, leaving Vi in the soft red glow of the tail lights.

 

Notes:

That final scene had me giggling and kicking my feet while writing it.
