Chapter 7: I swam across, I jumped across for you

Summary:

Vander keeps Vi and Powder in the dark.

Meanwhile, Caitlyn and Maddie lead a climbing safety demonstration.

And later, Vi takes Caitlyn to the water for some exposure therapy.

Notes:

Happy Friday, Everyone!

This chapter has been done for a few days, but I wanted to hold off on posting it until I finished writing Chapter 8 so that I could be a chapter or 2 ahead, that way you're not waiting a long time for an update!

Chapter 7 is a bit shorter than I usually write, but chapter 8 is a doozy so it'll surely make up for it :)

Anyways, thanks so much for reading and tagging along this journey with me! Happy Reading!

(See the end of the chapter for more notes.)

Chapter Text

Caitlyn woke to the sound of someone grunting, followed by repeated heavy thunks echoing from somewhere downstairs.  Night still covered everything outside in shadow, with the moon hidden behind a couple lazy clouds hovering high in the sky. 

She reached for her phone where it had been charging on the nightstand like it always was at this time of night and tapped the screen.  The bright light blinded her for a moment, her cerulean eyes taking a moment to adjust to it.  The screen showed that it was just barely past four in the morning.

Caitlyn groaned, sliding out of bed and padding out of the guest room in her socked feet.  In her sleepy haze, she didn’t even bother grabbing an object for self-defense, knowing full well that whatever was going on was Jayce-related.

The breathless grunts continued, and when the stairwell wall opened to the rest of the living room, Caitlyn hesitated.  Part of her didn’t want to know what she was about to find.  It reminded her too much of her last night in New York.

Thankfully, however, when she eventually turned her head, she found Jayce on the floor between the coffee table and the television, locked in a vicious cycle of push-ups.  His shirtless, tanned back was covered in a sweaty sheen.  He’d clearly been at it for a while.

“Are you really doing push-ups at four in the morning?” She croaked, voice still hoarse from sleep.

Startled, the man fell mid-pushup, faceplanting right into the rug.  For a second he thought he may have broken his nose, his hand racing up to check for blood that, thankfully, wasn’t there.  Jayce regained his composure and pushed to his feet, glaring at Caitlyn, “You scared the shit out of me.”

“Sorry?” Caitlyn shrugged and ambled over to the couch, where she flopped down and elevated her injured leg.  The bruise on her hip peeked out from under her short, blue silk pajama shorts, a nasty combination of purple and yellow.

Jayce sighed, “What If I was hooking up with someone?”

Her eyes narrowed, “Well, I’d hope you’d do so in your room. You know, with the door locked.”

“Noted,” he chuckled, wiping sweat from his brow with the back of his hand.  His hazel eyes flicked to her wrapped up knee, then back to her sleepy face, “Are you sure you’ll be okay coming back today?”

“I need to work, Jayce.” Caitlyn pleaded, “Besides, you need someone to mentor the senator’s daughter. What better way to show her that there’s nothing to be afraid of by getting back up on the slab after a fall?”

Jayce couldn’t argue with that logic, although he wanted to point out that perhaps she’d been a bit reckless and he’d rather not instill that behavior in the younger woman, but he knew better than to question Caitlyn’s methods.  Especially considering he’d been the one who asked her to mentor.

He held up his hands in surrender, “Alright, alright.”

They sat in awkward silence for a moment, until a thought popped into Caitlyn’s head unannounced and she couldn’t hold it in anymore, “Is Violet seeing anyone?”

Jayce’s eyebrows shot up into his hairline, “Wha—where is this coming from?”

“Is she?” Caitlyn pushed, ignoring the question.

He rubbed a hand through his sweat-slick hair, “I mean, she sees people, pretty regularly, but uh—no. Vi doesn’t do relationships.”

She frowned at that, “Doesn’t do…relationships? What does that even mean?”

Jayce sighed, suddenly feeling trapped in a conversation he wasn’t sure he was supposed to be having.  As much as he wanted his two friends to get together, at least for the fun of it, he didn’t think it was his place to talk about Vi’s personal life, “It’s not for me to say, you’ll have to ask her.”

Caitlyn groaned and grabbed a couch pillow, tossing it at him with lazy force, “What good are you?!”

The pillow just grazed his shoulder, “Why do you want to know?!” He chuckled and tossed the pillow back in her direction.

“I’m just…curious.” It wasn’t a complete lie.  She really did want to know more about the younger woman she’d been spending so much time with these past couple of days.  But she wouldn’t admit that she wanted to know for selfish reasons, too.

Jayce clearly knew her better than that, too. “You like her!” he exclaimed, mischief lining his wide grin.

“Again...I am just going through a breakup here!” She reminded him, rolling her eyes.  Still, her heart fluttered at the thought.  Did she like Vi?  Or was she just happy to have someone pay attention to her for once?  Well, someone other than Jayce.

“Riiight,” Jayce teased, still smirking wildly.

Caitlyn ignored his antics, “Vi is going to help me get comfortable with being in the water again.” She admitted quietly.

His expression softened in the way that it only ever did for his best friend, “That’s great, Cait. If there’s one thing I know about Vi, is that she’s reliable. I’m glad you’re trusting her with this.”

“Me too.” She replied. “Though, if I’m honest, I’m quite terrified.”

Jayce let out a sympathetic sigh, “I know. But just imagine, how cool would it be if by the end of the summer, you two were out there running rapids together?”

Her blue eyes went wide, “Oh, gosh, I don’t think I could do that.” She shook her head, unable to wrap her brain around the thought of it.

He hummed, “You never know.”

Then, when it was clear she was stuck somewhere in her head, he offered an out from the conversation, “Well, get dressed. I want to get to the station early so we can double-check the equipment before sending you out on the field today.”

Caitlyn groaned again, because yeah, it was going to be a long morning of inspecting climbing ropes and quickdraws, “Fine.”

The wind slammed against the building all morning, a battering ram that rattled the loose metal siding of the warehouse and sent the old sign hanging over the bay doors creaking on its chains.  Through the large lobby windows, the trees beyond the gravel lot swayed and bent, their leaves looking like they’d rip right off at any minute.

The sun still shone brightly over the Blue Ridge, blanketing the slopes in shades of emerald and gold, but strong wind meant that the water would be rough out there today.

Vi sat behind the front counter with one boot hooked over the rung of her stool and a mug of coffee cooling between her palms.  The smell of roasted beans mixed with the familiar scents of rubber rafts and old wood that always lingered inside the lobby.

On her laptop, weather maps glowed on the screen in color coded sections of reds, greens, and blues.  Forty-mile-per-hour gusts were expected to blow through the area by mid-day. 

She clicked her tongue.  The private kayaking tour was supposed to launch in three hours.  If the wind settled, she’d still take them out, but if it didn’t, she’d have to cancel.  Again, another refund in the face of uncontrollable weather. 

Grey eyes drifted toward the accounting software still open in another tab—spreadsheets and numbers that might as well have been in another language.  Vi rubbed a hand over her jaw and stared at the screen.  The totals weren’t adding up.

Maybe they actually were and she was simply too stupid to understand them, but every time she’d logged in over the last week, something felt off.  It was like looking at the river and knowing a strainer was lurking ahead without being able to see it.

Her fingers drummed against the countertop.  If Vander were here, he would’ve known exactly what the issue was.  If he’d just answer his damn phone.

The thought soured the coffee in her mouth.  She barely managed to swallow it when the staff-only door slammed open hard enough to bounce off the stopper, revealing a wide-eyed Powder.

The look on her younger sister’s face was almost enough to make her drop her mug, “It’s Dad.” The blue-haired girl announced, holding out her cellphone, the screen illuminated with Vander’s contact card.

Vi’s veins flooded with relief, which was then quickly shoved aside by anger as she snatched the phone from her sister’s hand.

“Suddenly remembered you have daughters?” The words came out more sarcastic than she’d honestly intended, but Vi would refuse to apologize for it.  She had every right to be frustrated with her adoptive father’s lack of communication over the past week.

Powder circled the counter and leaned in close enough that her blue hair brushed against Vi’s shoulder.  Through the speaker came a familiar low chuckle, “Ha, ha. Very funny, kiddo.”

His voice sounded too normal, too…nonchalant for having been unreachable.  Too calm like they hadn’t spent every day wondering whether something had gone wrong.  For fuck’s sake, she’d almost called the NYPD the other day and filed a missing person’s report.

Vi’s grip tightened around the phone, “Are you going to tell us what the hell is going on?” she asked. “Or are you going keep dodging the question?”

Silence crackled briefly through the speaker, followed by a heavy sigh.  Vander was clearly already tired of this conversation, “I know you girls are worried, but everything is fine. I’m just working.”

Vi stared out the window; a gust of wind ripped through the parking lot, sending gravel and dust skittering about in tiny cyclones. “On what?” She asked after a heavy silence, not accepting his vague explanation.

“Violet.” The sound of her own name made her jaw clench.  It was the sound of stubbornness, of the infuriating refusal to budge.  Unfortunately, she’d inherited every bit of it from him, “Don’t worry about it. Tell me how you girls are doing.”

Translation: Stop asking.

Vi’s nostrils flared as she contemplated pushing.  But she knew where that road would lead.  Neither of them would back down and she’d leave the conversation even angrier than when it started.  So, she bit her tongue instead.

 “Yeah, we’re fine. Though I noticed something off about the accounts and I don’t know what to make of it.”

Maybe that would finally get his attention, maybe—

“Has Powder submitted all of her applications yet?” He asked, completely ignoring everything Vi had just said.

Vi blinked, and across the counter, Powder slowly closed her eyes, “Oh my God.” She muttered.

The frustration bubbled up her throat like trapped whitewater, “Uh, yeah, I guess so.”

Again, he plowed right past the topic as if she’d never spoken, “Listen, you’re going to be on your own a little longer.”

Vi’s stomach twisted in a knot, “A little longer?” she asked, voice smaller than it had been just a minute ago.  Her eyes met Powder’s, neither of them liked the sound of that.

“I need you girls to stay tight on the money for a while.” He instructed without further explanation, and the knot in Vi’s stomach tightened even further, this time pulling at her ribs. “And tell Powder no sleepovers with Ekko in my house.”

She frowned, “Dad what’s going on with the money—”

“Gotta go!” Vander cut her off and the call went dead before she could even finish her thought.  He had hung up on her.

What the fuck?

Vi stared at the darkened phone screen, her reflection staring back.  The phone suddenly felt heavier in her hand with the weight of unanswered questions.  A strange unease settled beneath her skin, like she was missing something important.

Powder’s brows furrowed.  She’d been thinking the same thing. “That was fucking weird, right?”

The wind rattled the building again and her coffee had gone cold on the counter.

“Yeah, you can say that again,” Vi mumbled, handing her younger sister her cellphone back.

Powder took the phone but didn’t immediately move away.  Instead, she leaned against the counter and studied Vi like a predator studying its prey. 

“So…you ever gonna tell me what the deal is with a that chick?”

The conversational whiplash nearly gave Vi a headache. 

Heat spread in her chest like she’d swallowed the embers of a campfire.  She knew exactly who Powder meant, and that wasn’t something she was particularly ready to confront.

Vi didn’t want to tell Powder about Caitlyn.  Not that she thought her friendship with the older woman was some sort of secret she needed to keep, or that she didn’t trust her younger sister with the knowledge of the woman, but because Vi, selfishly, wanted to keep whatever it was that was happening between them to herself for a while.

She purposely focused on the coffee in her hand, scrunching her nose slightly at its cold temperature when she took a sip, “Oh, the one from last night?” she asked, hoping to look casual and unbothered, “I don’t really know her. Jayce just hired her for the climbing ranger position.”

Powder tilted her head, her blue eyes narrowing in suspicion, “You were pretty cozy for not really knowing her.”

“I don’t know what you’re talking about.”

Her sister scoffed, “Right, like you don’t remember sitting on top of a car and staring at the stars like you were in some kind of rom com last night.”

Vi groaned, “You’re insufferable.”

“And you’re being cagey.” Powder retorted, crossing her arms against her chest.

She dragged her hand through her pink hair, because Gods this conversation was suddenly harder than navigating dangerous rapids. 

Why was talking about Caitlyn somehow the terrifying thing?

Was it because thinking about Caitlyn made her feel things she’d spent years avoiding?  Because every time those blue eyes landed on her, something inside her softened that’d previously been frozen in stone?

Vi exhaled, settling on the safest answer possible, “Look, Cait’s just going through a breakup and I’m…keeping her company. It’s nothing more than that.”

Powder snorted with pure, unfiltered disbelief.  When Vi looked up, her sister had one eyebrow raised so high it nearly disappeared into her hairline.  The message couldn’t have been clearer: You’re full of shit.

She sank further into the stool, wishing it would just swallow her whole, so she didn’t have to face her nosey sister any longer.

“Don’t you have anything else to do today?” Vi mumbled into her mug, avoiding Powder’s curious eyes.

Her sister chuckled but clearly decided that she’d given Vi enough grief for one day.  She pushed off the counter, “Ekko and I were going to hit up Dark Hollow today, unless you need me for anything…” she trailed off, voice lilted in the way that told her older sister she was only offering out of obligation.

Vi was perfectly fine with that.  She needed some time alone to gather her thoughts, “No, no. Go, have fun.”

“Later, Toots!” Powder replied, relieved that she wouldn’t get stuck at the family business all day and skipped out the door with a pep in her step.

Vi let out a groan when the wind slammed the door shut behind her sister and finally let her head fall onto the counter’s surface.  She was fully caught in a recirculating current of midnight-blue and cerulean, with absolutely no hope of paddling out.

By the time Caitlyn and Jayce had finished inspecting the seemingly endless lines of climbing rope, the rest of the morning shift began filing into the ranger station in a sea of khaki uniforms and wide-brimmed campaign hats.

Maddie practically sprinted over to her when the younger woman realized that Caitlyn was back at work, “Hey Caitlyn! You’re back!”

Despite herself, Caitlyn actually smiled back, “Hey, yeah, all better.” She replied, eyes lowering pointedly to her new, non-shredded uniform trousers.

A flicker of lingering guilt crossed Maddie’s expression and her voice shifted to a lower volume, “I’m really sorry for letting go of the rope, I—”

Caitlyn held up a hand, immediately shut down that line of thinking, “Maddie, I want you to promise me that whenever I give you a command, you follow it to the letter. If you hadn’t moved when I told you to…” she let that thought trail off, not wanting to say it out loud.  They both understood what the consequences would’ve been, anyways.

The younger woman simply nodded, not knowing what else to say.

“Good,” Caitlyn smiled, “Now, Jayce has us scheduled to lead a climbing safety demonstration over at the Skyline Wall. You up for it?”

Maddie huffed, “Depends. What age group?” she joked, smirking with a sparkle in her eye.

That made Caitlyn laugh, because she understood that sentiment all too well.  She wasn’t exactly fond of kids, either.  They were much too raucous and persistent for her to handle.  Kind of like her first impression of Maddie, though now she found she was much more open-minded to working with her than she had been pre-fall.

“It’s a group of college climbers, so, the best kind.”

Nearly two hours later, they’d reached the location of their safety demonstration and hauled their equipment from the camry to the slab.

Morning mist still hung over the lower elevations of the Blue Ridge Mountains.  The Skyline Wall sat atop a 3,000ft tall ridge, directly east of Old Rag and northwest of the Reflector Oven climbing routes. 

It was easy to see why these climbing routes were a favorite amongst visitors to Shenandoah National Park.  The ridge offered a wide-stretching view of the valley, unobstructed by tree-tops and other ridges.  Boulders the size of cars sat sturdy on the cliff face, offering the perfect conditions for climbing enthusiasts.

Caitlyn stepped onto a flat boulder at the base of the wall and smiled at the assembled crowd, a group of college-aged young adults from an indoor climbing gym in Charlottesville, all of whom wanted to branch out into more thrill-seeking climbing adventures.

Most wore climbing shoes already, and several were already strapped into their harnesses, eagerly studying the racks of gear that Caitlyn and Maddie had spread out on a staked-down tarp.  The wind had started to pick up as the morning carried on, not yet dangerous, but something they’d certainly pay attention to as the demonstration continued on.

Their enthusiasm reminded Caitlyn of herself years ago, before she forgot what it felt like to love something.

“Before anyone climbs today,” Her confident, accented voice rose above the din of conversation and whistling wind, “We’re going to talk about safety.”

A chorus of groans rippled through the group, which she’d expected.  She raised her hands, gesturing for everyone to settle down before continuing, “How many of you have ever placed trad gear?”

Crickets.

“Okay…” Caitlyn glanced at Maddie, who only shrugged back at her.  While these weren’t professional climbers, she’d still expected at least one of them to have some sort of experience with outdoor climbing.  Oh well, years spent in the courtroom meant that the older woman was used to making last-minute adjustments.

“Trad climbing introduces a rather different mindset than top-roping or sport climbing,” She stepped forward, a rack of cams jangling against her harness.

Caitlyn pulled a cam off her gear loop and held it up, “In sport climbing, someone has already decided where the protection goes. The bolts are fixed into the rock before you ever arrive. In traditional climbing, you’re responsible for building your own safety system as you climb.”

As she compressed the cam’s trigger, the lobes folded inward, giving the crowd a view of the mechanics of the cam, “The consequence is that protection becomes a skill you learn, rather than an assumption to rely on.”

A young climber raised his hand, his shoulder-length sandy-brown hair pulled back in a neat bun at the base of neck, “So how do you know if it’s safe?”

“Experience,” Caitlyn replied.

The student laughed in response, but she didn’t. “It’s truly the only answer. You learn by feeling what a good placement feels like.”

Maddie walked over to a waist-high crack and slid a cam into place, “Like this,” she demonstrated, releasing the trigger.  The lobes expanded against the granite and she tugged downward; it didn’t budge.

“Form a line, we want each of you to be able to feel how secure this is,” the younger ranger directed.

 The group followed her instructions, lining up by the cam.  Each climber tugged on it, some of them with as much force as possible, to the point that they were no longer testing the hold, but their strength, and then joking about it when they couldn’t force the cam to shift.

After thirty minutes of discussing protection, anchor systems, rope management, and Leave No Trace ethics, the group migrated toward one of the Skyline Wall’s shorter, beginner-friendly routes.  A forty-five-foot climb with a 5.6 rating called ‘Vegemite,’ which requires smaller-sized cams to scale the crack.

It was perfect for demonstration purposes.

Maddie stood with a coil of rope hanging from her shoulder, “You climbing first?” she asked, voice pitched down so the rest of the group couldn’t hear.

Caitlyn flinched at the question despite herself, because just three days ago, she’d directly confronted the possibility of dying.  That sort of traumatic experience would have lingering effects on anyone, even someone with as much professional experience as she had.

Her blue eyes shifted upward, studying the route without a word.  Objectively speaking, she could probably free-solo it without increasing her heart rate—hand jam to start, short reach toward a small ledge, finger jam the rest.  And yet, her palms still felt damp.

“Yeah,” she finally answered, “I’ve got it.”

Maddie caught the brief hesitation, but much to Caitlyn’s relief, the younger woman only said, “We can swap if you want.”

Caitlyn eyed her for a moment, like she was half-considering the offer.  But she knew that if she didn’t climb now, especially on a route that was designed for beginners, her anxiety would only grow in the future.  It was like her mother always said…get back up on the horse.

“No,” she finally replied, clipping herself into the belay.

Maddie took her answer for what it was and wisely chose not to comment.  Instead, she stepped back, giving the older ranger enough berth to make her start.  She grabbed the rope, holding it steady between her hands and spoke loudly enough for the group to hear, “On belay!”

“Climbing,” Caitlyn responded, equally as loud.  And when Maddie’s response, “Climb on,” came, her fingertips grazed granite. 

The first few moves required her to hand-jam a fist-sized crack to ascend the route.  As she traversed the rock, she reached her right hand down to her harness, where her chalk bag was clipped right by her hip, letting the powder cover her unusually sweaty hand before switching arms and repeating the process.

About midway through the route, the crack narrowed.  Caitlyn found a sturdy ledge just before the move and held on with one hand, while unclipping a cam with the other.  She called down to the group watching below, “This crack narrows here.”

Holding up the cam so the students could see her instruction, she slowly inserted the protection into the crack and released the trigger, watching closely to ensure the lobes expanded properly.  Caitlyn gave the cam a good tug and released a breath that she hadn’t realized she’d been holding when it didn’t move.

“All four lobes are engaged, and as you can see,” she tugged firmly again for emphasis, “it’s secure.”

She began to move again, finger-jamming the rest of the narrow crack until it veered drastically left.  Before scaling the overhanging ledge, she placed another cam, once again testing its hold before calling down to the group, “If the cam is over-cammed or under-cammed, it becomes significantly weaker. You want it to expand without forcing it.”

Caitlyn scaled the route as it shifted left near the top of the route and began building the anchor system that would be used for the rest of the group to safely climb the route without having to replace cams each time someone ascended.  They could simply rely on a belay and an anchor point to hold them should they bail out.

“Secure!” she exclaimed once the anchor was built and she was clipped in.  The group applauded below, whistling and cheering her on, filling her chest with a warmth she hadn’t felt all morning.  It was endearing—was this how Vi felt every time she made a run downriver guiding a bunch of tourists in her raft?

With one last command, she was ready to get her feet back on solid ground, “Take!”

Unfortunately, the winds never died down that afternoon. 

Already irritated enough that she had to cancel her kayaking tour, Vi’s mood worsened when she called the wife of the couple she was supposed to meet, and the woman chewed her out for the last-minute decision.

She tried to explain that the decision wasn’t made lightly, that she had hoped the weather would cooperate, but ultimately the safety of her guests was paramount.  But the woman insisted that Vi was a fucking idiot.

Customer service.  Ain’t it great?

The only thing that was keeping her from retreating to the bungalow and slipping under the covers for the rest of the day, was the fact that she was currently on her way to Jayce’s cabin to pick up Caitlyn Kiramman.

The name itself acted like a mood stabilizer for Vi.

Golden light streamed through the tree canopy as she pulled into the driveway, casting leaf-shaped shadows across the dirt.  There, sitting on the steps on the front porch waiting for her, was Caitlyn.  Just knowing that the older woman was sitting there waiting for her in anticipation was enough to make Vi’s head spin.

And then, of course, was the sight of the woman in general.  Vi always thought she was beautiful, but the way that the halter straps of her bathing suit hugged her neck and disappeared beneath a navy crewneck made her mouth go dry.

Caitlyn’s smile grew when she caught Vi’s eyes through the windshield, the sight of which filled Vi’s chest with warmth like cookies baking in an oven.  The older woman waved awkwardly as she stood, her long legs carrying her to the passenger side. 

“Hi,” she greeted breathlessly as Caitlyn slid into the seat, buckling her seatbelt with ease.  The familiar scent of her vanilla perfume followed the gust of wind that rushed in through the open door.

The older woman’s cheeks flushed pink, visible enough in the golden afternoon light that Vi actually noticed, though she didn’t comment on it.

“Hey, are you alright?” Caitlyn asked, her smile giving way to a frown.

Confused, Vi was about to ask why when, suddenly, the pad of the woman’s thumb was smoothing out the tense wrinkles between her eyebrows that she hadn’t even realized were there.  Probably the lingering undercurrent of irritation from the day’s events, if Vi could guess.

“You’re tense,” Caitlyn commented, sliding her thumb from Vi’s forehead down to her cheek, “did something happen today?”

Vi’s lips fell open, the younger woman momentarily dumbfounded at the softest caress against her skin.  She couldn’t believe just how easily Caitlyn could undo her with a simple touch.  The warmth of her palm on her cheek melted into her skin, to the point that she almost wondered if they’d fused together.

The older woman was looking at her like she hung the stars, her eyes so bright and shimmery like the ocean’s surface sparkling under the moonlight. 

Shaking her head and forcing herself to come back to reality, Vi reached up, covering Caitlyn’s hand with her own before pulling it down and into her lap, “It’s nothing, just some family stuff going on. Nothing to worry about,” she answered.

And before the older woman could ask anything else, Vi changed the subject, “Are you nervous?”

Caitlyn shifted in her seat, her hand naturally pulling away from Vi’s grasp and brushing a strand of her hair behind her ear, “A bit, yes.” She replied honestly.

Vi hummed, throwing the car in drive and easing on the gas, the tires rolling over the dirt and gravel of Jayce’s driveway, “Don’t worry, we will start off slow. I won’t push you to do anything you aren’t comfortable with.” She assured her. 

And just as quickly as she’d said it, Vi recognized the unintended double-entendre that was laced within her words, “I mean,” she cleared her throat, “In the water.”

Caitlyn held back a smirk, quirking a brow at that, and decided to tease the younger woman, “What else could you have possibly meant?” She asked with feigned innocence.

Horrified, Vi’s eyes went impossibly wide and she stuttered, “Oh, I—”

This was new territory for her.  Normally, she would have no problem talking about sex with a friend.  Hell, she had bedded half of them.  But, there was something about the older woman that made her want to hold back. 

The older woman cackled, “Violet, I’m just messing with you.”

“That was mean.” Vi replied, though not without glancing over at her passenger with a wide grin.  Damn, so this woman really did have a great sense of humor.  She really liked this playful side of her.

Caitlyn’s cute tooth-gap was visible.  It made Vi swoon.  Gods, she really needed to get a grip.

The drive lasted nearly an hour down Route 81, passing truck stops and small towns nestled beside the highway, with the mountains towering on either side of them.  The whole time, the older woman teased her relentlessly.

Vi wouldn’t have had it any other way.  Caitlyn had a way of pulling her out of her own head, of getting her to fall into an easy rhythm that always existed between them. 

She pulled into a small trailhead parking lot at the far end of a dead-end road, swallowed by a canopy of trees deep in the mountain forests just north of Harrisonburg.  There wasn’t a single other vehicle parked in the lot.

Caitlyn raised a brow, that smirk she’d been wearing the entire time still plastered on her lips, “Is this the part where you drag me into the woods to kill me?”

Vi choked on air and let out a string of coughs that wracked through her lungs, “Cait!”

“What?” The older woman chuckled, “You take me to an empty parking lot an hour away from home…this is exactly how every true crime documentary starts.” she shrugged, letting the joke play out just a little longer.

Vi stared at her in disbelief, “You’re not actually worried that…Caitlyn, I would never—”

The older woman covered her face with her hand, “Oh my god.”

She’d been teasing Vi the entire drive, and the younger woman had fallen for every single joke, “I’ve been tormenting you this entire time.” She realized.

Vi barked out a laugh, relief washing over her, because Caitlyn wasn’t actually uncomfortable, she was just being a fucking menace.

“Yeah, you kinda have.” Vi replied.

“I’m sorry,” Caitlyn replied with a slight frown.

That only seemed to amuse Vi more.  She leaned back against her seat and smiled warmly, “It’s okay. I need someone to remind me to lighten up sometimes.”

The sincerity of it made Caitlyn’s chest tighten, but before she could dwell on it any longer, Vi opened her door and hopped out into the fading sunshine.

“Come on, Miss Kiramman,” Vi said with a smirk.

Caitlyn followed her from the car toward the treeline.  The trailhead sat at the edge of the forest, framed by dense summer greenery.  A wooden sign stood beside the dirt path, marking its name, Fridley Gap Trailhead.

The surrounding woods were quiet except for the distant rush of moving water.  Caitlyn adjusted the straps of the backpack she’d brought and eyed the trail disappearing into the trees, “Should I have prepared for a proper hike?”

Vi glanced back over her shoulder, “Nah, it’s a short walk.”

Caitlyn looked unconvinced, “The sign back there said otherwise.” she noted.  The sign in question showed a map of the Fridley Gap Trail, a 6-mile loop of trails that wound through the mountain pass and back.

“True,” Vi acknowledged, “But we aren’t doing the whole loop.”

Shade seemed to swallow them like the bowels of a monster and the temperature dropped, the sun unable to penetrate the dense canopy above them.  Silence settled between them, with only the sound of the dirt crunching beneath their shoes and the trickle of water.

Caitlyn found herself watching the way what little sunlight reached them caught the strands of pink hair that peeked beneath Vi’s hat.  And then kicked herself for letting these feelings take root within her.  Vi was her friend, the best friend she’d made in years.  She would do anything to protect what they had now, and that meant shoving the thoughts of more somewhere deep in the trenches of her mind.  Instead, she forced her attention toward the steep ravine that dropped off drastically to her right, where the creek trickled softly over sediment.

A few feet ahead, Vi was engaged in a similarly hopeless battle.  Every few minutes, she found herself glancing sideways toward the older woman and the way that tiny smile always seemed to appear whenever Caitlyn thought nobody was looking.

It made Vi’s chest ache, a feeling she promptly ignored.

The footpath grew rockier closer to the stream, weaving through clusters of boulders beside flowing water.  Over the next switchback, Mountain Run widened into a crystal-clear pool hidden in dense forest.  A small waterfall flowed into the basin, sending ripples of dim sunlight across the surface.

There weren’t any other hikers here—a fact that Vi was increasingly grateful for.  She knew the Mountain Run Swimming Hole could get crowded in the summer, but she’d lived in Shenandoah Valley long enough to know when the off-hours were. 

Caitlyn released a quiet breath, “This is…” her voice trailed away.  She couldn’t imagine a more serene spot to learn how to be comfortable in the water again.  The pool was shallow, no deeper than two or three feet at its deepest spots.  She still didn’t want to think about stepping into the water, but the idea that she wasn’t doing this alone made the idea of it easier.

Vi’s voice had gone soft when she replied, “Beautiful.”

Caitlyn slowly turned toward her, wearing a smile so small and real and entirely unguarded.

“Do you come out here often?” The older woman asked with a tilt of her head.

Vi laughed, completely unabashed, “Why, Miss Kiramman,” she replied teasingly, “Are you flirting with me?”

Caitlyn rolled her eyes, letting out a shy giggle, “I deserved that.”

“I had to get you back at least once,” the younger woman replied, a goofy grin on her face.  Vi took a few steps closer to the water, finding a flat rock that perched at the edge of the basin and sat down on it, her legs dangling above the water.

Slowly, watching each step she took on the slippery rocks so that she didn’t inadvertently fall in, Caitlyn followed, lowering herself next to Vi with a quiet sort of anxiety that only got worse the closer she got to the edge.

Vi didn’t say anything yet, just let the older woman get acquainted with even being near the water.  But she kept a close eye on her, shifting closer to Caitlyn so that, if anything, she could catch her before she slipped in. 

Caitlyn had gone still, her knees drawn to her chest and her arms wrapped around them, making herself as small as possible.  Her blue eyes stared at the water, seemingly lost in thought, or perhaps in traumatic memories, Vi couldn’t be sure.

So, instead of putting any pressure on the older woman, she cleared her throat, “How did it go today?”

Caitlyn blinked and turned, finding Vi looking at her with the most patient expression she’d ever seen.  She exhaled, letting go of the anxiety that she’d kept bottled up in her throat, “Great, actually. I was a bit nervous about falling again but being able to teach people about something I’m so passionate about felt…good.”

Vi nodded, understanding the exact feeling Caitlyn was talking about, “Yeah, and then they get that look in their eyes when it clicks for them that they’ve finally found the thing.  The thing they know is going to change the course of their life.  And you get the knowledge that you helped them discover something about themselves. It’s so rewarding.”

Cerulean eyes flashed with something unnamed, and her soft lips parted slightly.  Caitlyn was looking at her with such a deeply focused expression that it felt like Vi had a spotlight shining right above her.  The tips of her ears went hot again, something she realized happened a lot in the older woman’s presence.

Eventually, Caitlyn blinked, pushing away whatever it was that she had been thinking about, “I—I think I’m ready.” She whispered.

“Okay,” Vi kept her voice light and easygoing, presenting herself as a source of support for the woman to lean on.  She stood, wiping the dirt from her palms on her pants before offering an outstretched hand, “Come with me.”

Caitlyn faltered, staring blankly at Vi’s hand until her own, timid, shaky hand accepted the gesture, letting those strong arms pull her up to her feet. 

Vi dropped her hand and tugged off her shirt, and Caitlyn’s mouth went dry on instinct.  She’d been well aware of the younger woman’s strength ever since the night that Vi had carried her from the camry into the LFA building, but nothing could’ve prepared her for the hard lines of her abs or the visible ripple of her back muscles. 

Nor could she have fully imagined the extent of just how much of her skin was covered in tattoos.  She’d seen the gear on Vi’s neck, peeking out from the collars of her shirts, and the sleeves of ink that trailed down her arms.  But her entire back?  Caitlyn felt incredibly buzzed at the sight of it.

However, it was neither of these things that truly stole the breath from the older woman’s lungs in that moment.  No, what truly made Caitlyn squeal internally was the sight of Vi’s top surgery scars on her chest.  Her incredibly flat, muscular chest.

Oh, she must have died and gone to heaven.  Surely this woman standing in front of her could not be real.

“Cait?” Vi chuckled when she noticed the older woman standing there with her mouth agape.  Normally, she would’ve felt insecure to show off her chest to someone she didn’t know that well, but something about Caitlyn’s expression told her that the woman wasn’t judging her.  Far from it.

Caitlyn blinked, “S-sorry. I just…”

“It’s okay,” Vi shrugged, “This doesn’t bother you, does it?” she asked, gesturing toward the scars.

“No!” The older woman exclaimed urgently and then realized she’d practically yelled at the top of her lungs and flushed red hot, “No,” she repeated, calmer this time, but no less firm. “You just look so…fit.”

Vi released another laugh, murmuring a timid ‘thanks’ as she took off her hat and ran a hand through her flattened hair, fluffing it up a bit.

Caitlyn swallowed, rolling her bottom lip between her teeth before she slowly tugged at the hem of her crewneck, pulling it up and over her head.  Underneath, she wore a simple, navy-blue halter bikini top.  Her toned stomach was scattered with constellations of cute little moles that Vi suddenly craved to touch. 

The younger woman’s hands fidgeted at her sides, her grey eyes unable to keep from staring as Caitlyn slid her shorts down her long, porcelain legs.  The bruise on her hip was faded now, a faint black and yellow blooming against her skin.  Her knee was scabbed over and healed, and thankfully had avoided infection.

Warmth spread in Vi’s chest.  The older woman’s muscle-tone was defined against her lithely limbs, her biceps flexing slightly as Caitlyn folded her clothes and placed them on a nearby stump.

When their eyes met again, uncertainty laced Caitlyn’s features again, and Vi felt even more determined to ensure the woman felt comfortable.

“Would you like me to go first?” She asked, in case seeing Vi getting in the water first would ease the older woman’s mind.

Caitlyn was unable to speak, the nerves getting the best of her, so instead she nodded.  Her cerulean eyes tracked Vi’s movements.  The younger woman made her way to the water’s edge, where it was so clear they could see the bottom of the basin.

Vi dipped a toe in, shivering slightly as the cold water touched her skin.  Despite the early-summer warmth, this water was still glacially cold since the tree canopy didn’t let much sunlight through.  With a deep breath, she stepped further into the natural pool, sending soft ripples outward until she was knee deep in the shallowest end of it.

She turned, finding Caitlyn watching her with such a despairingly forlorn expression that it made her stomach drop.  Vi frowned and walked close to the edge, where the older woman had once again perched on the flat rock and pulled her knees to her chest.

“It’s not deep here,” Vi noted, her palms finding the cold surface of the rock as she leaned forward. “Do you at least want to try dipping your feet in from there?”

With a shaky breath, the older woman unfolded her legs, letting the backs of her thighs rest against the surface of the rock, while hesitantly letting the lower half of her legs dangle over the edge.

“I’ve got you,” Vi whispered, her callused hands gently gripping Caitlyn’s muscular calves, guiding her feet to the water’s surface. “Are you ready?” she asked, pausing before a single toe touched the water.

Finally, the older woman seemed to push through the anxiety that had a grip on her vocal cords, “I trust you, Violet.” She croaked hoarsely.

That sparked a fire within Vi’s chest, a feeling that was getting harder and harder to push down.  She glanced down, avoiding that cerulean gaze, and nodded.  Slowly, her hands guided Caitlyn’s foot to the water, just the tips of her toes breaking the surface tension. 

Caitlyn gasped, a small and shaky thing, like her lungs couldn’t fully inflate.  It wasn’t the fear that flooded her at that moment, it was the cold.

She hadn’t expected the water to feel like ice against her skin.

“It’s freezing!” Caitlyn exclaimed, the breath she’d inhaled released in a shaky laugh.

Vi chuckled, relieved that the older woman hadn’t fallen into a panic attack like she’d half-expected, “Other than that, how does it feel?”

A soft giggle came from somewhere above her, and when Vi looked up, she found Caitlyn smirking down at her. 

“It feels…wet,” she joked, quirking her brow just slightly.

It caught Vi off-guard.  Not that she didn’t think the older woman was capable of a salacious joke, but that it came as she was in the middle of facing her fear.  It only made Vi admire her more.  Instead of commenting on it, she played into it.

She feigned shock, “Miss Kiramman!”

Caitlyn only shrugged, her lips twitching in amusement.  She lowered her other foot until it too sunk below the water’s surface.  Absently, her legs started kicking back and forth, which accidentally splashed Vi right in the face.

“Oh! I’m so sorry, Darling,” she covered her mouth, trying to pretend like she wasn’t laughing at the younger woman, who only looked at her with a deadpan expression.

“I’m definitely getting you back for that,” Vi retorted, stepping back up close to the ledge that Caitlyn was sitting on.  She leaned in, their faces only a few inches apart now, close enough that Vi could smell the vanilla on the older woman’s neck.

“Don’t you dare pull me in, Violet.” Caitlyn warned, her breathing gone ragged.  She instinctively leaned closer, their noses just a hair’s breadth away from touching.

Vi shook her head, “I told you, you can trust me.” she whispered.

“I know,” Caitlyn swallowed, their breaths syncing as the rest of the woods around them seemed to fade away.  All she could focus on was the sincerity within those grey eyes, and she found that she could face anything with Vi by her side. “Violet…” she swallowed the tension that’d lined the inside of her throat, “Will you carry me?”

The younger woman nodded, a soft grin on her lips, “Whatever you want, Cait.”

Caitlyn wrapped her long arms around Vi’s neck, letting her palms roam over the ridges of her muscled shoulders briefly before she felt the younger woman’s callused hands settle on the dips of her hips.

“I’ll count to three and then I’ll lift you up, okay? Just…” Vi cleared her throat, “just lock your legs around me.”

The older woman sensed the shift in her and the way her cheeks flushed pink at the thought of what she was asking. “Okay,” Caitlyn replied, “I’m ready.”

“One, two…” Vi began counting down, and Caitlyn felt the muscles of her tattooed back flex beneath her palms, “three.”

Before Caitlyn could even blink, she was hoisted from the rock and hovering above the water, with Vi’s hands shifting slightly, holding her thighs up as her long legs came around the younger woman’s waist, her ankles hooking together as if they’d done this a thousand times before.

She looked down at Vi, breathless with the feeling of their bare skin touching.  Her feet were still dipped in the water, and it didn’t even bother her. 

They stayed like that for a while, with Vi’s steady arms holding her above the water, pacing slowly through the pool and sending ripples across the surface.  They talked about their days, losing themselves in their surroundings and easy conversation.  It was their own little oasis, with the trees above sheltering them from the world outside and the birds singing like their own personal choir.

Vi was looking up at her with adoring eyes, so wide and open for her that she could’ve melted into her then.  But Caitlyn couldn’t.  She shouldn’t.

All of her worries and insecurities began to seep back in, like water rushing through a burst dam.  It flooded her senses until her hands shook and her lips trembled with the force of it.  She closed her eyes so she couldn’t watch Vi’s smile fade into a frown.  Caitlyn couldn’t bear to see it.

“Cait?” The younger woman’s worried voice reverberated against her chest, and she couldn’t help the tears that leaked down her cheeks.

She felt Vi move, felt the way the slick rock felt beneath her as she was placed back down on it, and the way those callused hands brushed a strand of blue behind her ear.  Still, she didn’t open her eyes.  Caitlyn didn’t want Vi to see the guilt swimming in them.

The sound of water sloshing echoed through the trees as she felt Vi’s presence shift, the younger woman clearly exited the basin, and her wet footsteps came squelching closer.  A warm hand landed on her shoulder, followed by the younger woman’s tender voice:

“Hey, come on, let’s get you home. You’ve done enough for today.”

Maybe it was easier to let Vi think that her reaction had been due to her fear of the water, rather than the fear of the feelings that were stubbornly taking root within her.  She could let the younger woman think it had nothing to do with her.  Maybe Caitlyn could hold Vi at arm’s length without making her suspicious.

It was for the best.  Caitlyn couldn’t afford to lose her composure right now.  She had a new job to worry about, a new home to start searching for, and a past that she needed to forget.  And she respected Vi too much to allow her feelings for the younger woman to become just a distraction.

They were friends.  Good friends.  That had to be enough for her.
