Chapter 8: That's the thing, It Lingers

Summary:

Caitlyn holds Vi at arm's length, but when a storm rolls through the valley, will she come running?

Notes:

Dual-chapter update, say what?!

Originally, chapter 8 ended up being like 15k words long, so I decided to split it up between chapter 8 and 9. But I didn't want y'all to have to wait a whole extra week for part of the story that should've been included in chapter 8 anyways, so you get both chapters at once!

Enjoy <3

(See the end of the chapter for more notes.)

Chapter Text

June – 1 week until Independence Day

As the weeks passed them by, Caitlyn and Vi still met up more often than not, whether it was down by the riverside, making slow progress in the water, or spending their evenings sitting on the hood of Jayce’s camry, staring at the stars and talking about the stupid shit that crossed their minds.

Despite spending so much time together, Vi felt something shift between them ever since that day at the swimming hole.  It was subtle, like the slow shift of the tide in the afternoon.  But it was enough for her to recognize that Caitlyn had built her walls up again.

There wasn’t anything specific that the older woman said, nor did she avoid seeing Vi in general.  It was simply the way her gaze no longer lingered, or the way her hands no longer found their way to Vi’s arms. 

And the worst of it all was the fact that Caitlyn had simply stopped calling her Violet.

While the older woman certainly didn’t owe her anything, Vi couldn’t help but feel like she’d done something wrong, and it only got worse as the days dragged on.

Her frustration was seeping into her professional life, too.  Something she never, ever, let happen before.  When she was out on the water, her guests relied on her to get downstream safely.  It was her job to create an experience for them that was memorable.  That was fun.

And while this latest guided trip was exactly that, she could feel that her own energy just wasn’t up to her normal levels.  She put on a smile, she laughed at the exact moments she was supposed to laugh, even cracked jokes as a standing wave crashed over the raft.

But her mind lingered on a certain midnight-haired woman.

Even now, with river-matted hair and a still-damp wetsuit, her phone pressed to her ear and Vander’s voice going in one ear and out the other, all Vi could think about was Caitlyn.

“Vi? Are you listening to me?”

She blinked, “S-sorry, I got distracted.”

Vander sighed through the speaker, “I need you to focus, kiddo. This is important.”

Right.  Her adoptive father was calling with some sort of update about whatever the fuck he’d been working on in New York for the last month.

“Yeah, I’m listening.”

Seemingly satisfied with her response, Vander continued, “So, as I was saying, I’ve got some more things to iron out up here, but I should be able to come home in about two weeks or so. Just depends on the judge at this point.”

That caught her attention, “Judge? What the fuck is going on, dad?”

“Oh, right.” He huffed, realizing he hadn’t exactly explained anything, “Listen, it’s nothing to worry about until this is all through. I’ll tell you everything when I come home.”

“You can’t just leave me hanging like this!” She exclaimed, slamming a hand on the table in the staff kitchen of LFA. “Why can’t you just tell me what’s going on? Don’t you trust me?”

Honestly, Vi was just sick of everyone keeping her in the dark.  Between Vander’s secretive bullshit and whatever was going on with Caitlyn, she was about ready to break.

“I’m trying to do what’s best for us, Vi, and there’s no point in causing you any undue stress unless it’s absolutely necessary.” His voice was laced with a condescension that she didn’t care for.

She was an adult.  What was best for her and for Powder wasn’t solely his decision anymore.

“I’m already stressed, dad!” Vi was yelling now, her pulse buzzing beneath her skin like a livewire, “I’ve been running the business on my own for a month, keeping this shit on lock, just waiting for you to come back. And then you call and don’t give any explanation for what you’re doing, and I’m just supposed to sit here and accept that?” Her chest heaved and her heart pounded in her chest.

“I’m not doing this right now with you, Vi. I’ve kept this business running while raising you and your sister by myself for years. I think you can handle an extra couple of weeks.”

The last thing she needed right now was a reminder of her dead parents, nor the sacrifices that her adoptive father had made for them as kids, or the way he sometimes held that over their heads.  Vi had grown to resent him any time he brought it up.

It’s not like she and Powder chose to be orphans.  Nor did they ask him to keep their family business running to honor their parents. 

This conversation was leading them nowhere. 

“Whatever,” Vi murmured, hanging up before Vander could get another word out.

She stared at the phone screen, her blood simmering and mind reeling.  Powder was out with Ekko somewhere, the two of them still spending every waking moment together until the grad program letters started arriving within the next week.

Yeah, as if Vi wasn’t already dealing with enough, she was also mentally preparing herself for the possibility that her sister would be moving within a month.  While Powder had applied to in-state programs, she knew that if she got into Colorado, her mind would already be made up.

Deciding not to linger at the warehouse any longer, she grabbed her bag, locked up, and drove home.

The bungalow greeted her with a sort of comfort that could only come from home.  It was the one thing that Vi could rely on to shelter her from everything else in her life.  A roof over her head and a space to breathe in, without the overwhelming, crushing pressure of responsibilities.

She didn’t even bother turning on any lights, just threw her bag down by the front door and ambled up the stairs with the sort of weighted steps that could only come from someone who was using every last bit of energy they had left to climb them.

Vi tossed her now dead phone on the mattress, too tired to plug it into the charger before turning to the bathroom, where she turned on the shower to its hottest setting and stepped in only when the steam filled the air, thick enough to hide her own reflection in the mirror.

The living room settled into a comfortable quiet that only existed between people who had known each other long enough to coexist without needing to fill every second with conversation.

The television cast flickering blue light across the cabin as an episode of The Clone Wars played in the background.  Lightsabers clashed somewhere on screen while Captain Rex barked orders at a squad of clone troopers trying to breach through the chaotic lines of the Geonosians.

Neither Caitlyn nor Jayce were paying much attention.

Caitlyn sat curled into one corner of the couch beneath a green and red throw blanket, scrolling through local home listings on a tablet balanced against her knees.  Every few seconds she would tap a photograph and make a face.

“No, absolutely not,” she muttered.

Jayce, seated beside her, hummed distractedly while thumbing through his phone.  The lingering scent of popcorn hovered in the air, the bowl sitting half-empty on the coffee table.

“This listing claims it’s a three-bedroom,” Caitlyn continued, “but the third bedroom is clearly just a converted storage closet. I mean, there’s not even a single window. How is this even legal without a second egress?”

Her best friend only shrugged.

“And the realtor keeps misspelling words in the descriptions.” She added, clicking her tongue in annoyance.

Caitlyn’s mother had called a couple of weeks ago with a recommendation for some top-notch realtor to help her with her house search, but she’d rejected Cassandra’s help out of pure unyielding pigheadedness.

She’d hired her own realtor instead, and in that time, had failed to find even one listing she thought worthy enough to tour, let alone buy.

“Mhm.” Jayce hummed in response.

Caitlyn continued to ramble, “Also, I genuinely think she might be completely inept.”

That finally earned a reaction.  Jayce snorted, briefly looking up from his phone to glance at her tablet.

Caitlyn pointed at the screen, “I’m serious! She called the kitchen an ‘open-consept masterpiece.’ With an S.”

He grimaced as he skimmed the E-mail thread.  Yeah, she was probably incompetent, or at the very least, a terrible speller.  Either way, it didn’t bode well for his best friend’s house search.

“Maybe it was a typo,” he replied, turning his attention back to his phone.

She scoffed, “It was not a typo, Jayce. She spelled it that way four separate times.”

Jayce chuckled, “What do you even need three bedrooms for, anyways? Why don’t you look for something smaller?”

She ignored him, muttering something about needing space for a weight room and a separate study.  Caitlyn clicked to the next listing, frowning at a hideously decorated living room that made it impossible to imagine any potential.

Minutes ticked on like this, until Jayce released another laugh, but this time, it wasn’t at something she’d said.  She slowly turned her head toward him, finding her best friend grinning widely at his phone screen with an expression that could only mean he was actively texting someone.

“What are you laughing at?” She asked, leaning over far enough to try to catch a glimpse at his screen.

Jayce, however, was quicker than she was.  He turned the screen in the opposite direction and shook his head, “Nothing!”

Caitlyn sat up straighter, eyes narrowing at her best friend as her tablet went abandoned in her lap, “Jayce Talis.”

His shoulders visibly tensed, enough to confirm her suspicions.  He was definitely talking to someone and didn’t want her to know about it.

“Oh my Gods!” She exclaimed, “You’re hiding something!”

Jayce groaned, “Am not!”

“You absolutely are! Show me,” she pushed to her knees, the blanket sliding off her shoulders and draping halfway onto the floor as she crawled over couch pillows to try to snatch his phone, “Show me!”

“No,” Jayce replied.

They were arguing like siblings, something that Cassandra would certainly chastise them for if she were here.

Caitlyn wasn’t taking no for an answer, “Show. Me.”

“Why!”

She quirked a brow, “Because. We’ve been best friends for years and you just made the face.”

His hazel eyes grew wide, “What face?”

“The Viktor face.”

“I do not have a Viktor face,” he lied, subconsciously rubbing his nose.

Caitlyn scoffed, “You absolutely have a Viktor face.”

He dragged both hands over his face, acquiescing to the fact that he wasn’t going to win this battle.

“Don’t freak out,” he sighed heavily, turning the phone around.  The text thread was already open, and he watched with a bated breath as Caitlyn’s eyes scanned the messages.

Her expression flattened, “Oh, you’ve got to be kidding me.”

Gone were the clipped responses and mutual irritation that had followed the breakup months ago.  Instead, the thread was full of typical flirting and gay posturing—dozens of shirtless photos and sexual innuendos were traded back and forth.  Caitlyn rolled her eyes.

“Cait—”

She shook her head, “He broke your heart, Jayce.”

Jayce’s smile faded slightly, eyes falling back down to the phone screen.  He shrugged, “I know.”

Caitlyn gave him an unimpressed look, “So then why do you think this time will be any different?”

“He’s different this time.”

The moment the words left his mouth, Caitlyn groaned loudly and buried her face in a couch pillow, “You cannot be serious. That’s literally what everyone says.”

“Because sometimes it’s true!” He argued, throwing his hands up in the air.

She huffed, “Sometimes people also approach a bear on the trail, that doesn’t make it a good idea!”

Jayce laughed, “Look who’s talking!”

Caught off guard, she sat back on her calves, her voice small, “What is that supposed to mean?”

Now it was his turn to smirk, “You’ve been acting weird for weeks, Sprout. You think I haven’t noticed the way you’ve started to shut everyone out again? I know you better than that.”

Caitlyn rolled her eyes dramatically, “I don’t know what you’re talking about.”

Jayce studied her for a moment, then asked the question that’d clearly been on the tip of his tongue, “What happened between you and Vi?”

Her casual mask slipped, her lips turning into a frown that he noticed despite the way she turned away from him, determinedly staring at the television, “Nothing at all.”

It was the truth; nothing happened between she and Vi.  But that was exactly the problem.  Nothing happened between them because Caitlyn hadn’t let it.  And now things between them had become distant.  Because she built up her walls again, protecting herself from something that’d probably save her.  Something she was sure she didn’t deserve.

Jayce’s brow furrowed, feeling that slight guilt at having pushed her so hard when it came to Vi, “Have you heard from her?”

She shook her head, “No, I haven’t.” she admitted.

“When did you text her?”

Caitlyn sighed, picking at the pilled threads of the throw blanket, brows set in a deep frown, “Three hours ago.”

Jayce tilted his head, “About?”

“The forecast is calling for severe thunderstorms and flash flooding.” She whispered.  Outside the cabin windows, the sky had already darkened into a deep, charcoal grey.  Wind rattled the tree branches beyond the glass. “I just wanted to make sure she knew.”

His expression softened, “And she didn’t answer?”

She shook her head, “No.”

That was the troubling part.  Vi always answered, even if she was busy.  If she had a rafting trip, she always texted Caitlyn before launching and again when she returned.  She convinced herself it was a safety thing, but the older woman knew better.

She glanced down at her phone, the message still sat there, the word ‘delivered’ haunting her from beneath it, “Hasn’t even read it.”

Jayce frowned at that, because they both knew how strange that was.  One thing they both knew about the younger woman was that she was an excellent communicator.  Ignoring a text for three hours wasn’t normal.

A distant rumble rolled across the mountains, catching their attention.  The sky flashed faintly beyond the trees, causing Caitlyn’s stomach to twist with concern.

Minutes passed in heavy silence before she finally pushed herself off the couch.

“Where are you going?” Jayce asked with pinched brows.

Caitlyn pocketed her phone and slipped on the same tie-dye crocs she’d worn the day she left New York, “To check on her.”

“Cait—” He was cut off by her stern expression, the one that meant her mind was already made up.

Another low growl of thunder echoed through the woods outside, and Caitlyn grabbed her keys from the bowl near the door. “I won’t be able to get to her once those storms move in.”

Jayce sighed, concern lingering in his expression despite knowing that Caitlyn was doing the right thing, “Just text me when you get there.”

She nodded, and a bolt of lightning illuminated the living room for the briefest moment.  She reached for the doorknob, then stepped outside into the gathering storm, unable to sit still another second while wondering whether Vi was alright.

Vi didn’t remember falling asleep.

One moment she had been staring at the ceiling, watching the last traces of the evening fade from the corners of her room, and the next she was opening her eyes to a darkness so complete that for several bewildering seconds she thought she had gone blind.

The room was silent in that peculiar way nights in the valley sometimes were—a silence that wasn’t truly silent at all.  There had always been an unwritten rule out here in the Appalachian Mountains, where the locals knew that if they heard the woods calling their name, no they didn’t.  Vi wasn’t fazed by the sound of the walls creaking or the wind whispering against the siding outside.  She’d learned to ignore it.

She lay still, her thoughts sluggish and distant, disoriented by the abrupt transition from unconsciousness to wakefulness.

The sky outside her window was black.  She dragged a hand across her face and reached toward the nightstand, patting around for her phone, wondering what time it was.

The screen remained dark when she pressed the power button, her faint reflection in the glass staring back at her.

“Great,” she muttered under her breath.  Her phone was dead.

She released a weary sigh and pushed herself upright, trying to shake off the lingering fog in her head.

That was when the world turned white, a flash of lightning erupting beyond the window, so bright that it illuminated every corner of the room.  Everything appeared all at once and subsequently vanished in the blink of an eye.

The lightning was followed by a violent crack, splitting the air with such force that Vi’s body reacted before her mind could catch up.  She launched herself off the bed, stumbling onto the floor as her heart seized in her chest.  She curled instinctively against the side of the mattress; hands clasped over her ears.

Vi squeezed her eyes shut.  The storm growled overhead, and every distant roll of thunder seemed to rattle her bones.  In an instant, she wasn’t twenty-eight anymore, she was twelve, watching rain slam against a window, waiting for headlights that would never come.

A sudden pounding at the front door shattered the memory.  Three heavy knocks, like cannon balls crashing against the wood.

She whimpered, her heart hammering in her chest.  Another burst of wind rattled the bungalow, the walls groaning against the growing gusts.  It felt like the cacophony of noise was going to swallow her whole, the darkness of night closing in on her.

Another round of knocks rang out, louder this time.  Someone was at her front door and her phone was dead—she had no way of knowing who it was, whether it was Powder coming to check on her or some nefarious entity that the Appalachian superstitions always warned about.

Deciding to test her luck, Vi pushed herself up on unsteady feet, padding down the stairs with unease settling in her stomach.

She reached the bottom stairs just as another sequence of knocks thudded against the door and a voice called out above the din of the storm.

“Vi, open the door!”

Caitlyn?

What was she doing here, at this hour, in weather like this?

The pounding resumed, and somehow that finally propelled her into motion.  By the time she reached the front door, her hands were shaking badly enough that she fumbled with the lock twice before it finally gave way.

The door swung open with the force of the wind, rain flooding into the entryway like a tsunami, and there was Caitlyn, drenched from head to toe.  Rainwater streamed from her frizzy hair, soaking through the soft fabric of her sweatshirt.  Her chest heaved with deep breaths as if she’d run the entire way there.  The porch light cast a pale orange across her soaked skin.

Relief swept across Caitlyn’s face with such naked intensity that it made something inside Vi ache.

“There you are,” the older woman released a breath, relieved that Vi had answered the door.  She’d half expected to find the bungalow empty.

Before Vi could respond, Caitlyn threw her arms around her, burying her face in the crook of Vi’s neck and inhaling the familiar, comforting scent of the younger woman’s cedar shampoo. 

Cold droplets of rain dripped from midnight hair and onto Vi’s neck, mixing with the warmth of the older woman’s breath against her skin.  The embrace hit with enough force that Vi staggered backwards, though somehow, she kept them upright. 

Another flash of lightning split the sky, the thunder rumbling loudly soon thereafter, a sign that the storm was quickly approaching, no longer lingering on the distant edges of the valley.

Vi flinched so violently that Caitlyn felt it in the way her muscles tensed within her embrace.  She pulled back, concern flickering across her features just before another tremor passed through Vi’s frame.

Without thinking, Vi grabbed Caitlyn’s wrist and practically dragged her inside.  She slammed the door shut, locked it, and backed away from it as another thunderclap shook the windows.  She retreated further into the house, like she couldn’t get far enough away, her face twisted in raw, overwhelming terror.

Caitlyn felt her stomach sink at the sight of it.  It was unsettling to watch someone that was usually so confident and sure of themselves succumb to a crippling fear. 

Slowly, she followed Vi into the living room, where she freed herself from the damp crewneck, tossing it to the floor somewhere.  The room glowed with warm amber light from a nearby lamp, but the storm outside kept forcing itself into the space, white lightning flashing through curtains, shadows shifting across the walls.

“Vi, look at me,” she pleaded, hovering at the edge of the room, not wanting to corner the younger woman when she already looked like a kicked puppy.

Vi wrapped her arms around herself and paced a circle around the coffee table, flinching when thunder cracked overhead again.

Caitlyn lowered her voice, gently coaxing the younger woman, “Please, you can do it. Just look at me.”

Grey eyes finally found cerulean, and Caitlyn’s heart broke a little.  There was genuine panic swimming inside them.

“I can’t breathe,” Vi croaked as she started to hyperventilate.

Caitlyn stepped closer, keeping her eyes locked on Vi’s, since it seemed that was the only thing keeping the younger woman above water.  Careful not to overwhelm her, she placed a hand on her shoulder, hoping the warmth of her palm against her skin would help ground her.

“Take a breath,” she instructed, taking a deep breath of her own to remind Vi how easy it was—sometimes panic makes it hard for your brain to perform the simplest of bodily processes.  Breathing being one of them.

Vi shook her head, “I can’t.”

She sounded so small, so desperate, like she was drowning in her own anxiety and couldn’t find her way out of it.  Caitlyn tried not to dwell on what would’ve happened if she hadn’t come to check on her.

“Yes, you can,” she replied, taking another visible breath, “With me.”

Slowly, painfully, Vi matched her.  Caitlyn guided her through each breath until the terrible pressure crushing her ribs began to loosen.  The world stopped spinning quite so fast, and the walls that’d threatened to suffocate her retreated. 

Keeping the younger woman close, Caitlyn led her to the couch, where Vi lay her head in her lap, letting the warmth of her seep into her cheek.  The older woman brushed slender hands through her wild pink hair, absently massaging her scalp as the silence droned on between them.

The rain continued to drum against the roof and the thunder rumbled in uneven intervals.  Vi stared at the folds of Caitlyn’s t-shirt, quietly picking the lint from the fabric as the older woman breathed steadily beneath her.

“My parents died in a storm,” Vi’s voice came what felt like an hour later, low and gravelly in the silence of the room.  Her words hit Caitlyn in the gut, making her inhale a breath at the unexpected confession.

Her fingers stilled their movements in soft hair, if only for a moment while she collected her thoughts, and then continued their ministrations.  Caitlyn didn’t say anything, just sat in the quiet and allowed Vi to work through everything that’d been weighing on her.

“It wasn’t in the forecast,” Vi’s voice cracked, “it was supposed to be one of those days where the sky was so blue that you almost wondered if it was even real.”

Caitlyn felt a pang in her chest at that, biting her cheek at the thought of a young Vi’s life being turned upside down without so much as a warning.

“There was a rafting group still on the river when the storm rolled in. Apparently, the raft got wrapped around a strainer…” her voice trailed off, and Caitlyn felt Vi take a deep breath as the memory unfolded piece by heartbreaking piece.

“Vi,” the older woman whispered, “what’s a strainer?” she asked, unsure if she should’ve interrupted or not, but she genuinely wanted to understand Vi’s trauma and in order to do that, she needed to know what she was talking about.

The younger woman hummed, the sound reverberating against the skin of her thigh, “It’s usually a fallen tree that sticks out over the water, it sucks the current beneath it, but if a person or a raft gets caught in it, the force of the current against the log holds them in place and they drown.” Vi replied, sucking in a breath.

Caitlyn’s heart sank, “Oh.”

Lightning flashed again, but this time Vi didn’t budge.  It seemed that, at least, the older woman’s presence was doing some good.  She tried not to think about that too deeply.  Especially not with the distance that’d grown between them the past few weeks.

“My parents were the reason I got my Swiftwater rescue certification. I wanted to be like them.” Vi admitted, so quietly that Caitlyn barely caught it. 

The older woman shifted slightly, running her free hand down to caress the length of Vi’s arm, rubbing lazy circles against tattooed skin.

Vi felt herself melt slightly under the touch and let herself get lost in the comfort of it for a long while, until eventually she decided to continue telling Caitlyn the story.

“Mom and dad were trying to help, and they did save most of them.” Vi’s eyes glistened, “But a flash flood came racing downriver and swept them away before anyone could even blink.”

Caitlyn’s hand found hers, offering something warm and steady for the younger woman to hold onto.  An anchor in a storm.

Vi looked down at their joined fingers, squeezing them slightly in acknowledgement of Caitlyn’s offer of support, “Now, anytime it storms, part of me still feels twelve.”

She felt the older woman shift closer, and she dared to look up, finding those cerulean eyes looking down at her with an expression so soft and open that it made Vi’s stomach do a flip.  Tears slipped from the corners of her eyes as a gentle thumb brushed across her knuckles.

“You’ve been carrying this alone for far too long,” Caitlyn muttered, wishing there was something she could do to go back in time and take the pain away.

Vi snuggled in closer before she could even second-guess herself, the tip of her nose pressing against the soft skin of Caitlyn’s stomach, where the hem of her shirt had bunched up from sitting so long. 

The older woman didn’t push her away, either.  Caitlyn just wrapped an arm around her shoulders, holding her tighter and promising never to let go.  A hand rested against the back of Vi’s neck, fingers tangling with the hair at the nape.  The gesture was devastatingly tender.  She was safe in this woman’s arms.

The next morning, Vi woke gradually, each of her senses booting up like the individual components of a computer after a power-cycle.  First, her nose twitched at the scent of vanilla and rainwater filling her nostrils.  Next, the sound of soft snores flitted into her eardrums, almost like a cat purring in her lap.  But Vi didn’t have a cat.  So, she chalked it up to her own breathing pattern and let sleep take her for a few more minutes.  And finally, she felt a warmth tickling the back of her neck in steady intervals.

Blinking awake, one eye opening, and then the other, Vi winced at the bright morning light that seeped in through the blinds.  She was on the edge of her couch, not remembering why she was there.  That is, until she felt a dead weight draped over her waist, and the feeling of a bony knee lodged in the back of her thigh.

Oh.

The memory of the night before came back to her, then.  She’d half thought it had all been a dream.  But Vi found that she much preferred reality to anything her sleeping brain could’ve conjured up.  Because Caitlyn Kiramman was snuggled up behind her, with her slender arms wrapped protectively around her.

It was then, Vi realized, that the warmth tickling the nape of her neck was in fact the older woman’s sleepy breath.

She went stock-still, afraid that if she moved a single muscle, it would wake Caitlyn up.  And that was far from what Vi wanted in that moment.  Call it greed, call it selfishness, but the younger woman simply wanted to revel in her warmth a little while longer, not knowing if things between them would go back to that careful distance whenever Caitlyn woke up.

There’s a feeling you get when you’re laid up in someone else’s arms, listening to the slow rhythm of their breathing.  It’s like the world outside seems to fade into the background, and all the things that plague you with anxiety or fear or unease soften at the edges.  These things are replaced by the simple quiet comfort of knowing you don’t have to carry them alone.

And that’s exactly what Caitlyn offered her—the unwavering support of someone determined to stand by her side.  A friend that, inexplicably, came running to her side even when she hadn’t been asked.

Vi didn’t even know how the older woman knew to come over last night.  It wasn’t something they talked about before they fell asleep in each other’s company.

Behind her, Caitlyn twitched slightly.  The arm draped over her waist shifted, and the breathing at her nape broke its regular pattern.  Vi still didn’t move, just simply waited for the older woman to stir awake.

Caitlyn’s hold on her tightened, and the woman released a contended sigh, nuzzling her face deeper into the base of Vi’s neck.  It made Vi feel hot all over, and as much as she was enjoying it, she wasn’t sure if the older woman would be too pleased to realize she was cuddling her friend in her sleep.

Vi shifted her legs, nothing too drastic, just enough that it would be noticeable.

She heard Caitlyn’s response come a second later, a displeased groan that transitioned into a startled gasp.  Vi felt the moment the older woman realized where she was.

“Fuck,” Caitlyn whispered, not realizing that Vi was already awake.

Vi chuckled, the sound low and warm, “Mmm, sorry Miss Kiramman, you’ll have to take me out to dinner first.”

Caitlyn should’ve shut Vi down right then, even if it had been a joke.  She should’ve pushed her away, not let her burrow herself even further into Caitlyn’s resolve than she already had.  But she was sick and tired of being alone.  Distancing herself from the younger woman had only made her bitter and hollow.

Yes, she’d just gone through a breakup.  But if she were truly honest with herself, things had been over between she and Charlie months, if not years ago.  She never loved her, or at least she only thought she did, because ultimately all Caitlyn wanted was to be seen, to be held.  And Charlie had been that for her, if only briefly.

In reality, their relationship had been an arrangement.  A front they could hide behind.

But with Vi, Caitlyn already knew her feelings were much deeper, the roots digging in, and would continue festering until she finally acknowledged them.

She decided it was time to make the leap.

Caitlyn exhaled a giggle, the arm that had been draped over Vi’s waist slid up to her shoulder, then down the length of a strong, tattooed arm, slotting her hand within a callused one, “Violet, if you wanted to ask me to dinner, you could just do so, no need to hide behind a joke.”

Vi’s heart stopped and she inhaled a disbelieving breath.  It had been weeks since Caitlyn had called her by her full name.  The sound of it on the older woman’s tongue made her knees weak. 

She pushed herself up, rolling over and coming face-to-face to, simply put, the most beautiful woman she’d ever seen.  Vi had always found Caitlyn attractive, ever since the moment they met she couldn’t get over just how stunning she was.  But there was something different about morning Caitlyn.  Something raw and unfiltered.

Vi reached a hand up, gently caressing the soft skin of the older woman’s cheek, “Are you pulling my leg or are you serious, Cait?”

Caitlyn let her guard down, swallowing the doubt that’d been plaguing her for weeks.  This, right here, right now, with Vi, was exactly what she wanted.

She reached up, taking Vi’s hand from her cheek and pulling it to her lips, pressing a kiss to rough, scarred knuckles before nodding, “I’m serious, Violet. If you’re up for it, I would love to have dinner with you.” Caitlyn replied breathlessly.

“A date?” Vi asked, still in utter disbelief.

The older woman chuckled, a grin growing wide on her face, showing off her cute tooth-gap, “Yes, darling. A date.”

Vi hummed and nuzzled in close, pressing the tip of her nose to Caitlyn’s neck as she felt the other woman’s chin come to rest on her head.  Long arms squeezed tightly around her, their legs tangling under the soft fabric of the throw blanket.

Caitlyn rubbed slow, absent circles on Vi’s back and stared at the dust motes floating in the heavy beams of sunlight streaming through the window.  Slow mornings like this didn’t exist in New York.  There was never a moment where she could just seek comfort in Charlie’s arms without the excuse of work and obligations getting in the way.

She never realized just how starved she’d been for attention.  How differently would things have worked out if anyone had bothered to give her a single minute of affection?

It didn’t matter, now.  Not when she could feel Vi’s warm breath heating the skin above her clavicle.  Caitlyn had spent too many days trying to fight the way her own body tried to tell her she liked Vi, and now she was determined to make up for it.

Vi broke the silence, her voice muffled against her skin, “Cait?”

“Hmm?” Caitlyn hummed, her fingers tracing the ridges of Vi’s shoulder blades.

“What made you come over last night?” Vi asked, small and unsure.

Caitlyn’s fingers stilled and she leaned back, shifting so that she could see Vi’s face, “You weren’t answering your phone.”

Vi grimaced, “Oh, shit.”

She should probably mention that it was, in fact, still dead.  Though Vi honestly preferred it that way, she’d probably rip the face off anyone that tried to interrupt their morning.

The older woman laughed, “What?”

Grinning, Vi shook her head, “Nothing. I’m just glad you came.”

Caitlyn smiled back and brushed a loose strand of pink away from Vi’s forehead, “Me too.”

And then, because she couldn’t imagine spending a second away from the younger woman today, an idea popped into Caitlyn’s head, “Would you—” she hesitated, pulling her bottom lip between her teeth and mulling the words over for a beat before continuing, “Do you want to come climbing with me today?”

Vi’s grin grew wider than Caitlyn had ever seen it, “You want me to come with you? To work?” she asked, hopeful.

“Yes, Violet.” The older woman giggled, charmed by Vi’s disbelief, “There’s a climbing event happening at Reflector Oven tomorrow, and Jayce wants Maddie and I to set up the belays today and make sure the anchors are set properly.”

The younger woman chuckled, “You’re still mentoring that little twerp?”

Caitlyn tapped Vi’s arm, her jaw dropping in feigned shock as she tried to keep from smiling wildly, “Violet! You can’t call her that.” She chided.

Vi laughed, throwing her hands up in innocence, “What! You said yourself that she’s annoying.”

“Was annoying. She’s quite grown on me, lately.” She shrugged.

“Bet. Let’s go. I’m in the mood to show off,” Vi replied, pushing up off the couch to stretch her arms over her head, feeling the tension in her muscles release.  While she loved waking up next to Caitlyn, the couch was much too small for them both.

Her muscle tank rode up with the motion, revealing that delicious ‘V’ cut of Vi’s lower abs.  Caitlyn’s mouth went dry at the sight of it.  She’d simply never seen a woman so strong in her entire life—the urge to trace those ridges with her tongue was as fierce as it was sudden.

She swallowed, her cerulean eyes raking up Vi’s form until she found those grey eyes shining down at her, a knowing smirk playing on the younger woman’s lips, “Mmm, I like your confidence, dear. Now go get dressed, we’re already running late.”

The drive to Reflector Oven took longer than Caitlyn had intended, considering Vi had convinced her to stop at a roadside stand selling boiled peanuts mid-way there.  She’d scrunched her nose at the sound of it, but when Vi insisted she try one, her face lit up.  The salty juiciness of the peanuts was simply delicious, and the easy smile on the younger woman’s face made the pitstop worth it.

Now, the late-morning sunlight flashed through the windshield as the camry wound along the mountain road, illuminating the granite walls that rose above the trees.  Vi sat in the passenger seat with one arm hanging out the open window, relaxed and completely unconcerned by the fact that they were running late.

Caitlyn, on the other hand, checked the dashboard clock for what felt like the tenth time.

“You worry too much,” Vi commented, turning to look at the woman with a mischievous grin.

Caitlyn huffed, “That is very easy for you to say. You don’t have a senator’s daughter breathing up your ass.”

“I can’t believe Jayce let himself be manipulated into hiring her,” Vi replied, lifting her ballcap and brushing back her pink hair before putting it back on backwards.

The older woman sighed, “Yes, well, there’s nothing to be done about it now.” She replied, then glanced over at Vi briefly, “And you need to be on your best behavior.”

“Me?!” Vi exclaimed, mildly offended, “When have I ever embarrassed you in public, Miss Kiramman?”

Caitlyn groaned as she finally pulled into the parking lot at the trailhead, parking the camry in a spot closest to the trail before unclicking her seatbelt and turning her body toward Vi in the passenger seat, “Will you stop calling me that?”

Vi smirked, unbuckling her own seatbelt before leaning over the middle console, “I don’t know, will you make me?”

Their gazes locked, noses just inches away from touching.  Caitlyn’s tongue jutted out slightly, slowly licking her bottom lip.  She watched the younger woman’s eyes track the movement, Vi’s own lips parting slightly as the heat between them had sucked all the oxygen from the air.

Then, in the blink of an eye, Caitlyn retreated, “You shouldn’t stare, darling, it’s quite unbecoming.”

And before Vi could even process what just happened, the older woman stepped out of the car and into the warmth of the summer sun.

‘Get it together, Lanes,’ Vi thought to herself, shaking her head in amusement while she followed suit, closing the car door behind her with a heavy thunk.

Everything under the June sky was flourishing.  The trees towered around them, their leaves so green that they contrasted brightly against the blue expanse.  Their branches swayed in the breeze that always seemed to exist up here in the higher elevations.

With it being a Saturday morning, the parking lot was packed.  Families of tourists milled about, children screamed as they chased butterflies, and parents carried hiking backpacks full of diapers and formula rather than any real hiking gear.

“Hey show-off, are you coming?” Caitlyn’s voice pulled Vi’s attention toward her, finding the older woman looking radiant in her climbing gear.  She was standing with a hand on her hip, her sunglasses protecting those gorgeous eyes from the harmful UV rays.  Caitlyn had already slipped into her harness, the straps hugging her hips in a way that made Vi’s stomach do a flip.

Vi grinned and fell into step with the older woman, a comfortable silence settling between them as they disappeared behind the tree line, the trail leading them deeper into the woods.

When they finally made it to the base of the impressive slab that made up the Reflector Oven wall, Maddie was already waiting.

The younger ranger stood beside a pile of climbing gear with her arms folded tightly across her chest.  Her expression darkened the moment she spotted them approaching through the trees.  Not only was Caitlyn late, but she’d also brought someone with her without running it by her first.

Maddie recognized the pink-haired woman as they neared.  She’d seen her around town before, usually at Jericho’s, surrounded by people fawning over her loud, attractive confidence.  The sort of woman who seemed to glide effortlessly through every room she entered.

“Sorry I’m late,” Caitlyn called as they approached, “Something came up.”

The excuse sounded flimsy even to her own ears.

Maddie’s mouth tightened as her eyes darted between the two women, “Right.”

Caitlyn either didn’t notice the quip or chose not to, “This is Vi,” she said, “A friend of mine and Jayce’s. She’s helping us get everything set up for the climbing event.”

Vi, mindful of Caitlyn’s earlier desire for her to behave, offered a cheerful wave, “Hey, good to meet you.”

Maddie returned a small nod and an automatic response, “You too.”

But beneath the platitudes, the younger ranger’s frustration simmered.  She wouldn’t be moved by Vi’s supposed easy charm.

“Okay, well, let’s get started, shall we?” Caitlyn directed them to the pile of equipment, the group sorting through the gear in a sort of awkward silence.

Without thinking too hard about what they were doing, Caitlyn and Vi moved into an easy rhythm.  One would reach for something before the other asked, they inspected each other’s gear without having to say anything, and the older woman helped Vi settle into her borrowed harness.  She tugged hard once and teased Vi for the sound that escaped her throat when the straps tightened around her thighs.

They included Maddie in their conversation whenever they remembered, but the problem was they didn’t seem to realize she wasn’t really participating.

Before long, Caitlyn tied into the lead rope, “Alright, you ready to see how it’s done?”

Vi huffed a laugh, stepping into belay position with a grin, “Go on, Miss Kiramman, let me see you show off.”

In the meantime, Maddie clenched her jaw slightly, because for a month, belaying had been her job.  She was the climbing ranger, not Vi.  And now she was just cast aside like nothing.

“Climbing,” Caitlyn said when she got in starting position at the base of the first route, named Chasm Crack, which was a classic crack line that rose through the center of the “Chasm” area of the slab. 

Vi’s callused hands gripped the rope, “Climb on!”

The route began with a technical crack climb and a short crux section before easing into easier terrain higher on the face.  Caitlyn moved methodically, fingers jamming the crack until she reached the first bolt.  She paused long enough to inspect its condition, tugging gently against the hanger and checking for any obvious wear.

Satisfied with the hold, she clipped a quickdraw into the bolt before pulling the rope through the lower carabiner, “Clipped!” she called.

“Looking good!” Vi answered below.

The granite forced her to switch between finger locks inside the crack and delicate smears on the slab beside it, repeating the process of checking each bolt as she climbed.  Near the upper section, the crack gradually lost its definition, and the climb became more balance-oriented.

Vi watched the woman scale the slab with the ease of a gecko, and then laughed to herself at the thought of Caitlyn Kiramman as a gecko.  She barely noticed Maddie muttering something behind her, too focused on the sense of pride that filled her stomach when Caitlyn reached the top of the route.

Caitlyn reached the anchor.  After examining both bolts thoroughly, she secured herself and threaded the rope through the anchor rings.  She leaned back to test the entire setup with her full weight before calling down that the station was bomber.

A moment later, the older woman rappelled gracefully down the slab, her boots barely touching the ground before she turned and looked directly at Vi, “Anchor’s good, your turn.” She said with a grin and a crooked brow.

Vi grinned, helping the older woman untie her harness from the rope before tying herself in, “I hope you’re ready to see what real climbing looks like,” she teased.

Caitlyn rolled her eyes and took hold of the rope.  Where she had climbed carefully, thinking each move through in her head before making them, Vi climbed with a confidence that bordered on reckless arrogance.

The younger woman flowed from hold to hold without hesitation, strong, callused fingers barely lingering on the rock before moving again.  Sure, she had the added benefit of the rope already being clipped into the bolts, but her climbing skills were still making Caitlyn swoon.

“Gods, she hasn’t even hesitated once,” she said to the younger ranger, who simply grunted in response.

Maddie saw the way Caitlyn smiled while watching Vi, a knot twisting tighter in her chest.  The worst part wasn’t that Vi was impressive.  Hell, she even admired the way she approached the climb without overthinking it.  The worst part was that Caitlyn barely seemed to acknowledge Maddie’s existence while Vi was there.

Maddie had spent the last month working alongside the older woman, hoping for some sign, some indication that Caitlyn saw her as anything more than just a fellow ranger.  Now, a single morning was enough to make it painfully obvious where Caitlyn’s attention lies.

Hours later, the routes were finished; top ropes hung neatly down the face of the slab, and the site was ready for the event.  Caitlyn and Vi sat on a tarp, sharing a sandwich as a post-climb snack, while Maddie finished coiling the last rope.

“Well,” the ranger announced quietly, “Everything’s packed up.”

Caitlyn swallowed the last bite of her half of the sandwich, wiping the crumbs from her palm on her pants as she looked up, “Thanks for all the help today.”

Her words were sincere, but somehow that only made them hurt more.  Maddie hoisted her gear onto her shoulder and mumbled out a dull, “Sure.”

The older woman didn’t notice, instead, she simply smiled and said, “See you tomorrow.”

Maddie nodded and turned on her heel, heading down the trail without looking back.  The sound of her quickdraws clinking against her harness gradually faded as she disappeared into the woods.

Vi’s eyes lingered on the tree line for a moment longer, eyes narrowed.  Her chewing slowed as she ate the last bit of her sandwich, replaying little moments from the past few hours as she thought about the weird vibe Maddie was putting off all day.

Something about it irked her, but she brushed it off when Caitlyn’s voice drew her attention somewhere next to her.

“Violet, normally if I ask a woman out on a date, I’d plan it myself, but…” she trailed off, doing that thing she always did where she bit her bottom lip in contemplation.

Vi finished her thought for her, “You want to know where we should go?” she chuckled.

Caitlyn nodded, flushing pink with embarrassment.

The younger woman thought about it for a while, sorting through her mental rolodex of every possible restaurant that could possibly suffice for a first date with a woman who’d probably been to every Michelin star restaurant in New York City.

Normally, Vi would just take a woman she was trying to fuck to one of the quaint restaurants on the main drag in Luray, and nothing too fancy.  Hell, it wasn’t like Luray had a wide variety of fine dining to choose from, anyway.

But Caitlyn, despite Vi’s insistence on never dating someone for real, was different.  The older woman deserved to be dined the old-fashioned way, and there was only one restaurant within reasonable driving distance of Shenandoah National Park that would be sufficient for a first date with the older woman.

And it just so happened that Vi was friends with the owner. 

Or, at least, the owner owed his life to her parents.  Getting a table without having a reservation wouldn’t be a problem.

She met Caitlyn’s curious gaze then, wearing a proud grin as she reached up and brushed a strand of blue behind the woman’s ear, “I know just the place, but I’m not going to tell you where we’re going.”

“Violet…” Caitlyn tried to argue, but it was clear in the way that Vi was looking at her that her mind was made up.

Vi stood and held out a hand, offering to help Caitlyn to her feet, “Come on, we should go if we want to have enough time to get ready.”

Chapter 9: Heard the Risk is Drowning, But I'm Gonna Take it

Summary:

Vi and Caitlyn go on their first date, but will they be able to jump over their own personal hurdles, or will they crash and burn?

Notes:

This is my absolute favorite chapter I've written thus far.

Cue the yearning!

(There will be some French in this chapter - translations are in the end notes.)

(See the end of the chapter for more notes.)

Chapter Text

After almost thirty minutes of back and forth, they’d agreed that Vi would pick Caitlyn up from Jayce’s cabin at 6:15 that Saturday evening, which would give them enough wiggle room to drive to the restaurant in case traffic backed up crossing through the park.  While they didn’t need a reservation, dinner would take a few hours, and Vi didn’t want to keep the older woman out too late.

Which meant, much to Caitlyn’s horror, that she had less than two hours to get ready.  What’s worse, she didn’t have nearly the right kind of outfit for what Vi had described.  No, the younger woman hadn’t told her where they were going, despite having pleaded with her to do just that, but Vi did have the foresight to tell her that she’d be wearing a suit.

Where was Caitlyn going to find a dress suitable enough for what sounded like a fine-dining experience in that amount of time?!

“Woah, woah, slow down, Sprout. Explain it to me again.” Jayce’s voice sounded amused, if not a little concerned, on the other end of the phone.

She paced the guest room, where fresh air breezed through the open window, keeping her from suffocating in the panic that clogged her lungs. “Jayce. I need a dinner gown, preferably within the hour! I didn’t bring anything nice from home! Fuck!”

The last word fell from her lips when she stubbed her toe on the bedframe.

Wind muffled the speaker and she could tell he was driving with his windows down.  The Chief Ranger was probably returning to the station after being out in the field all day, “What on earth do you need a dinner gown for?!”

“Jayce! Not now!” Caitlyn exclaimed, keeping the phone perched between her shoulder and ear while she dug through her jewelry bag for earrings that weren’t just the standard small hoops she normally wore.  Did she even bring any other jewelry?  Gods, she was really regretting her impulsive decision to leave New York without packing a proper suitcase.

Jayce sighed, “Okay, damn! Don’t freak out, I think I know someone that would loan you a dress. I’ll be there in…” He paused, probably checking his location on the GPS, “45 minutes, give or take. Please don’t have a coronary.”

“I won’t,” Caitlyn said through gritted teeth, still attending to her bashed pinky toe.

When they hung up, she flopped back onto the bed and stared at the ceiling, the panic in her throat unwavering even though Jayce had offered a small respite.  She closed her eyes and inhaled a deep breath, trying to find the root of the panic and work through it.

Was she afraid of the embarrassment that may come from being underdressed?  Or did she worry that if she didn’t dress appropriately, it would make Vi feel overdressed and therefore uncomfortable?

That wouldn’t bode well for a first date, she figured.  But no, neither of those options sounded right.  Especially because the panic still lingered.

As she lay there, counting the popped nails in the ceiling drywall, Caitlyn’s thoughts wandered to pink hair and freckles, and the way that crooked smile seemed to light up any room.  How pathetic, she thought, that she had even tried to push the younger woman away, when deep down she knew just how gone she was for her.

Caitlyn thought she’d been doing the right thing.  Because where would these feelings lead her?  She’d only just gotten out of a relationship; would she even be capable of dating someone else so soon?  Or would this entire date crash and burn before their…relationship, or whatever the hell this was, even got started?

And then there was the elephant in the room.  Caitlyn hadn’t forgotten what Jayce had said: Vi doesn’t do relationships.

What if they weren’t on the same page on this?  What if Caitlyn followed her heart and ended up falling head over heels for the younger woman, only to be rejected outright because Vi couldn’t commit?

There it was again.  The panic twisted her stomach like a snake, tightening around her insides and squeezing the life out of her.  It wasn’t about the dress, it was the fear of being rejected and heartbroken, again.

Caitlyn hadn’t realized just how long she’d been staring at the ceiling until she heard the telltale sound of the front door opening and closing on its hinges, the creaking so loud in the silence of the cabin that it was as if it were a sound effect playing on surround-sound speakers.

She blinked, pushing herself up on her elbows as Jayce’s footsteps thudded up the stairs, her best friend appearing at the threshold of her bedroom just a few seconds later with a long garment bag.

Jayce’s breath was ragged as if he’d run a marathon, “H-here, I was told this is the perfect dress for a dinner.”

He stepped into the room and hung the bag on the clothing rack in the closet, and then turned to where she still lay on the bed with furrowed brows, worrying her bottom lip, “Now will you tell me what this is all about, Sprout? I thought you’d be running around here like a chicken with its head cut off.”

Caitlyn huffed a laugh, “Well, I couldn’t do much without the dress.” She replied, finally getting up from the bed and approaching the garment bag with a look of trepidation, “Where’d you get this?”

Jayce found his favorite chair in the corner and plopped down into it, “I have this friend,” he started, and Caitlyn whipped her head back to look at him, because her best friend never mentioned being friends with another woman elegant enough to own a garment bag, let alone a dinner gown, “She’s the owner of the Massanutten Ski Resort not far from here.”

“Ski resort? You hate skiing. How did you meet?” Caitlyn asked, turning her attention back to the garment bag.  She fiddled with the zipper, pulling it down to reveal one of the most beautiful dinner gowns she’d ever seen.

It was made of pure silk, the fabric cool against her fingers as they trailed along the slit, which ran high along the left side of it.  The deep, viridian green shimmered in the low lamplight, and the fabric bunched up near the chest, swooping down in an arc that was held up by two very thin straps just over the shoulders.

The dress was as elegant as it was sexy, the perfect combination for Caitlyn’s tastes.

“Uh, well,” Jayce laughed awkwardly as she admired the dress, “We kinda hooked up once. But this was long before I knew I was gay!”

Caitlyn whipped her head around once again, her eyebrows lifted in surprise, “What?! Why didn’t I know this?!”

“I didn’t know I had to tell you everything about my sex life!” Jayce exclaimed.

“Yes! You do! That’s the whole point of our friendship, Jayce. We tell each other everything!”

She regretted saying that the moment the words left her mouth, because her best friend leaned back in his chair with a mischievous smirk, crossing his legs like he was about to start an interrogation, and he was the investigator.

“If that’s the case,” he drawled, “Then tell me who you’re going to dinner with.”

Fuck.

Caitlyn groaned and grabbed the dress off the hanger, careful not to let it drag onto the floor or let it wrinkle within her grip and moved toward the bathroom to put it on.  She didn’t bother closing the door—she and Jayce were far past the point in their friendship where modesty was even necessary.

“Violet and I are going out on a date,” she replied eventually.

Nothing could’ve prepared her for the ear-shattering screech that came out of her best friend’s mouth in reaction to those words.  Jayce had turned red as a tomato with excitement, “No fucking way!” He exclaimed, buzzing with energy, “I knew it! I freaking knew it.”

“Jayce,” her stern voice warned him that she wasn’t in the mood for an ‘I told you so’ moment.

Cool, smooth silk settled against her skin as she slid into the dress, the fabric draping over her body in all the best ways.  Thankfully, it fit like a glove.  Whoever this woman was, she was almost as tall as Caitlyn was.  Skinny straps emphasized the strength of her shoulders, and the swooping fabric settled just below her clavicle, showing off just enough cleavage that it was alluring without being skimpy.

When she turned her back toward the bathroom mirror, Caitlyn finally saw just how low it plunged.  Her entire back was visible, down to her dimples of venus.  She made a note to ask Jayce if she could buy the dress from his friend later.

Her best friend appeared at the doorway of the bathroom then, his wide grin visible in his reflection in the mirror, “Vi’s going to absolutely lose her shit when she sees you.”

Gone was the teasing lilt in his voice, replaced by wistful pride for the way his best friend was putting herself out there again.

Caitlyn hummed in response, a hairgrip perched between her lips as she used both hands to tie her hair up in a neat bun, smoothing out the loose strands until everything sat just right.  With one hand holding the bun steady, she used the other, grabbed the hairgrip from her mouth, and pinned up the shorter strands.

Only once she was satisfied did she respond, catching Jayce’s gaze in the mirror, “Do you think this is just a one-time thing for her?” she asked pointedly before digging in her makeup bag for the blush.

Silence filled the air between them for a moment.  Caitlyn could practically see the gears turning in his head as her best friend tried to decide what to say. 

“I think…” he paused, choosing his words carefully, “I think this is a first-time thing for Vi, not a one-time thing.”

Those words struck a chord with her, more than Jayce probably realized when he said them.  Caitlyn contemplated it for a moment, and decided that, since it’s only their first date, she wouldn’t ask Vi about her intentions tonight.  Why ruin a good dinner with such a heavy conversation?

With her cheekbones painted a subtle pink, she moved on to her eyelids, working a small brush into the pigment of an eyeshadow pallet, “I like her,” she admitted under her breath, staring determinedly in the mirror.

Multiple events unfolded simultaneously.  First, Jayce squealed louder than a train horn for the second time that night, which startled Caitlyn so bad she poked her eye with the eyeshadow brush, effectively ruining the smokey eye she’d almost finished.  Second, in the chaos of stabbing her eye, a bony elbow caught the edge of the vanity, resulting in an immediate bruise that grew a darker shade of purple with each passing second.  And finally, as if the entire evening was a sitcom written by Phoebe Waller-Bridge, a series of knocks thudded against the front door and echoed throughout the cabin. 

No one moved a muscle.

Jayce, regretful in his own right, simply stared blankly at the floor while Caitlyn glared at him so intensely, he felt as though his skin was burning from lasers shooting out of her eyes.

Another round of knocks sounded through the deafening silence.

“Go. stall her while I fix this.” Her accented voice had turned to stone.  She turned back to the mirror, frantically wiping the eyeshadow with a makeup wipe while her pulse thundered in her ears.

Scolded, Jayce backed out of the bathroom so fast he left a gust of wind in his wake.  Caitlyn had to bite her cheek to keep from laughing.  She imagined this is what it was like having to discipline a child who’d been acting out in a humorous, albeit badly behaved way, and you can’t laugh because the child needs to understand that discipline is, in fact, no laughing matter.

Downstairs, Jayce swung open the door just as Vi was about to knock again.  Her hand hovered mid-air and her mouth fell open in surprise at how quickly the door opened, “Jesus, man. Fuckin’ scared the shit out of me.”

Jayce chuckled and stepped to the side, allowing Vi to step inside.  He closed the door softly behind them, then led her into the kitchen, “Cait…ah, may have had a small incident. She’s still getting ready. Do you want a drink?”

Vi raised a brow, “Uh, sure? What kind of incident we talkin’?”

The fridge light spilled yellow across every surface of the kitchen as he reached in for a couple of beers, popping the caps on the bottle opener fastened on the side of the island with soft clinks.

He handed one to Vi, “The kind where I may have accidentally bumped into her while she was doing her makeup?”

Vi, mid-sip, choked on a laugh and subsequently coughed the beer from her lungs, “Oh, I already know she’s so pissed.”

Jayce groaned, “She’s so scary! Doesn’t even yell. Just stares at you with real flames in her eyes.”

Vi realized that Caitlyn never looked at her that way and shivered at the thought of it.  Yeah, she’d be sure to stay on her good side.  She took another sip of the beer, letting the cold liquid ease her nerves.  She’d never tell Jayce that it took her fifteen minutes just to work up her nerves to knock on the door.

“Listen…Vi,” Jayce’s voice went serious.  He set down his beer on the counter and crossed his arms.  It was clear he was about to do that big-brother thing where he intimidates his sister’s date with some posturing and thinly veiled threats.

She held up a hand, stopping him in his tracks, “I know what you’re going to say. Please don’t.”

“But—”

Vi shook her head, “She’s different. I promise. This is very new and we haven’t even talked about what we want, but you don’t have to worry about her coming home crying because I lied about being able to commit to whatever it is that we do want.”

He tilted his head, “Can you commit to her, though?”

“Long-term? I don’t know,” she said honestly, “but I don’t even know if she would want that right now, either. We’re just having fun.”

“As long as fun doesn’t lead to her falling head-over-heels while you’re just looking for a quick fuck.” Jayce replied pointedly.

She punched his arm at that.

“Ow!” He exclaimed, holding his arm, “You know I’m right!”

“No, you’re an ass. I told you this is different. You’re just going to have to trust me.”

Jayce changed the subject, “Where are you even taking her? You’re dressed like a fucking GQ model and there’s not a single restaurant in town that justifies that suit.”

Just before Vi answered, the unmistakable sound of heels clacking against the hardwood interrupted her.

Heads turned in unison toward the staircase as the sound grew louder.  For Vi, time seemed to slow as Caitlyn appeared, impossibly radiant in the dim cabin lights.  The first thing that registered for the younger woman was skin.  So much porcelain skin that her mouth went dry instantly.

The older woman was wearing a beautiful, shimmering viridian silk gown, the fabric hugging her body in all its sultry glory.  A slit ran up the length of her leg, showing off smooth skin each time she took a step, and the skinny, barely there straps emphasized the strength of her bare arms.  While her muscles weren’t as built as Vi’s were, they were impressive in a subtle sort of way that made heat spread within Vi’s chest.

And then, of course, there was her back.  Completely bare, the fabric just coming up to rest over her hips, leaving little for the imagination. 

Caitlyn’s midnight-blue hair was tied up in a neat bun, which only allowed Vi to imagine, just for a brief second, her lips pressing to the skin of her long neck.

She must’ve been staring for an uncomfortably long stretch of time, because suddenly Jayce was elbowing her in the ribs.

“Ow,” Vi whispered, then turned her attention to the older woman, who was standing in front of her with the most adorably bashful expression on her face, “Miss Kira—” she coughed, stopping herself, “Caitlyn, you look…” she let her eyes rake over her dress once more, then slowly returned to those glimmering blue eyes, “Ravishing.”

Caitlyn’s cheeks flooded with heat.  She moved to brush an invisible strand of hair behind her ear out of habit, until she realized how foolish that looked and dropped her hand.  She was unbelievably flustered, and not just because of Vi’s compliment.

No, what had her speechless was Vi in that suit.

The younger woman was clad in a perfectly tailored, coffee-brown three-piece suit.  Her broad shoulders looked impossibly strong in the blazer, and the buttoned vest emphasized her waist in a way that made Caitlyn’s stomach do a somersault.  Even though Vi was shorter than she was, she wasn’t actually short compared to most women, and the loose, pleated trousers did wonders for elongating her legs.

The crisp white shirt underneath was pressed to perfection, unbuttoned far enough that it showed off the ridges of her clavicle. 

And then there was her hair.  Her usual wild and untamed pink strands were slicked back, curling slightly at the nape of her neck.  It gave the older woman a clear view of her strong jawline and freckled cheeks, which Caitlyn quite enjoyed for selfish reasons.

There simply weren’t words to express just how attractive Vi was in that moment, but Caitlyn supposed it’d be rude if she didn’t return the compliment, regardless.

“Violet…” she breathed, and because she couldn’t help herself, Caitlyn stepped closer, gently gripping the younger woman’s bicep, “You are so ruggedly handsome, darling.”

Together, they looked like the sprawling Shenandoah forests personified.

Vi’s arm tingled where Caitlyn’s hand rested, and she could feel the heat radiating from the taller woman, the air thick with an intoxicating tension.

“Ooookay,” Jayce said, breaking the moment.

Caitlyn, remembering that her best friend was standing right there, stepped back to put space between them. 

Something caught Vi’s eye when the older woman moved to lift her purse strap over her shoulder.

“Cait…what happened?” She frowned, closing the distance between them again as callused hands found Caitlyn’s injured elbow.  The bruise had grown deep purple, angry in contrast to the surrounding porcelain.

Caitlyn lifted her elbow slightly, brows pinched when she noticed what Vi had been talking about.  Then, without a word, she swung her purse at Jayce, “This is your fault!”

“It’s not my fault that you’re clumsy,” he muttered, crossing his arms with a pout.

Vi ignored them both, still examining the bruise gingerly.  Caitlyn winced in pain, pulling away instinctively.

“Get her an icepack for the road, pretty boy.” Vi turned to Jayce, shooing him off with a wave of her hand.

Jayce muttered something unintelligible as he walked away, which only made Caitlyn giggle, “I’m alright, Vi. There’s really no need for this.”

Vi clicked her tongue, “Who am I to ignore an injured princess? Especially one dressed as beautifully as you.”

Grey eyes looked up at her, so full of awe and wonder that Caitlyn had to look away, for fear that she’d absolutely melt where she stood.  The compliment went straight to her gut, flooding it with a warmth she desperately wanted to ignore, “You’re ridiculous.”

Vi shrugged, “Hmm, maybe.”

Jayce returned with a cold pack and handed it to Vi, who pressed it carefully against Caitlyn’s elbow, “Hold this,” she instructed, retreating her hand only when the older woman nodded.

“I’ll have her home by Midnight,” Vi said, winking at Jayce with a wide grin.

“Just text me when you get there and when you leave, okay?” He replied, placing a hand on her shoulder.

Rolling her eyes, Vi smirked, “Okay, Dad.”

The night sky hung over the valley like a celestial blanket, full of a million little specks of fire that Caitlyn couldn’t help but try to count as she stared out the open passenger window.  Fresh, earthy air rushed in, testing the strength of her hairgrips like a game of tug-of-war.

Vi still hadn’t told her where they were going, and they’d already been driving for fifteen minutes in the opposite direction of town.  Caitlyn’s interest was piqued, to say the least.  She didn’t mind the drive, the road led them on a winding path through the national park itself, bending around jagged mountains and towering pines. 

The cold pack lay abandoned in the middle cupholder, though her elbow still ached and the chill lingered, making her shiver in the misty, nighttime air.

“I can roll the windows up if you’re cold,” Vi offered, glancing briefly at the older woman, who looked much too regal to be sitting passenger in her Subaru.

A stubborn strand of pink curled on her forehead, escaping the gel that she’d used to slick her hair back.  Caitlyn stared at it, biting back the impulse to reach over and smooth it back into place.

She shook her head, “I quite like the fresh air, it’s refreshing.”

Vi hummed, “Didn’t get a lot of this in the city, did you?”

“If you count diesel exhaust and putrid subway air as refreshing, sure.” Caitlyn joked.

The younger woman scrunched her nose as if she could smell the rancid air of New York City streets, “That’s gross.”

Caitlyn giggled, “If you think that’s bad, wait until I tell you about the time this random man, who was absolutely badgered, soiled his trousers on the pavement next to me as I was waiting in line for a jacket potato.”

“Oh, Gods! There goes my appetite,” Vi chuckled, “Guess we’ll have to turn around, then.”

“No!” Caitlyn laughed, the carefree sound of it was honey to Vi’s ears.  The older woman’s hand came to rest on her knee, like it had been the most normal thing in the world. “Our night is just starting.”

Vi swallowed, “You’re right. We can’t let a dress like that go to waste, anyways.”

“Hmm, I’m glad you like it, dear.” Caitlyn’s voice went low and resonant, with a tinge of a teasing lilt that made the butterflies in the younger woman’s stomach flutter.

There was something about being more open about their blooming feelings toward each other that made it easier for them to press each other’s buttons, to test what they could get away with and what was off-limits.

“Have you ever been to the city?” Caitlyn asked after a beat of comfortable silence passed between them.

Uncertainty flickered in Vi’s expression, “Um,” she drummed her fingers against the steering wheel, “No, actually.” And then she realized she probably sounded a bit closed off, so she added, “My adoptive father goes there a lot, though. He’s actually there now…”

The hand on her knee squeezed once, a sign of reassurance, “Is this related to the same family stuff you had going on last month?” Caitlyn inquired gently.

Vi sighed, nodding, “Yeah.”

Once again, she put a wall up when it came to the subject of her family.  Vi didn’t bother explaining what was going on, and Caitlyn, perceptive as always, didn’t push.  The younger woman’s trauma regarding what had happened to her parents was clear enough, so it didn’t feel right to continue down this line of questioning if she wasn’t ready to talk about it.

Caitlyn hummed, then changed the subject, “So, how much further is this dinner, darling?”

The combination of the older woman’s slender hand resting on her knee, and the sound of the endearment on her tongue, made Vi lose focus for a few seconds.  The ringing in her ears continued until it was drowned out by the sound of Caitlyn’s giggle.

“W-what?” Vi blinked, glancing at the woman in the passenger seat briefly, before remembering she was, in fact, still driving.

“I asked you how much further we had to drive, Violet.” Caitlyn scolded playfully, her lips twitching with amusement.

Just as she was about to check the GPS, the digital voice chirped, ‘In one-hundred feet, turn left onto Main Street.’

Vi’s expression turned mischievous, “Well, Miss Kiramman, we’re about there.” She announced as they turned onto a well-lit road, which led them further into a small town.

A historical plaque on the side of the road indicated that they’d arrived in Little Washington.

Main Street was lined on either side with colonial-era buildings, some with brick façades and others with wood, painted in light, pastel colors and nestled between stretches of farmland in the foothills of the Blue Ridge Mountains.

It reminded Caitlyn of the small villages in the English countryside; the nostalgic feeling of home caught in her throat.

“Pretty sure there’s more chickens that live here than humans,” Vi joked, noticing the way the older woman’s eyes went reverent as they slowly made their way toward the end of the street.

“It reminds me of the villages back home,” Caitlyn whispered in reply.

Finally, Vi pulled the Subaru into a small parking lot beside a large colonial-style building with a two-tiered front porch that stretched the length of the façade.

Vi rounded the front of the car and hurried to Caitlyn’s side before the valet could reach them.

The evening air carried the scent of freshly cut grass, the perfectly manicured landscaping proof of just how much care and detail went into maintaining the Inn.  It was that exact level of meticulousness that made this restaurant so renowned for its food.

She opened Caitlyn’s door and extended her hand without thinking. “Milady,” Vi said with a goofy grin.

Caitlyn rolled her eyes affectionately as she placed her hand in Vi’s, “Are you going to keep this bit running all night?”

Vi shrugged, “Do you not find it charming?”

Their fingers settled together naturally, as though they’d done it a hundred times before.

“I suppose,” Caitlyn replied with an air of nonchalance, though internally she knew damn well she’d let the younger woman play her shining knight any day of the week.

Vi helped her from the car and neither of them seemed particularly eager to let go once both sets of feet were firmly on the ground.  Instead, they started toward the building side by side, their joined hands swinging slightly between them.

Ahead, warm golden light spilled from the windows of the white clapboard building.  Caitlyn’s gaze drifted upward and caught the wooden sign hanging above the entrance, which read, ‘Inn at Little Washington.’

Her steps slowed, “Vi,” she said quietly.

“Hmm?”

“You brought me to The Inn at Little Washington?”

Vi stopped walking and turned to face the woman with a confused expression, “Yeah? Is that okay? I know it’s not exactly Le Jardinier…”

Caitlyn cut her off, “Okay? Are you kidding? The attorneys at my firm always complained that it takes months to get reservations…”

Because it wasn’t just a restaurant.  The Inn at Little Washington was designated as a three-star Michelin-starred restaurant that’s been open for forty-five years.

The younger woman grinned, “What can I say, princess? I have my secrets.”

Caitlyn could only shake her head and laugh.

As they approached the entrance, her fingers shifted against Vi’s, adjusting their grip unconsciously.  Vi became painfully aware of every point of contact, just hoping that her hand didn’t go clammy.

Caitlyn’s palm was warm despite the cool evening.  Her fingers were long and elegant, softened by what she could only assume was an expensive lotion.  A perfect contrast to her more rugged, callused hands, roughened by years of holding paddles.

Vi opened the door, allowing Caitlyn to walk into the building’s warmth ahead of her, following behind without once letting go of her hand.

Inside, they were greeted by a man standing by the host stand, dressed in a pristine black tuxedo.  His eyes widened the moment he recognized Vi.

“Miss Lanes!” He chirped, eyes sparkling at the sight of her, “We weren’t expecting you this evening. I am delighted to see you!”

‘Weren’t expecting them?’ Caitlyn inwardly wondered.  How did Vi expect to get a table without a reservation?

She watched Vi’s ears turn a comical shade of pink as the younger woman greeted the man, “Hey, Pierre.”

“Hey?” Pierre repeated in mock offense, “You do not come to visit me for months and now all I get is a ‘hey’?” The man chastised with his thick French accent.

Vi laughed, the corners of her eyes crinkling in response, and Caitlyn thought it was the most adorable sight she’d ever seen.  The interaction between the younger woman and the host was clearly familiar, and she wondered just how many times Vi had been here.  Had she brought other women to the Inn for their first dates?

Pierre’s gaze drifted past Vi and landed squarely on Caitlyn, “And who is this?” He asked with a quirk of a brow.

Vi glanced at her, their eyes meeting in what could only be described as a moment of pure reverence.  At least, that’s how Caitlyn interpreted the way the younger woman looked at her then.  She automatically missed those grey eyes when they turned back to the host, “This is Caitlyn,” she replied, clearing her throat, “My date.”

The man’s eyes widened in surprise, and he leaned in closer to Vi, voice low as if not wanting Caitlyn to hear, “You’ve never brought anyone with you for dinner, she must be special?”

Nevertheless, the older woman did hear what he’d said.  So, what?  Vi always ate alone?  She hated the thought of the younger woman sitting at a table in a corner by herself with no one to share a meal with.

This time, it was Vi’s cheeks that went pink, “Don’t make it weird, Pierre. I was hoping you’d have a table available?”

The man didn’t even need a moment to think, “One moment.” He said, snapping his fingers.  A nearby server dressed in black trousers, a white button-down, and a perfectly centered tie appeared almost instantly.

Pierre leaned down and whispered something into the server’s ear, the shorter man’s eyes widening as they flicked briefly toward Vi and Caitlyn before nodding with alarming urgency and disappearing deeper into the dining room.

“Ahem, well,” the host cleared his throat, “While your table takes a few moments to prepare, perhaps you’d allow me to show you around.” He swept one arm dramatically, “Come, come.”
